with
s>0
.
Applying the identity

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,f(s)\delta^{(4)}(p-ns)=2f(p^{0})(p^{0})^{-1}\delta_{+}(p^{2})\delta^{(2)}(\Omega_{\vec{p}}-\Omega_{\vec{n}})\,,

(93)

with
f(s)
being a test function, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)}\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle\sim\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\delta_{+}((q-ns)^{2})w^{(s)}_{J}(s,s+\hat{\omega})\theta(s+\hat{\omega})

\displaystyle={1\over 4\pi}{(q^{2})^{J}\over(qn)^{J+1}}w^{(s)}_{J}\left(1,1+\omega\right)\theta\left(1+\omega\right)\,.

(94)

Here in the second relation we introduced the dimensionless variable
\omega
defined in (
43
) and took into account that
w^{(s)}_{J}(s,s+\hat{\omega})
is a homogenous function of degree
J
.
Replacing the weights
w_{J}
by their explicit expressions (
82
), we verify that
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)}\rangle_{q}
has the expected form (
57
), with the function
f_{{\cal O}_{J}}
given by (
58
) and (
59
) evaluated at
\Delta_{\phi}=2
.

3.3
Cross-talk

To demonstrate the phenomenon of cross-talk between the detectors, we consider the two-point functions
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}
with the source and sink given by a complex scalar field,

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle{\varphi^{\dagger}(\tilde{q})\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})\varphi(q)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{0|a_{\tilde{q}}\,\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})a^{\dagger}_{q}|0}\rangle\,,

(95)

where
\tilde{q}=q+n_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}+n_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2}
and
q^{2}=\tilde{q}^{2}=0
. The expression on the right-hand side coincides with the transition amplitude on the left-hand side of (
71
) with single-particle
in
and
out
states. If the relation (
71
) were correct, the above expression would be proportional to the vacuum expectation value of the flow operator and, therefore, it would vanish.
Let us show that this is not the case.

As in the previous case, the inelastic terms in
\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})
and
\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})
do not contribute to (
3.3
)
for
n_{1}\neq n_{2}
. The contribution of the elastic terms can be found using (
91
):

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}=\int_{0}^{\infty}{dsds^{\prime}\over(2(2\pi)^{3})^{2}}w_{J_{2}}(s,s+\hat{\omega}_{2})w_{J_{1}}(s^{\prime},s^{\prime}+\hat{\omega}_{1})

\displaystyle\times\langle{a_{\tilde{q}}a^{\dagger}_{(s^{\prime}+\hat{\omega}_{1})n_{1}}}\rangle\langle{a_{s^{\prime}n_{1}}a^{\dagger}_{(s+\hat{\omega}_{2})n_{2}}}\rangle\langle{a_{sn_{2}}a^{\dagger}_{q}}\rangle+(\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2},J_{1}\leftrightarrow J_{2},n_{1}\leftrightarrow n_{2})\,,

(96)

where the second term ensures the symmetry under the exchange of the detectors. The first term describes the sequential propagation of a particle through the two detectors, as shown in Figure
1
. Since the detectors are located on the celestial sphere at two different directions, we expect that the energy of the particle exchanged between them should vanish.

Figure 1:
Cross-talk between the detectors. The grey blobs represent the detectors, the solid line depicts the propagator of a scalar particle, the arrows indicate the direction in which it propagates.

To show this we examine

\displaystyle\langle{a_{s^{\prime}n_{1}}a^{\dagger}_{(s+\hat{\omega}_{2})n_{2}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}2s\delta^{(3)}(s^{\prime}\vec{n}_{1}-(s+\hat{\omega}_{2})\vec{n}_{2})

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}2s\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}{1\over(\pi\epsilon)^{3/2}}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{-(s^{\prime}\vec{n}_{1}-(s+\hat{\omega}_{2})\vec{n}_{2})^{2}/\epsilon}={(2\pi)^{2}\over(n_{1}n_{2})}\delta(s^{\prime})\delta(s+\hat{\omega}_{2})\,,

(97)

where we used (
74
) and replaced
n_{1}=(1,\vec{n}_{1})
and
n_{2}=(1,\vec{n}_{2})
with
\vec{n}_{1}^{2}=\vec{n}_{2}^{2}=1
. To check the relation on the second line it is sufficient to integrate both sides against a test function. The expression on the right-hand side of (
3.3
) is different from zero only for
\hat{\omega}_{2}<0
. Substituting (
3.3
) into (
3.3
) and taking into account (
74
) we obtain

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}={(2\pi)^{2}\over(n_{1}n_{2})}q^{0}\tilde{q}^{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[\delta^{(3)}(\vec{q}+\vec{n}_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2})\delta^{(3)}(\vec{\tilde{q}}-\vec{n}_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1})w_{J_{2}}(-\hat{\omega}_{2},0)w_{J_{1}}(0,\hat{\omega}_{1})\theta(-\hat{\omega}_{2})\theta(\hat{\omega}_{1})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ +\left.\delta^{(3)}(\vec{q}+\vec{n}_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1})\delta^{(3)}(\vec{\tilde{q}}-\vec{n}_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2})w_{J_{2}}(0,\hat{\omega}_{2})w_{J_{1}}(-\hat{\omega}_{1},0)\theta(\hat{\omega}_{2})\theta(-\hat{\omega}_{1})\right].

(98)

Notice that this expression is different from zero for
\hat{\omega}_{2}
and
\hat{\omega}_{1}
of different signs. For instance, for
\hat{\omega}_{2}<0
and
\hat{\omega}_{1}>0
, a particle with on-shell momentum
q^{\mu}=(q^{0},\vec{q})
enters the detector
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})
and leaves it with momentum
q+n_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2}=0
, or equivalently
s^{\prime}=0
. The second detector
\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})
transfers the momentum
n_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}
and the particle reaches the sink with momentum
\tilde{q}=n_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}
.

In the above analysis we chose the source and sink to be a scalar field. It is straightforward to show that the relation (
3.3
) also holds for fermion and gauge fields (up to an overall factor proportional to a power of
q^{0}\tilde{q}{}^{0}
). However, comparing the relations (
82
) and (
87
), we find
that, unlike the scalars, the weights
w_{J}(0,s)
and
w_{J}(s,0)
vanish for fermions and gauge fields. Thus, cross-talk can only be generated by the exchange of scalars with zero energy. As follows from (
3.3
), its contribution is accompanied by a factor of
1/(n_{1}n_{2})
that becomes singular for
z\sim(n_{1}n_{2})\to 0
. Below we show that an analogous phenomenon occurs in the interacting field theory.

3.4
Two-point functions in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM from amplitudes

In this subsection, we apply the technique described above to compute the two-point function (
50
) in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM at weak coupling.
This theory describes a gauge field coupled to four gauginos
\psi_{i}
(with
i=1,\dots,4
) and six real scalars
\varphi_{I}
(
I=1,\dots 6
), all in the adjoint representation of the gauge group
SU(N)
. The index
i
belongs to the fundamental representation of the R-symmetry group
SU(4)
, while
I
is an index of the vector representation of
SO(6)\sim SU(4)
.

For the sake of simplicity,
we choose the initial and final states,
\phi(q)|{0}\rangle
and
\langle{0}|\phi^{\dagger}(\tilde{q})
, respectively, to be defined by the simplest gauge invariant scalar operator of the form

\displaystyle\phi=Y^{I}Y^{J}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\varphi_{I}\varphi_{J})\,,\qquad\qquad\phi^{\dagger}=\bar{Y}^{I}\bar{Y}^{J}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\varphi_{I}\varphi_{J})\,,

(99)

where
Y
are auxiliary (complex) six-dimensional null vectors,
Y^{I}Y^{I}=0
.
The operator (
99
) is half-BPS and its scaling dimension
\Delta_{\phi}=2
is protected from quantum corrections.
It creates a pair of scalars out of the vacuum, which carries zero total color charge and nonzero R-charge corresponding to the representation
\bf 20^{\prime}
of the
SU(4)
.

As mentioned above, we shall consider three different flow operators, scalar (
S
), charge
(Q)
and energy
(E)
operators. They are defined by local twist-two operators of spin
J=0,1,2
, respectively. The stress-energy tensor is given by the spin
J=2
operators defined in (
3.1
) and (
3.1
) with the only difference that the scalar and fermion fields are replaced by
\varphi_{I}
and
\psi_{i}
, respectively. The scalar
J=0
operator is defined as

\displaystyle O_{J=0}(x,S)=S^{IJ}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\varphi_{I}\varphi_{J}(x))\,,

(100)

where the symmetric traceless ‘polarization’ tensor
S^{IJ}
determines the orientation of the detector in the isotopic R-space. Like (
99
), the operator (
100
) is protected and lives in the same representation
\bf 20^{\prime}
of
SU(4)
. Finally, the spin
J=1
operator is given by the R-current

\displaystyle O_{J=1}(x,Q)=iQ^{IJ}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits[\varphi_{I}\partial_{+}\varphi_{J}]+\dots\,,

(101)

where the charge polarization matrix
Q^{IJ}
is an antisymmetric tensor in the adjoint representation
\bf 15
of
SU(4)
. The dots denote terms involving gauge fields and gauginos, which do not contribute to the two-point correlations
\langle{\cal O}_{J_{1}}(\omega_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{J_{2}}(\omega_{2},n_{2})\rangle_{q}
to one-loop order at weak coupling. The operators (
100
) and (
101
) have scaling dimension
\Delta=2+J
and twist
t=\Delta-J=2
.

According to (
61
), the two-point correlations are defined by the functions
f_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}{\cal O}_{J_{2}}}
. At weak coupling,
they admit an expansion in the powers of the ’t Hooft coupling
a=g^{2}N_{c}/(4\pi^{2})
,

\displaystyle f_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}{\cal O}_{J_{2}}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)=f^{(0)}_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}{\cal O}_{J_{2}}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)+{a\over 4}f^{(1)}_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}{\cal O}_{J_{2}}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)+\dots\,,

(102)

where the first and the second terms describe the Born and the one-loop approximations, respectively.

In the Born approximation, the function
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}^{(0)}
is given by a sum of two terms describing the two possible propagation channels of the two scalars to the final state: (i) each scalar goes through one of the detectors (see Figure
2
(a)) and (ii) one of the scalars goes sequentially through the two detectors and the other one remains undetected (see Figures
2
(b) and (c)). The second channel generates cross-talk between the detectors.

Figure 2:
The two-point correlations
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}
in the Born approximation.

Each of these contributions is accompanied by a R-symmetry factor arising from the contraction of the indices of the scalar fields in the source and sink with those of the detectors. In particular, for the scalar-scalar and scalar-charge detectors the first contribution comes with a factor of
(\bar{Y}S_{1}Y)(\bar{Y}S_{2}Y)
and
(\bar{Y}S_{1}Y)(\bar{Y}Q_{2}Y)
, respectively, where we used a shorthand notation for the contraction of the
SO(6)
indices,
(\bar{Y}S_{1}Y)=\bar{Y}^{I}S^{IJ}Y^{J}
and similarly for the charge. For the energy detector, the corresponding isotopic polarization tensor is diagonal
in the
SO(6)
indices,
(E_{i})^{IJ}=\delta^{IJ}
and yields
(\bar{Y}E_{i}Y)=(\bar{Y}Y)
. To simplify the formulae we normalize the
SO(6)
tensors as follows,

\displaystyle(\bar{Y}SY)=(\bar{Y}QY)=1\,.

(103)

For the cross-talk contribution the R-symmetry factors look differently. For instance, for the scalar-scalar and scalar-charge correlations they take the form
(\bar{Y}Y)(\bar{Y}S_{1}S_{2}Y)
and
(\bar{Y}Y)(\bar{Y}S_{1}Q_{2}Y)
, respectively, plus the same expressions with the detectors exchanged. As mentioned above, the cross-talk between the detectors induces corrections that are
singular at
z=0
. We can eliminate them by imposing additional conditions on the detector polarization tensors,

\displaystyle S_{1}S_{2}=S_{1}S_{2}=0\,,\qquad S_{1}Q_{2}=Q_{2}S_{1}=0\,.

(104)

Following Refs.
Belitsky:2013xxa
;
Belitsky:2013bja
, we choose

\displaystyle Y=(1,0,1,0,i,i)\,,\qquad S_{1}=\text{diag}(1,-1,0,0,0,0)\,,\qquad S_{2}=\text{diag}(0,0,1,-1,0,0)\,.

(105)

Notice that a similar condition cannot be imposed on the polarization tensor of the energy detector
(E_{i})^{IJ}=\delta^{IJ}
. This means that the correlations involving the energy detector will necessarily get a cross-talk contribution and, therefore, we expect them to contain additional terms that are
singular for
z\to 0
.

The contribution of the first channel to the two-point correlation is

\displaystyle\int{d^{4}p_{1}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\delta_{+}(p_{1}^{2})\int{d^{4}p_{2}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\delta_{+}(p_{2}^{2})(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(q-p_{1}-p_{2})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1,2}w^{(s)}_{J_{i}}(p_{i}^{0},p_{i}^{0}+\hat{\omega}_{i})\delta^{(2)}(\Omega_{\vec{p}_{i}}-\Omega_{\vec{n}_{i}})\theta(p_{i}^{0}+\hat{\omega}_{i})(p_{i}^{0})^{-1}\,,

(106)

where the first line contains an integral over the Lorentz invariant two-particle phase space and the second line
contains the weights (
82
) corresponding to a scalar going through the two detectors.
The evaluation of (
3.4
) can be simplified by going to the rest frame of the source,
q^{\mu}=(Q,\vec{0})
. Then,
\vec{p}_{1}=-\vec{p}_{2}
and
p_{1}^{0}=p_{2}^{0}=Q/2
, so that the integral does not vanish
only if the detectors are located back-to-back,
\vec{n}_{1}=-\vec{n}_{2}
, or equivalently
z=1
. The calculation results in

\displaystyle f^{(0)}_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}{\cal O}_{J_{2}}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)=\frac{1}{4}\delta(1-z)\prod_{i=1,2}w^{(s)}_{J_{i}}(1,1+\omega_{i})\,.

(107)

This relation holds for all detectors but the energy one. In the latter case, the function
f^{(0)}_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}{\cal O}_{J_{2}}}
receives an additional contribution due to the cross-talk
between the detectors,

\displaystyle f^{(0)}_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}E}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\delta(1-z)w^{(s)}_{J_{1}}(1,1+\omega_{1})w^{(s)}_{E}(1,1+\omega_{2})

\displaystyle+{1\over z}w^{(s)}_{J_{1}}(1,0)w^{(s)}_{E}(1,0)\left[\delta(\omega_{1}-1)\delta(\omega_{2}+1)+\delta(\omega_{1}+1)\delta(\omega_{2}-1)\right]\,,

\displaystyle f^{(0)}_{E{\cal O}_{J_{1}}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=f^{(0)}_{{\cal O}_{J_{1}}E}(\omega_{2},\omega_{1},z)\,.

(108)

where the product of delta functions originates from (
3.3
). Namely, in the Born approximation the source creates a pair of scalar particles
each having energy
Q/2
in the rest frame
q^{\mu}=(Q,\vec{0})
. The energy of one of the particles is absorbed by the detector and, therefore, the two-point correlation should be localized at
\omega_{i}=-1
. The second delta function arises because the other detector has to transfer the energy
Q/2
to the same scalar particle before it reaches the sink. To avoid such contributions we tacitly assumed elsewhere that
\omega_{i}>-1
.

The relation (
3.4
) holds for
0<z\leq 1
and it does not take into account the contact terms proportional to
\delta(z)
. Such contact terms have interesting properties and we shall discuss them elsewhere
contact
.

Turning on the interaction, we find that to order
O(a)
the two-point correlation (
69
) receives contributions from the states
X
and
X^{\prime}
containing up to four particles. Following the discussion in the previous subsection, we split
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\omega}_{1},J_{1}}(n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\omega}_{2},J_{2}}(n_{2})}\rangle_{q}
into a sum of elastic and inelastic contributions. The former is given by (
69
) with
X
and
X^{\prime}
containing the same number of particles. For
z\neq 1
, or equivalently
\vec{n}_{1}\neq-\vec{n}_{2}
in the rest frame of the source, there are only three particles. The inelastic contribution takes into account the possibility for the detector to create or annihilate a pair of particles. As a result, the states
X
and
X^{\prime}
can have
2
or
4
particles.

Elastic contribution

In this case, the states
X
and
X^{\prime}
in (
69
) contain three particles, either two scalars and a gluon, or a scalar and a pair of gauginos. Two of these particles with on-shell momenta
p_{i}
,
i=1,2
, go through the detectors which transfer to them the energy
\hat{\omega}_{i}
and modify their momenta to
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i}+n_{i}\hat{\omega}_{i}
.
The remaining undetected particle propagates from the source to the sink and has momentum
p_{3}=q-p_{1}-p_{2}
. This results in the contribution

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\omega_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\omega_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}^{\text{elastic}}

\displaystyle=2\int{d^{4}p_{1}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\delta_{+}(p_{1}^{2})\int{d^{4}p_{2}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\delta_{+}(p_{2}^{2})\,2\pi\delta_{+}((q-p_{1}-p_{2})^{2})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1,2}w_{J_{i}}(p_{i}^{0},p_{i}^{0}+\hat{\omega}_{i})\delta^{(2)}(\Omega_{\vec{p}_{i}}-\Omega_{\vec{n}_{i}})\theta(p_{i}^{0}+\hat{\omega}_{i})(p_{i}^{0})^{-1}

\displaystyle\times A_{q\to p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}}{\left({A_{\tilde{q}\to p_{1}^{\prime}+p_{2}^{\prime}+p_{3}}}\right)}^{*}\,,

(109)

where the first two lines contain the integral over the phase space of the three particles with on-shell momenta
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
and
p_{3}=q-p_{1}-p_{2}
. The particles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
enter the detectors located in the direction
\vec{n}_{1}
and
\vec{n}_{2}
, respectively.
The two factors in the last line of (
3.4
) describe the transition amplitudes
|{\text{source}}\rangle\to|{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}}\rangle
and

|{p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}}\rangle\to|{\text{sink}}\rangle
. They coincide with the on-shell form factors of the operators (
99
),

\displaystyle A_{q\to p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}}=\langle{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}|\phi(0)|0}\rangle\,,

(110)

and similarly for the sink.

The evaluation of (
3.4
) can be simplified by making use of the identity (
93
),

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\omega_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\omega_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}^{\text{elastic}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\int{d^{4}p_{1}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\int{d^{4}p_{2}\over(2\pi)^{3}}2\pi\delta_{+}((q-p_{1}-p_{2})^{2})

\displaystyle\times A_{q\to p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}}{\left({A_{\tilde{q}\to p_{1}^{\prime}+p_{2}^{\prime}+p_{3}}}\right)}^{*}\prod_{i=1,2}w_{J_{i}}(p_{i}^{0},p_{i}^{0}+\hat{\omega}_{i})\theta(p_{i}^{0}+\hat{\omega}_{i})\delta^{(4)}(p_{i}-n_{i}s_{i})\,.

(111)

In this representation, it is manifest that the particle momenta
p_{i}=n_{i}s_{i}
are aligned with the null directions of the detectors and
s_{i}
play the role of their energy. Switching to the dimensionless integration variables
s_{i}\to s_{i}q^{2}/(2(qn_{i}))
and taking into account the homogeneity of the weights
w_{J_{i}}
we obtain from (
3.4
)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}}(\omega_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}}(\omega_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}^{\text{elastic}}

\displaystyle\sim\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,\delta_{+}(1-s_{1}-s_{1}+s_{1}s_{2}z)\theta(s_{1}+\omega_{1})\theta(s_{2}+\omega_{2})\mathcal{M}(s_{1},s_{2})\,.

(112)

Here the dimensionless parameters
z
and
\omega_{i}
are defined in (
51
) and (
43
), respectively, and we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(s_{1},s_{2})=A_{q\to p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}}{\left({A_{\tilde{q}\to p_{1}^{\prime}+p_{2}^{\prime}+p_{3}}}\right)}^{*}\prod_{i=1,2}w_{J_{i}}(s_{i},s_{i}+\omega_{i})\,.

(113)

The product of on-shell form factors is evaluated at

p_{i}^{\mu}=n_{i}^{\mu}s_{i}q^{2}/(2(qn_{i}))
and
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i}+n_{i}\omega_{i}
.

As was already mentioned, to order
O(a)
we have to consider two form factors (
110
) describing
the transitions
\text{source}\to s+s+g
and
\text{source}\to s+\psi+\psi
, where
s
,
\psi
and
g
denote the scalar, gaugino and gluon on-shell states.
These form factors are given by (up to overall normalization factors)

\displaystyle A(s(1)s(2)g^{\lambda}(3))=\epsilon_{\mu}^{\lambda}(p_{3})\left[{p_{1}^{\mu}\over(p_{1}p_{3})}-{p_{2}^{\mu}\over(p_{2}p_{3})}\right],

\displaystyle A(\psi(1)\psi(2)s(3))=\sqrt{2}{\langle{p_{1}p_{2}}\rangle\over(p_{1}p_{2})}={2\sqrt{2}\over[p_{2}p_{1}]}\,,

(114)

where
s(i)
denotes the scalar state with momentum
k_{i}
and similarly for the gaugino and the gluon. Here

\epsilon_{\mu}^{\lambda}(p_{3})
is the polarization vector of the gluon with helicity
\lambda=\pm 1
and in the second relation we employed the spinor-helicity representation of the on-shell momenta,
2(p_{1}p_{2})=\langle{p_{1}p_{2}}\rangle[p_{2}p_{1}]
.

Replacing the transition amplitudes
A_{q\to p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}}
in (
113
) with their expressions (
3.4
), we have to specify which particles (scalars, gauginos or gluons) enter the detectors and replace the weights
w_{J_{i}}
with the corresponding expressions (
82
) and (
87
).
As an example, consider the scalar-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}=0}(\omega_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}=0}(\omega_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}={1\over 8\pi^{2}(qn_{1})(qn_{2})}\text{SSC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\,.

(115)

In this case, the detector only selects the scalar particles and the second amplitude in (
3.4
) does not contribute. The corresponding expression for the function (
113
) looks as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\text{SSC}}(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=w^{(s)}_{S}(s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})w^{(s)}_{S}(s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime})\sum_{\lambda=\pm 1}A(s(1)s(2)g^{\lambda}(3))\overline{A(s(1^{\prime})s(2^{\prime})g^{\lambda}(3))}

\displaystyle={4\over q^{2}}{s_{1}+s_{2}-1\over(1-s_{1})(1-s_{2})}\,.

(116)

Here we replaced
w^{(s)}_{S}=1
and substituted the momenta
p_{i}=s_{i}n_{i}q^{2}/(2(qn_{i}))
and
p_{i}^{\prime}=s_{i}^{\prime}n_{i}q^{2}/(2(qn_{i}))
with
s_{i}^{\prime}=s_{i}+\omega_{i}
.

For the energy-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J_{1}=2}(\omega_{1},n_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J_{2}=0}(\omega_{2},n_{2})}\rangle_{q}={(q^{2})^{2}\over 8\pi^{2}(qn_{1})^{3}(qn_{2})}\text{ESC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\,,

(117)

the energy detector sees all the particles and the function (
113
) takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\rm ESC}

\displaystyle=w^{(s)}_{E}(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})w^{(s)}_{S}(s_{2},s^{\prime}_{2})\sum_{\lambda=\pm 1}A(s(1)s(2)g^{\lambda}(3))\overline{A(s(1^{\prime})s(2^{\prime})g^{\lambda}(3))}

\displaystyle+w^{(g)}_{E}(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})w^{(s)}_{S}(s_{2},s^{\prime}_{2})\sum_{\lambda=\pm 1}A(s(3)s(2)g^{\lambda}(1))\overline{A(s(3)s(2^{\prime})g^{\lambda}(1^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle+w^{(\psi)}_{E}(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})w^{(s)}_{S}(s_{2},s^{\prime}_{2})\,A(\psi(1)\psi(3)s(2))\overline{A(\psi(1^{\prime})\psi(3)s(2^{\prime}))}\,,

(118)

where we changed the indices
i
and
i^{\prime}
in the arguments of
A
to specify the particles that go through the detectors.
Using the expressions for the energy weights, Eqs. (
82
) and (
87
), and going through the calculation we find from (
3.4
), term-for-term,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\rm ESC}=\frac{z\left(6s_{1}\omega_{1}+6s_{1}^{2}+\omega_{1}^{2}\right)}{6(1-z)}+\frac{\left(1-s_{1}z\right){}^{2}}{(1-z)z}+\frac{\left(2s_{1}+\omega_{1}\right)\left(1-s_{1}z\right)}{1-z}\,,

(119)

where we used the delta function in (
3.4
) to replace
s_{2}=(1-s_{1})/(1-s_{1}z)
.

Substituting (
3.4
) and (
119
) into (
3.4
) we obtain the elastic contribution to the two-point functions (
115
) and (
117
),

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{\rm elastic}={1\over 1-z}I_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}(z)\,,

\displaystyle\text{ESC}^{(1)}_{\rm elastic}={1+z\omega_{1}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{6}}(z\omega_{1})^{2}\over z^{2}(1-z)}I_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}(z)\,,

(120)

where the superscript refers to the perturbative order in (
102
). The function

\displaystyle I_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}(z)=\int_{0}^{1}{zds_{1}\over 1-zs_{1}}\theta(s_{1}+\omega_{1})\theta\left({1+\omega_{2}\over 1+z\omega_{2}}-s_{1}\right)

(121)

depends on the angle variable
z
and on the energies
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
transferred by the detectors.
It takes different forms depending on the signs of
\omega_{i}
:

\displaystyle I_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\log(1-z)\,,&\ \ \omega_{1}>0\,,\omega_{2}>0\,,\\[3.41432pt]
-\log\left({1-z\over 1-z|\omega_{2}|}\right)\,,&\ \ \omega_{1}>0\,,\omega_{2}<0\,,\\
-\log\left({1-z\over(1-z|w_{1}|)(1-z|\omega_{2}|)}\right)\,,&\ \ \omega_{1}<0\,,\omega_{2}<0\,,\end{array}\right.

and satisfies the relation
I_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}(z)=I_{\omega_{2},\omega_{1}}(z)
.
The following comments are in order.

It is straightforward to verify that the obtained expressions (
3.4
) verify the crossing symmetry relations (
2.6
) and (
2.6
).
For
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=0
the inelastic contribution to the detectors vanishes and the relations (
3.4
) match the one-loop result for the two-point correlations previously found in Ref.
Belitsky:2013bja
.

The elastic contributions to the correlations (
3.4
) are proportional to the same function
I_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}(z)
and they only differ by a rational prefactor. The same is true for all the remaining two-point correlations of flow operators that can be evaluated in a similar manner. To save space, we do not present their explicit expressions here. We shall encounter these expressions later in the paper when we compute the correlations using another approach based on the correlation functions.

We observed previously that the one-point correlations of the flow operators have different dependence on the detector energies for positive and negative
\omega
. It follows from (
3.4
) and (
3.4
) that the same is true for the two-point correlations.

Inelastic contribution

In this subsection we compute the additional contribution to the correlations due to the possibility for the detectors
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\omega,n)
to create (for
\omega>0
) and annihilate (for
\omega<0
) particles.

We start with the scalar correlation (
115
). As was already mentioned,
depending on the signs of
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
, we can distinguish between four different functions

\text{SSC}_{\pm,\pm}
and
\text{SSC}_{\pm,\mp}
, with
\text{SSC}_{++}
corresponding to
\omega_{1}>0
and
\omega_{2}>0
, etc. The
crossing symmetry relations (
2.6
) allow us to limit the discussion to the two functions
\text{SSC}_{++}
and
\text{SSC}_{+-}
.
The elastic contribution to these functions is given by (
3.4
) and (
3.4
).

Figure 3:
Creation of particles by the scalar detector
\mathcal{O}_{J=0}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})
.

To one-loop order, the creation of particles by the scalar detector
\mathcal{O}_{J=0}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})
is described by the diagrams shown in Figure
3
.
The annihilation of particles is described by similar diagrams in which the initial and final states are swapped. These diagrams contain interaction vertices describing the quartic scalar coupling and the interaction of scalars with gluons in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. The creation and annihilation of particles by the detector
\mathcal{O}_{J=0}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})
is described by analogous diagrams.

It is clear from Figure
3
that to order
O(a)
only one of the detectors can create/annihilate particles. This leads to the following expressions for the correlations:

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{inelastic}}

\displaystyle=\langle{\tilde{q}|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{creation}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|q}\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle{\tilde{q}|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|{\cal O}^{\text{creation}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|q}\rangle\,,

(125)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{+-}^{\text{inelastic}}

\displaystyle=\langle{\tilde{q}|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{annihil.}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|q}\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle{\tilde{q}|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|{\cal O}^{\text{creation}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|q}\rangle\,,

(126)

where
\langle{ssss|q}\rangle
and
\langle{ss|q}\rangle
are the transition amplitudes for the source going to two- and four-scalar states and the detector operators were defined in Eqs. (
77
) – (
3.1
).

Let us consider the first term in the expression for
\text{SSC}_{++}
, Eq. (
3.4
). According to (
3.1
), the operator
{\cal O}^{\text{creation}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})
creates a pair of scalars with momenta
p_{1}=sn_{2}
and
p_{2}=(\omega_{2}-s)n_{2}
, so that the four-particle state looks
as
|{s(p_{1})s(p_{2})s(k_{1})s(k_{2}))}\rangle
. Here
k_{1}
is the momentum of the scalar that leaves the detector
{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})
. The incoming two-particle state is
|{s(k_{1}^{\prime})s(k_{2})}\rangle
where
k_{1}^{\prime}
is the momentum of the scalar that enters the detector
{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1})
. The momenta
k_{1}
and
k_{1}^{\prime}
should be aligned with the null vectors of the detectors,
k_{1}=En_{1}
and
k_{1}^{\prime}=(E-\omega_{1})n_{1}
. This leads to

\displaystyle\langle{\tilde{q}|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{creation}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|q}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\omega_{2}}ds\int dE\,{\left({A_{\tilde{q}\to s(p_{1})s(p_{2})s(k_{1})s(k_{2})}}\right)}^{*}A_{q\to s(k_{1}^{\prime})s(k_{2})}2\pi\delta_{+}((q-k_{1}^{\prime})^{2})\,,

(127)

where the integration goes over the energy of the scalars
s(p_{1})
and
s(k_{1})
.
Here we took into account that the scalar detector assigns a trivial weight to the scalar particles,
w^{(s)}_{S}=1
.
The delta function in (
3.4
) comes from the on-shell propagator of the scalar
s(k_{2})
with momentum
k_{2}=q-k_{1}^{\prime}
.
Replacing
k_{1}^{\prime}=(E-\omega_{1})n_{1}
we find that the on-shell condition
(q-k_{1}^{\prime})^{2}=0
leads to
E=(\omega_{1}+1)q^{2}/(2(qn_{1}))
and fixes
k_{1}=En_{1}
unambiguously.

The transition amplitudes entering (
3.4
) are given by
A_{q\to s(k_{1}^{\prime})s(k_{2})}=1
and

\displaystyle A_{\tilde{q}\to s(p_{1})s(p_{2})s(k_{1})s(k_{2})}

\displaystyle={1\over(p_{1}+k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}}{-}{1\over(p_{1}+p_{2}+k_{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle+{1\over 2(p_{1}k_{2})}\left[{2(p_{2}(p_{1}-k_{2}))\over(p_{1}+p_{2}+k_{2})^{2}}-{2(k_{1}(p_{1}-k_{2}))\over(p_{1}+k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}}\right].

(128)

This relation takes into account the contribution of the diagrams shown in Figure
3
. Substituting the particle momenta
p_{1}=sn_{2}
,
p_{2}=(\omega_{2}-s)n_{2}
,
k_{1}=n_{1}(\omega_{1}+1)q^{2}/(2(qn_{1}))
and
k_{2}=q-k_{1}+n_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}
, and performing the integration in (
3.4
) we obtain

\displaystyle\langle{\tilde{q}|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{creation}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|q}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\sim-\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)z}{(1-z)\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\log\left(1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\over\omega_{1}+1\right)\theta(\omega_{2})\theta(1+\omega_{1})\,.

(129)

The calculation of the first term in the expression for
\text{SSC}_{+-}
, Eq. (
3.4
), goes along the same lines.
We have

\displaystyle\langle{\tilde{q}|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{annihil.}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|q}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{-\omega_{2}}ds\int dE\,{\left({A_{\tilde{q}\to s(k_{1}^{\prime})s(k_{2})}}\right)}^{*}A_{q\to s(p_{1})s(p_{2})s(k_{1})s(k_{2})}2\pi\delta_{+}((\tilde{q}-k_{1}^{\prime})^{2})\,,

(130)

where
p_{1}=sn_{1}
and
p_{2}=-\omega_{2}n_{2}-p_{1}
are the momenta of the scalars absorbed by the detector
{\cal O}^{\text{annihil.}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})
. The momenta of the two remaining scalars are
k_{1}=En_{1}
,
k_{1}^{\prime}=n_{1}(E+\omega_{1})
and
k_{2}=\tilde{q}-k_{1}^{\prime}
. As in the previous case, the relation
(\tilde{q}-k_{1}^{\prime})^{2}=0
fixes the energy
E=(1+\omega_{2})/(1+z\omega_{2})q^{2}/(2(qn_{1}))
. Going through the calculation of (
3.4
) we find

\displaystyle\langle{\tilde{q}|ss}\rangle\langle{ss|{\cal O}^{\text{elastic}}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}^{\text{annihil.}}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})|ssss}\rangle\langle{ssss|q}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\sim-\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z}{(1-z)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}\log\left({1\over\omega_{2}+1}\right)\theta(-\omega_{2})\theta(1+\omega_{2})\,.

(131)

Note that this relation is independent of
\omega_{1}
.

Substituting the relations (
3.4
) and (
3.4
) into (
3.4
) and (
3.4
), we obtain the inelastic contribution to the scalar-scalar correlations.
Combining it with the elastic contribution (
3.4
) we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{++}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-z}\log\left(\frac{1}{1-z}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{1}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{1}z+1)}\log{\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over\omega_{1}+1}\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{2}z+1)}\log{\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over\omega_{2}+1}\right)}\,,

(132)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{+-}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1+\omega_{2}z}{1-z}\right)}{1-z}+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z}{2(1-z)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}\right)\,.

(133)

Here the first terms in both relations correspond to the elastic contribution and the remaining terms describe the inelastic contribution. The relations (
3.4
) and (
133
) are valid for
0<z<1
and they do not take into account contact terms of the types
\delta(z)
and
\delta(1-z)
.

For
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=0
the inelastic contribution to (
3.4
) and (
133
) vanishes and one recovers the known result for the SSC derived in Ref.
Belitsky:2013bja
.
We observe that the above relations are given by a linear combination of logarithms with rational coefficients. To make this property manifest, it is convenient to assign weight
1
to the logarithm and weight
0
to the rational functions. Then, all the terms in the expressions for the correlations have the same weight
1
. This is yet another manifestation of the uniform weight property previously observed for various quantities in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM.

Applying the crossing symmetry relations (
2.6
) we can find from (
3.4
) and (
133
) the two remaining scalar-scalar correlations,

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{--}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-z}\log\left(\frac{(1+\omega_{1}z)(1+\omega_{2}z)}{1-z}\right)

(134)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{1}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{1}z+1)}\log{\left({1\over\omega_{1}+1}\right)}+\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{2}z+1)}\log{\left({1\over\omega_{2}+1}\right)}\,,

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{-+}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{+-}(\omega_{2},\omega_{1},z)\,.

(135)

Here the last relation can be obtained by taking into account the symmetry of the SSC under the exchange of the detectors.

Comparing the above expressions we observe an interesting relation,

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{++}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)+\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{--}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)=\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{+-}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)+\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{-+}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\,,

(136)

where it is tacitly assumed that the functions are continued from the domain of their validity (positive or negative
\omega
’s) to arbitrary real
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
. We elucidate the origin of this relation in Section
4.4
below. We will show that
it is rather general and holds for the various correlations both at weak and strong coupling.

It is straightforward to extend the above analysis to the correlations involving charge and energy detectors. These detectors receive contributions from all the particles (scalars, gauginos and gluons). Due to the growing number of relevant diagrams, the calculation is more involved. To save space we do not present it here. In the next section, we introduce a more efficient approach to computing the various correlations based on correlation functions. We have checked that the two approaches yield the same expressions for the correlations.

4
Generalized event shapes with scalar detectors

In this section, we consider the four-point Wightman function of scalar primaries. We introduce the Mellin representation
for the connected part of this function and use it
to compute the
\omega
-deformed
event shapes (
39
). This section generalizes the analysis of

Belitsky:2013xxa
;
Belitsky:2013bja
to
\omega_{i}\neq 0
.

Following
Belitsky:2013bja
, we consider the four-point function of
{\bf 20}^{\prime}
operators in
{\cal N}=4
SYM

\displaystyle\langle\phi^{\dagger}(x_{4})O(x_{1},S_{1})O(x_{2},S_{2})\phi(x_{3})\rangle=\frac{G^{\bf(105)}(u,v)}{x_{12}^{4}x_{34}^{4}}\,,

(137)

where the explicit expressions for the operators can be found in
(
99
), (
100
) and (
105
), and the two conformal cross-ratios are defined as

\displaystyle u={x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\,,\qquad\qquad v={x_{14}^{2}x_{23}^{2}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\ \,.

(138)

In (
137
) the notation
G^{\bf(105)}(u,v)
emphasizes that with the choice of R-symmetry polarizations
(
105
) only the
{\bf 105}
representation of
SU(4)
appears in the OPE of the detector
operators
O(x_{1},S_{1})O(x_{2},S_{2})
. This particular representation played a privileged role in
Belitsky:2013xxa
because for
\hat{\omega}_{i}=0
it is very simply related to the energy-energy correlation, which is the observable of our prime interest.

It is convenient to write
G^{\bf(105)}(u,v)
as follows

\displaystyle G^{\bf(105)}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{T}}{2(2\pi)^{4}}\Big(u^{2}+\frac{u^{2}}{v^{2}}\Big)+\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{u^{2}}{v}\Big(\frac{1}{2}+u\Phi(u,v)\Big),

(139)

where the central charge
c_{T}
is given by

\displaystyle c_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{4}.

(140)

Let us briefly comment on the structure of (
139
). The rational term
\frac{c_{T}}{2(2\pi)^{4}}\Big(u^{2}+\frac{u^{2}}{v^{2}}\Big)
is the disconnected part of the correlation function. It does not contribute to the generalized event shapes at separated points (
z\neq 0
) and can be safely discarded.
The other rational term
\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{u^{2}}{2v}
is the connected correlator at zero coupling (i.e. Born level or free theory). Finally, the most important for our purposes part of (
139
) is the function
\Phi(u,v)
. At weak coupling it encodes the perturbative corrections, i.e. it is proportional the ’t Hooft coupling constant
a\equiv{g_{\mathrm{YM}}^{2}N_{c}}/{(4\pi^{2})}
. This function satisfies the crossing symmetry relations

\displaystyle\Phi(u,v)=\Phi(v,u)=\frac{1}{v}\Phi\left(\frac{u}{v},\frac{1}{v}\right)\,.

(141)

The starting point of our discussion is the Mellin representation of the function
\Phi(u,v)
in the form used in

Belitsky:2013xxa
;
Belitsky:2013bja

\displaystyle\Phi(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\cal C}_{0}}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}\left[\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(1-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\right]^{2}M(j_{1},j_{2})u^{j_{1}}v^{j_{2}}\ ,

(142)

where the integration contour
{\cal C}_{0}
runs parallel to the imaginary axis and satisfies
{\rm Re}(j_{1}+j_{2})>-1
and
{\rm Re}(j_{1}),{\rm Re}(j_{2})<0
.
The crossing symmetry (
141
) implies that

\displaystyle M(j_{1},j_{2})=M(j_{2},j_{1})={(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(j_{1}+j_{2}+1)^{2}\over j_{1}^{2}(1+j_{1})^{2}}M(-1-j_{1}-j_{2},j_{2})\,.

(143)

Equivalently, if we write

\displaystyle M(j_{1},j_{2})=(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(j_{1}+j_{2}+1)^{2}\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})

(144)

then
\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})
is
fully crossing-symmetric
\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})=\tilde{M}(j_{2},j_{1})=\tilde{M}(-1-j_{1}-j_{2},j_{2})
.

The leading weak and strong coupling results take the form

\displaystyle M^{\text{weak}}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle=-{a\over 4}{(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}\over j_{1}^{2}j_{2}^{2}},

\displaystyle M^{\text{strong}}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle=-{1\over 2}{(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(1+j_{1}+j_{2})\over j_{1}j_{2}}\ \,.

(145)

Using the
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry Ward identities, similar but more complicated expressions can be written for other four-point functions, in particular those with the insertions of the R-symmetry current and the stress-energy tensor at the detector points 1 and 2 (see Section
5
).

Our next task is to go from the Mellin representation of the correlator to the Mellin representation of generalized event shapes. This amounts to three steps:

1.

Take the detector limit (
31
).

2.

Fourier transform with respect to the detector working time.

3.

Fourier transform with respect to the position of the sink.

In the Mellin representation of the correlator (
142
) all the dependence on the positions of the operators is encoded in the factor
u^{j_{1}}v^{j_{2}}
, to which we apply the steps above. All the dynamical information (i.e., the coupling dependence) on the other hand is contained in the Mellin amplitude
M(j_{1},j_{2})
which factors out. This computation is a straightforward generalization of the one done in
Belitsky:2013xxa
and we refer the reader to appendix
E
for the details. The result takes the following form

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}(\omega_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}(\omega_{2},n_{2})\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle={1\over 8\pi^{2}}{\text{SSC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\over(qn_{1})(qn_{2})},

(146)

where
\text{SSC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)
is the convolution of the Mellin amplitude of the four-point correlation function
M(j_{1},j_{2})
and the detector kernel
K_{\text{SS}}

\displaystyle\text{SSC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\cal C}_{0}}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}M(j_{1},j_{2})K_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\,.

(147)

In the undeformed case
\omega_{i}=0
the detector kernel
K_{\text{SS}}
is very simple
Belitsky:2013bja
:

\displaystyle K_{\text{S}\text{S}}(j_{1},j_{2}|0,0,z)={2\pi\over\sin\pi(j_{1}+j_{2})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\left(1-z\right)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}\ \,.

(148)

Let us now see how this simple result changes when
\omega_{i}\neq 0
.

We start by quoting the result for the detector kernel in the most compact form. As discussed earlier we consider separately the cases where the detector frequencies have the same sign
\omega_{i}\geq 0
(we denote this case by
++
), and the case when they have opposite signs
\omega_{1}\geq 0
,
\omega_{2}\leq 0
(we denote this case by
+-
). The result takes the form (see Appendix
E
for details)

\displaystyle K_{\text{S}\text{S}}^{+-}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle={1\over 2(1-z)}\Big(-{z\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over 1+\omega_{2}}\Big)^{-j_{2}}\Big({z(1+\omega_{2})\over 1-z}\Big)^{-j_{1}}{\Gamma(1-j_{1})^{2}\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\over\Gamma(1+j_{2}-j_{1})}

\displaystyle\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(j_{2},1-j_{1},1+j_{2}-j_{1},{(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})\over\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)}\right)\,,

(149)

\displaystyle K_{\text{S}\text{S}}^{++}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle={1\over 2(1-z)}\Big({z\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over 1+\omega_{2}}\Big)^{-j_{2}}\Big({z(1+\omega_{2})\over 1-z}\Big)^{-j_{1}}\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(1-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})

\displaystyle\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(j_{2},1-j_{1},1,1-{(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})\over\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)}\right)\,\,.

(150)

If one of the frequencies vanishes, the kernels become simply related to the undeformed one in (
148
), e.g.

\displaystyle K_{\text{S}\text{S}}^{+-}(j_{1},j_{2}|0,\omega_{2},z)=K_{\text{S}\text{S}}^{++}(j_{1},j_{2}|0,\omega_{2},z)=K_{\text{S}\text{S}}(j_{1},j_{2}|0,0,z)(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}.

(151)

While the above formulas are rather compact, they obscure some of the properties of the kernels which are useful in the subsequent computation of the Mellin integral in (
147
). Below we present another representation of the kernels which makes these properties manifest.

4.1
SSC kernel: opposite signs

Let us first consider the case where the frequencies of the detectors have opposite signs, namely
\omega_{1}\omega_{2}<0
. This covers the cases
+-
and
-+
.
The kernel (
4
) can be recast in the following convenient form (see appendix
E
for the derivation)

\displaystyle{K^{+-}_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\over K_{\text{S}\text{S}}(j_{1},j_{2}|0,0,z)}=(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{{\cal C}_{K}}{ds\over 2\pi i}\Gamma(-s){\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2}-s)\Gamma(j_{2}+s)\Gamma(1-j_{1})\over\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{2})\Gamma(1-j_{1}-s)}\Big(-{(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{s}\,.

(152)

The integration contour
{\cal C}_{K}
runs parallel to the imaginary axis and separates the poles generated by the product of gamma functions in the numerator, i.e. ascending poles coming from
\Gamma(\dots-s)
from the left and descending poles coming from
\Gamma(\dots+s)
from the right, see Figure
4
.

0
2
1
3
4
-1-j_{2}
-j_{2}
-2-j_{2}
-3-j_{2}
-4-j_{2}
1-j_{1}-j_{2}
5-j_{1}-j_{2}
4-j_{1}-j_{2}
3-j_{1}-j_{2}
2-j_{1}-j_{2}
s

Figure 4:
Integration contour
{\cal C}_{K}
in the
s
-plane for the kernel in (
4.1
). The poles come from
\Gamma(-s)\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2}-s)\Gamma(j_{2}+s)
. In drawing the plot we used the fact that
{\rm Re}[j_{2}]<0
and
-1<{\rm Re}[j_{1}+j_{2}]<0
as in (
142
).

For
\text{SSC}_{-+}
we define

\displaystyle K^{-+}_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\equiv K^{+-}_{\text{SS}}(j_{2},j_{1}|\omega_{2},\omega_{1},z),

(153)

where on the right-hand side we permuted both
\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}
and
j_{1}\leftrightarrow j_{2}
using the symmetry of the Mellin amplitude
M(j_{1},j_{2})=M(j_{2},j_{1})
.

The Mellin integral in (
4.1
) can be done explicitly resulting in (
4
). The representation (
4.1
) makes many of the properties of the kernel manifest, so we can equally use it instead. Let us discuss these properties one by one.

Firstly, consider the small
\omega_{i}\to 0
limit of the kernel. This corresponds to closing the
s
-contour in
(
4.1
)
to the right with the contribution from
s=0
producing
1
and thus correctly reproducing (
148
). Similarly, if we are interested in the small
\omega_{i}
expansion of our event shapes, we can consistently do it by keeping more and more residues from the poles at
s=n
and
s=1-j_{1}-j_{2}+n
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. The poles at
s=n
correspond to the analytic part
(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{n}
of the expansion of the kernel around
\omega_{i}=0
. The poles at
s=1-j_{1}-j_{2}+n
result in the non-analytic part
(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}+n}
.

Secondly, the ratio of kernels (
4.1
) is analytic for
{\rm Re}(j_{2})>0
,
{\rm Re}(j_{1})<0
and fixed
j_{1}+j_{2}
. This is clear from the fact that
no pinch in the
s
integration contour arises in this case. This is particularly useful if we take into account that
K_{\text{S}\text{S}}(j_{2},j_{1}|z,0,0)
is analytic for fixed
j_{1}+j_{2}
as well. We find this property very helpful in the perturbative computations.

For
\omega_{1}>0
,
\omega_{2}<0
and
j_{1}+j_{2}
fixed the kernel decays if we deform the
j_{1}
contour to the left. The fact that the kernel is analytic means that we only pick the contribution from the poles of
M(j_{1},j_{2})
. As can be seen from (
4
), both at weak and at strong coupling only a single pole at
j_{2}=0
contributes. This dramatically simplifies the calculation.

Thirdly, note that
\text{SSC}_{+-}
should go into itself under the combination of the crossing symmetry transformation (
2.6
) and the permutation of the detectors. This is indeed reflected in the following property of the kernel,

\displaystyle K^{+-}_{\text{SS}}(j_{2},j_{1}|\omega_{2},\omega_{1},z)={1\over(1+\omega_{1}z)(1+\omega_{2}z)}K^{+-}_{\text{SS}}(j_{2},j_{1}|\omega_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\,.

(154)

To check this property note that the argument of the
s
-integral in (
4.1
) is invariant under this transformation, namely

\displaystyle-{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)\over 1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z}=-{\omega_{1}^{\prime}\omega_{2}^{\prime}(1-z^{\prime})\over 1+\omega_{1}^{\prime}+\omega_{2}^{\prime}+\omega_{1}^{\prime}\omega_{2}^{\prime}z^{\prime}}\ ,

(155)

which makes checking (
154
) trivial.

Using the properties of the kernel described above and the explicit expressions (
4
) for the Mellin amplitudes it is easy to compute

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{+-}^{\text{weak}}

\displaystyle={a\over 4}\bigg[\frac{\log\left(\frac{1+\omega_{2}z}{1-z}\right)}{1-z}+{(1+\omega_{2})z\over 2(1-z)(1+z\omega_{2})}\log\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}\right)\bigg],

(156)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{+-}^{\text{strong}}

\displaystyle={z^{2}\over 2}\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}\,\,.

(157)

Let us reiterate that only the
j_{2}=0
pole of the Mellin amplitude contributes to the above computations.
The expressions for
\text{SSC}_{-+}
can be obtained from
\text{SSC}_{+-}
by permuting
\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}
.
We have also checked our calculation by performing the Mellin integrals numerically and found perfect agreement with the formulas above.

4.2
SSC kernel: same sign

Consider next the case when the frequencies of the detectors have the same sign,
\omega_{1}\omega_{2}>0
. This covers both cases
++
and
--
. The convenient representation of the kernel (
4
) takes the form (see appendix
E
for the derivation)

\displaystyle{K^{\text{++}}_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\over K_{\text{S}\text{S}}(j_{1},j_{2}|0,0,z)}=(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{{\cal C}_{K}}{ds\over 2\pi i}\Gamma(-s){\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2}-s)\Gamma(j_{2}+s)\Gamma(j_{1}+s)\over\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1})}\Big({\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{s},

(158)

where the contour of integration
{\cal C}_{K}
is defined in the same way as in (
4.1
).

Let us comment on the relevant properties of (
4.2
).
Firstly, as expected, the kernel is invariant under the permutation of the detectors
\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}
combined with
j_{1}\leftrightarrow j_{2}
. The latter exchange does not affect the event shape since
M(j_{1},j_{2})=M(j_{2},j_{1})
. Due to the relation (which one can explicitly check using (
4.2
))

\displaystyle K^{\text{++}}_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)={1\over(1+\omega_{1}z)(1+\omega_{2}z)}K^{\text{++}}_{\text{SS}}(j_{2},j_{1}|\omega_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\,,

(159)

where
\omega_{i}^{\prime}<0
, and the crossing symmetry relations (
2.6
), the right-hand side of (
4.2
) defines the kernel for the
--
case as well.

Secondly, as before it is trivial to extract the
\omega_{i}\to 0
limit from (
4.2
) by closing the Mellin contour in (
4.2
) to the right and picking the contributions form the poles at
s=n
and
s=1-j_{1}-j_{2}+n
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. For example, the case
\omega_{i}=0
is simply given by the residue at
s=0
, which produces
1
for the ratio (
4.2
). These two series of poles can be resummed into a pair of hypergeometric functions (
E
).

Thirdly, there is an important difference in the analytic properties of the kernel (
4.2
) compared to the
+-
case.
Namely, for fixed
j_{1}+j_{2}
the kernel has an infinite number of singularities both to the left and to the right of the
j_{1}
contour. This means that in evaluating the Mellin integrals we have to deal with infinitely many poles in both Mellin variables, even though the Mellin amplitudes of interest (
4
) are relatively simple. We will see that the result can still be computed analytically.

Because of the complicated properties of the Mellin kernel,
the computation of
\text{SSC}_{++}
is more difficult than in the
+-
case. The result agrees with the amplitude computation and takes the following form
(the details of the computation can be found in Appendix
F
)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{weak}}

\displaystyle={a\over 4}\bigg[\frac{1}{1-z}\log\left(\frac{1}{1-z}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{1}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{1}z+1)}\log{\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over\omega_{1}+1}\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{2}z+1)}\log{\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over\omega_{2}+1}\right)}\bigg],

(160)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{strong}}

\displaystyle={z^{2}\over 2}\bigg[\frac{6\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{5}}+\frac{3\omega_{1}\left(2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\omega_{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+2\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{3}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}\bigg]\,.

(161)

As a consistency check, we verify that the relations (
156
) and (
4.2
) coincide for
\omega_{2}=0
.
The singularities at
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z=0
are related to the bulk point singularities of the correlator. They only appear at strong coupling and we discuss them in more details in Section
4.5
.

For the
--
case, we use the transformation (
2.6
) to get

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{--}^{\text{weak}}

\displaystyle={a\over 4}\bigg[\frac{1}{1-z}\log\left(\frac{(\omega_{1}z+1)(\omega_{2}z+1)}{1-z}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{1}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{1}z+1)}\log{\left({1\over\omega_{1}+1}\right)}+\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)z}{2(1-z)(\omega_{2}z+1)}\log{\left({1\over\omega_{2}+1}\right)}\bigg],

(162)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{--}^{\text{strong}}

\displaystyle={z^{2}\over 2}\bigg[\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{3}}+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{6\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{5}}-\frac{3\omega_{1}\left(2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\omega_{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+2\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{3}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}\bigg]\,.

(163)

We recall that the above relations are valid for
0<z<1
and
\omega_{i}>-1
.

4.3
Closing the contour

The reader might have noted something puzzling about our result above: if the kernel does not depend on the choice
++
versus
--
, as explained around (
159
),
how is it possible that the expressions for
\text{SSC}_{++}
and
\text{SSC}_{--}
are different?

To understand the reason,
it is instructive to consider the following simple integral

\displaystyle\int_{-\epsilon-i\infty}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}{dj\over 2\pi i}{(1+\omega)^{-j}\over j}=-\theta(\omega)\,.

(164)

For negative
\omega
we evaluate the integral by closing the contour to the left and get zero. For positive
\omega
we are allowed to close the contour to the right. Picking up the residue of the pole at
j=0
we arrive at (
164
).

The same mechanism is at work for our observables. Consider first the simpler case
\omega_{1}=0
with the kernel given by (
151
). Switching to the integration variables
j_{1}
and
j=j_{1}+j_{2}
in the Mellin integral, we see that the dependence on
j_{1}
enters only through the factor
(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}
. Therefore, as in the simple example (
164
) above, the allowed direction of closing the
j_{1}
contour depends on the sign of
\omega_{2}
. For
\omega_{2}\geq 0
we can close the contour to the right, whereas for
\omega_{2}\leq 0
we can close the contour to the left. This can lead to different results and it explains the difference between
\text{SSC}(0,\omega_{2},z)
for positive and negative
\omega_{2}
, even though the Mellin kernel does not depend on the sign of
\omega_{2}
.

For
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\neq 0
, the kernels become much more complicated. Nevertheless, one can identify by inspection the allowed directions of closing the
j_{1},j_{2}
contours, depending on the parameters of the problem. We found that the representations of the kernel (
E
) and (
E
) are particularly useful for this purpose. The result is, as expected, that the generalized event shapes are not analytic around
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}=0
.

4.4
Detector identity

We observe that both at weak and strong coupling the scalar-scalar correlations satisfy the identity (cf. (
136
))

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{--}+\text{SSC}_{++}-\text{SSC}_{+-}-\text{SSC}_{-+}=0\,.

(165)

It is understood as an
analytic continuation
from the region where each function has been originally computed to some
common values of
\omega_{i}
. In Section
6
we check that this relation also holds for the other detectors
except for the energy one. In the latter case, the identity is violated at weak coupling due to the contribution from the cross-talk between the detectors.
Here we derive a general formula for the left-hand side of (
165
) which is valid at finite coupling and from which its vanishing at weak and strong coupling follows immediately.

In order to proceed we have to specify the analytic continuation in
\omega
of the event shapes in (
165
). Consider for example
\text{SSC}_{++}
written for the detector operators defined in the null plane conformal frame (
2.2
). We have schematically

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle O_{4}O_{1}(u_{1})O_{2}(u_{2})O_{3}\rangle\,,

(166)

where we suppressed the dependence on the positions of the source and the sink, as well as the transverse coordinates of the detector operators. Such details will not be relevant for what follows.

Since the light-ray operators with
\hat{\omega}_{i}\geq 0
annihilate the left vacuum (see Eq. (
2.3
)), we can rewrite (
166
) in the following equivalent way

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle[[O_{4},O_{1}(u_{1})],O_{2}(u_{2})]O_{3}\rangle\,.

(167)

Now we can ‘analytically continue’
\text{SSC}_{++}
to any sign of
\hat{\omega}_{i}
by using Eq. (
167
) instead of (
166
) for any signs of
\hat{\omega}_{i}
. Let us emphasize again that (
167
) is equivalent to (
166
) only for
\hat{\omega}_{i}>0
but they differ otherwise. Similarly, we get the following relations for other choices of the signs of
\omega_{i}

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{--}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle O_{4}[O_{1}(u_{1}),[O_{2}(u_{2}),O_{3}]\rangle,

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle[O_{4},O_{1}(u_{1})][O_{2}(u_{2}),O_{3}]\rangle,

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle[O_{4},O_{2}(u_{2})][O_{1}(u_{1}),O_{3}]\rangle,

(168)

where we used again Eq. (
2.3
). Switching to commutators does not produce any effect in the region where each of the event shapes was originally defined. However, as we go away from this region (by flipping some of the signs of
\omega_{i}
), formulas (
167
) and (
4.4
) define the analytic continuation needed to check the identity (
165
).

Now we can evaluate the formulas above for any sign of
\hat{\omega}_{i}
. We get the following result (where we also use the fact that the detectors commute with each other for
z\neq 0
)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{--}+\text{SSC}_{++}-\text{SSC}_{+-}-\text{SSC}_{-+}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\Big[\langle O_{1}(u_{1})O_{2}(u_{2})O_{4}O_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle O_{4}O_{3}O_{1}(u_{1})O_{2}(u_{2})\rangle-\langle O_{1}(u_{1})O_{4}O_{3}O_{2}(u_{2})\rangle-\langle O_{2}(u_{2})O_{4}O_{3}O_{1}(u_{1})\rangle\Big]\,.

(169)

Let us now specify the signs of
\hat{\omega}_{i}
and choose, for example,
\hat{\omega}_{i}>0
. Since the positive frequency detectors annihilate the left vacuum (see Eq. (
2.3
)), we find that only one term survives,

\displaystyle{\text{SSC}_{--}+\text{SSC}_{++}-\text{SSC}_{+-}-\text{SSC}_{-+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\omega}_{i}>0}}{{=}}\int du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle O_{4}O_{3}O(u_{1})O(u_{2})\rangle\,.}

(170)

This formula is completely general and it should hold for any choice of the detectors.

Compared to (
166
), the Wightman function on the right-hand side of (
170
) has different operator ordering.
In a close analogy with (
147
), the integral over
u_{i}
in (
170
) can be expressed as the convolution of the Mellin amplitude
M(j_{1},j_{2})
and the kernel

\tilde{K}_{SS}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)
. Going through the same steps as in Appendix
E
, we find that this kernel takes the form

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{SS}(j_{1},j_{2}|

\displaystyle\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)={(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)(qn_{1})(qn_{2})\over 2^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}(n_{1}n_{2})^{j_{1}+j_{2}}}\int_{0}^{\hat{\omega}_{1}}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{\hat{\omega}_{2}}ds_{2}

\displaystyle\times\big[(\hat{\omega}_{1}-s_{1})s_{2}\big]^{-j_{1}}\big[s_{1}(\hat{\omega}_{2}-s_{2})\big]^{-j_{2}}\big[(q+n_{1}s_{1}+n_{2}s_{2})^{2}\big]^{j_{1}+j_{2}-2}\,.

(171)

When integrated against the Mellin amplitudes (
4
) it gives zero both at weak and strong coupling.
Indeed, from the integral representation (
4.4
) it follows that
\tilde{K}_{SS}
is an analytic function of
j_{2}
for
{\rm Re}\,j_{2}<0
. The same is true for the Mellin amplitudes (
4
). Closing the integration contour for
{\rm Re}\,j_{2}<0
we get that the integral on the right-hand side of (
170
) is zero.
For more complicated Mellin amplitudes with extra poles at negative
j_{2}
the integral in (
170
) will not vanish.
This happens in particular for the correlations involving the energy detector.
We checked that (
170
) correctly reproduces our results in these cases as well.

4.5
Bulk point singularity

The reader might have noticed something peculiar about the expressions for SSC at strong coupling, Eqs. (
157
) and (
4.2
). We see that there is a new type of singularity that appears in
\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{strong}}
at

\displaystyle\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z=0\,,

(172)

but is absent in
\text{SSC}_{+-}^{\text{strong}}
.
This locus cannot be reached for
\omega_{1,2}>0
and
0<z<1
but a singularity appears if we analytically continue
\omega_{2}
to negative values. Using (
167
) and (
4.4
) we get

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\omega}_{2}<0}}{{=}}\text{SSC}_{+-}-\int du_{1}du_{2}\,e^{-i\sum_{k=1}^{2}\hat{\omega}_{k}u_{k}}\langle O_{2}(u_{2})O_{4}O_{1}(u_{1})O_{3}\rangle\,.

(173)

Given that
\text{SSC}_{+-}^{\text{strong}}
is regular at (
172
), we deduce that the singular contribution is generated by the second term in (
173
).

Coming back to the four-point correlation function (
137
), it is a well-known fact that there are certain Lorentzian singularities which are present at strong coupling but are absent at weak coupling
Gary:2009ae
, namely the bulk point limit singularities
Maldacena:2015iua
. It is therefore natural to conjecture that the singularity at (
172
) is nothing but an avatar of the bulk point singularity in the
\omega
-deformed event shape. We will now demonstrate that this is indeed the case.

Recall that the emergence of the bulk point singularity in Mellin space is related to the behavior of the Mellin amplitude (
142
) in the limit
j_{i}\to\infty
with
{j_{1}/j_{2}}
fixed, see
Penedones:2010ue
. We see from (
4
) that

\displaystyle M^{\text{weak}}(\lambda j_{1},\lambda j_{2})=O(\lambda^{-2})\,,\qquad\qquad M^{\text{strong}}(\lambda j_{1},\lambda j_{2})=O(\lambda)\,,

(174)

and indeed at strong coupling the Mellin amplitude grows as
\lambda\to\infty
.

To make the connection between the bulk point singularity and the structure of the
\omega
-deformed event shapes more direct, let us consider the following toy model

\displaystyle M_{p}(j_{1},j_{2})=(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(1+j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}\big[j_{1}j_{2}(1+j_{1}+j_{2})\big]^{p-2}\,,

(175)

where
p
is positive integer.
This Mellin amplitude is polynomial for
p\geq 2
and thus corresponds to a contact quartic interaction in the AdS dual theory
Penedones:2010ue
.
In the large
j
limit we have
M_{p}(\lambda j_{1},\lambda j_{2})=O(\lambda^{3p-2})
.

We now want to compute the
\omega
-deformed event shapes (
147
) for the toy Mellin amplitude (
175
). First, one can readily see that

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{p,+-}=0\,.

(176)

This immediately follows from the analytic properties of the kernel described in the previous sections. A more interesting case is
\text{SSC}_{p,++}
for which we get

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{p,++}=z^{2}{f_{p}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{6p-1}}+\dots\ \,.

(177)

The details of the computation are given in Appendix
G
. For the first few values of
p
we find

\displaystyle f_{1}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=-12(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,,

\displaystyle f_{2}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=6\times 7!\times(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{3}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})^{2}\,.

(178)

As expected, the maximal power of the singularity in (
177
) is linked to the growth of the amplitude in the large
\lambda
limit.

Let us also write down the complete answer for
p=2

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{p=2,++}={12\omega_{1}^{3}\omega_{2}^{3}\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{11}}\sum_{k=0}^{7}c_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{k}\,,

(179)

where the explicit form of the coefficients
c_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
can be found in appendix
G
.

The function

\text{SSC}_{p=2,++}
has interesting properties in the zero frequency limit
\omega_{i}\to 0
. If we consider
\omega_{1}\to 0
with
\omega_{2}
fixed the result vanishes as
O(\omega_{1}^{3})
. If on the other hand we take both
\omega_{i}\to 0
at the same rate, the result diverges as
\text{SSC}_{p=2,++}(z,\lambda\omega_{1},\lambda\omega_{2})\sim{1/\lambda}
.

Another interesting subtlety related to polynomial Mellin amplitudes of the type (
175
) is that they lead to nontrivial distributional terms in the
generalized event shapes which are
supported at
\omega_{i}=0
. Let us consider for simplicity
\omega_{1}=0
and
p=2
(this example will also be relevant in section
7
when we discuss one-loop supergravity and stringy corrections).
In this case we apply (
147
), (
148
) and (
151
) to get the following expression for the scalar-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{p=2}(0,\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\int{djdj_{1}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}{\pi\over 2\sin\pi j}z^{-j}\left(1-z\right)^{j-1}j^{2}(1+j)^{2}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}

\displaystyle=2z^{2}(1-6z+6z^{2})\delta(\omega_{2})\,.

(180)

Here the
j_{1}
integral was obtained by differentiating (
164
) with respect to
\omega
.

5
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry Ward identities for the kernels

In the previous sections we used the known results for the four-point correlation functions of scalar half-BPS operators in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM to compute the correlation between the scalar detectors SSC. In this section, we extend the consideration to more complicated correlations that involve charge and energy flow operators. According to their definition (
31
), they are light-ray operators built out of the
R-
current
J^{R}_{\mu}
and the stress-energy tensor
T_{\mu\nu}
. As a consequence, their correlations can be expressed in terms of the four-point correlation functions of these operators.

We start in Section
5.1
with a brief review of the
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry Ward identities for the four-point correlation functions and for the undeformed flow correlations, i.e. for
\omega_{i}=0
. Then in Section
5.2
we give a summary of the new relationships between the Mellin kernels of the various flow correlations with the frequencies
\omega_{i}
turned on. The detailed derivation is presented in Appendix
H
.

5.1
Stress-energy tensor multiplet in
{\cal N}=4
SYM

In what follows, we assume that the source and sink are given by scalar operators
\phi(3)\equiv O_{\bf 20^{\prime}}(x_{3},Y)
and
\phi^{\dagger}(4)\equiv O_{\bf 20^{\prime}}(x_{4},\bar{Y})
(see (
99
)) and consider the following correlation functions
19
19

19

For definiteness we always assume that
J_{1}\geq J_{2}
.

\displaystyle G_{J_{1}J_{2}}=\langle{\phi^{\dagger}(4)O_{J_{1}}(1)O_{J_{2}}(2)\phi(3)}\rangle\,,

(181)

where
O_{J}(i)\equiv O_{J}(x_{i})
denotes an operator with spin
J
whose Lorentz indices are projected on the light cone,

\displaystyle O_{J=1}(i)=\bar{n}_{i}^{\mu}J_{\mu}^{R}(x_{i})\,,\qquad\qquad O_{J=2}(i)=\bar{n}_{i}^{\mu}\bar{n}_{i}^{\nu}T_{\mu\nu}(x_{i})\,,

(182)

and
O_{J=0}
was defined in (
100
).
In a generic CFT, the correlation functions (
181
) are very complicated and are not related to each other.

A unique feature of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM is that the operators (
182
) belong to the same stress-tensor supermultiplet. As a result, their correlation functions are related to each other by
\mathcal{N}=4
superconformal Ward identities. Solving them one can
express (
181
) in terms of the simplest four-point function involving scalar operators only
Belitsky:2014zha
;
Korchemsky:2015ssa
.
The correlation functions (
181
) have a rather complicated R-symmetry structure. It corresponds to the overlap of the tensor product of two real
SO(6)\sim SU(4)
representations
\mathbf{20^{\prime}}\times\mathbf{20^{\prime}}=\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{15}+\mathbf{20^{\prime}}+\mathbf{84}+\mathbf{105}+\mathbf{175}
for the scalar source (
\phi
) and sink (
\phi^{\dagger}
), and that for the detectors
O_{J}
, for example for two R-symmetry currents
\mathbf{15}\times\mathbf{15}=\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{15}_{s}+\mathbf{15}_{a}+\mathbf{20^{\prime}}+\mathbf{45}+\mathbf{\overline{45}}+\mathbf{84}
. We are not going to study this rich structure in full generality. Instead, we will concentrate on a single R-symmetry channel in each correlation function, namely the one corresponding to the top value of the
SU(4)
quadratic Casimir. In the example above this is the channel
\mathbf{84}
. The reason for this choice is the remarkably simple relation between the various flow correlations in this channel for vanishing
\omega_{i}
discovered in
Belitsky:2013bja
;
Belitsky:2014zha
(see (
192
) below).

We wish to find out to what extent these relations are preserved in the case of interest
\omega_{i}\neq 0
. So, we concentrate on the preferred channel and show only the space-time dependence of the correlation functions, dropping the isotopic variables
Y
and
S
of Section
3.4
. For instance, the all-scalar correlation function in the top Casimir channel
\mathbf{105}
(denoted by
G^{\mathbf{(105)}}
in (
137
)) is

\displaystyle G_{00}=(x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2})^{2}F(x)\,.

(183)

Here the dependence on the coupling resides in the function
F(x)
given by

\displaystyle F(x)={1\over(2\pi)^{4}}{\Phi(u,v)\over uv(x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2})^{4}}\,,\qquad u={x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\,,\qquad v={x_{23}^{2}x_{14}^{2}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\,,

(184)

where
\Phi(u,v)
is a function of the conformal cross-ratios
u,v
that admits the Mellin representation (
142
).
20
20

20

Note that in this section we do not consider the explicit rational (Born-level) terms in (
139
).

Following
Korchemsky:2015ssa
, in order to present explicit expressions for the higher-spin correlator components
G_{J_{1}J_{2}}
, it is convenient to introduce a pair of auxiliary commuting Weyl spinors,
\lambda_{i,\alpha}
and
\bar{\lambda}_{i,\dot{\alpha}}
, for each spinning operator in (
181
). They form the null vectors
\bar{n}_{i}
in (
182
),

\displaystyle\bar{n}_{i,\mu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{i,\alpha}\bar{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad i=1,2\,,

(185)

where
\sigma^{\mu}=(1,\vec{\sigma})
is the four-vector of Pauli matrices. The general form of the correlation function (
181
) is (up to normalization and only for the top Casimir channel), see Ref.
Korchemsky:2015ssa
,

\displaystyle G_{J_{1}J_{2}}={\cal D}_{1}^{J_{1}}{\cal D}_{2}^{J_{2}}\Big[{\cal M}_{J_{1},J_{2}}F(x)\Big]\ ,

(186)

where
{\cal M}_{J_{1},J_{2}}
is a homogenous polynomial in
\lambda_{1}^{\alpha}
and
\lambda_{2}^{\alpha}
of degree
2J_{1}
and
2J_{2}
, respectively.
{\cal D}_{i}
is a differential operator acting both on the coordinates of the operator
x_{i}
and the spinors
\lambda_{i}
,

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{i}=\bar{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}{\left({\sigma^{\mu}}\right)}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}{\partial\over\partial\lambda_{i}^{\alpha}}{\partial\over\partial x_{i}^{\mu}}\,.

(187)

The functions
{\cal M}_{J_{1},J_{2}}
are expressed in terms of three building blocks,

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{1[234]}=\lambda_{1}^{\alpha}(x_{12})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\tilde{x}_{23})^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}(x_{34})_{\beta\dot{\gamma}}(\tilde{x}_{41})^{\dot{\gamma}\delta}\lambda_{1,\delta}\equiv\langle{1|x_{12}\tilde{x}_{23}x_{34}\tilde{x}_{41}|1}\rangle\,,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{[12]i}=\lambda_{1}^{\alpha}(x_{1i})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\tilde{x}_{i2})^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\lambda_{2,\beta}\equiv\langle{1|x_{1i}\tilde{x}_{i2}|2}\rangle\,,\qquad(i=3,4)\,,

(188)

as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{0,0}=1\,,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{1,0}=x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}{\cal X}_{1[234]}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{2,0}=({\cal X}_{1[234]})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{1,1}=x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}{\cal X}_{[12]3}{\cal X}_{[12]4}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{2,1}={\cal X}_{1[234]}{\cal X}_{[12]3}{\cal X}_{[12]4}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{2,2}=({\cal X}_{[12]3}{\cal X}_{[12]4})^{2}\,.

(189)

As explained in Section
4
, we can use the correlation functions
G_{J_{1}J_{2}}
to obtain the various flow correlations. To this end, we have to apply the transformations specified in (
31
) to the operators at points 1 and 2. This amounts to sending both operators to the celestial sphere in the direction of the null vectors
n_{i}
, that is
x_{i}=r_{i}n_{i}+\alpha_{i}\bar{n}_{i}
with
r_{i}\to\infty
, and integrating along the light-rays
\bar{n}_{i}
with the exponential weight
\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{-i(n_{i}\bar{n}_{i})\hat{\omega}_{i}\alpha_{i}}
. As a result the operators at points 1 and 2 in the four-point function (
181
) are converted into flow operators and the correlator becomes a function of the two sets of detector variables
(n_{i},\hat{\omega}_{i})
and of the separation between the source and sink
x_{34}
. This is followed by a Fourier transform to momentum space,
x_{34}\to q
. In the simplest case of SSC , i.e.
J_{1}=J_{2}=0
, the result is the convolution of the Mellin amplitude
M(j_{1},j_{2})
and the detector kernel
K_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
(see Eq. (
147
)). It is important to remember that the dynamical information (i.e. the coupling dependence) resides in the Mellin amplitude while the kernel is universal, so it applies to both weak and strong coupling.

Because the various correlation functions (
186
) are determined by the same function
\Phi(u,v)
, we expect that the same Mellin representation (
147
) should hold for the two-point correlations of flow operators with arbitrary spin, schematically

\displaystyle\text{XYC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)=\int{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}\,K_{\text{XY}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\,M(j_{1},j_{2})\,\,.

(190)

Here
X
and
Y
stand for the scalar
(S)
, charge
(Q)
or energy
(E)
flow operators. The kernels
K_{\text{XY}}
are coupling-independent and have a fixed form for each pair of detectors. They are functions of the scaling variable
z
(the angle between the detectors) and the energies
\omega_{i}
transferred by the detectors.

The special form of the correlation functions (
186
) is dictated by
\mathcal{N}=4
superconformal symmetry. It is natural to ask whether analogous relations exist between the correlations (
190
). Indeed, in Ref.
Belitsky:2014zha
it was shown that for
\omega_{i}=0
the correlation of two flow operators of spins
J_{1},J_{2}
in the top
SU(4)
Casimir channel, before the final Fourier transform
x\to q
,
21
21

21

Without loss of generality we can set
x_{3}\equiv x
and
x_{4}=0
.
is a
total space-time derivative
of the fundamental scalar-scalar one (for
J_{1}\geq J_{2}
):

\displaystyle{(\Box_{x})^{J_{2}}(n_{2}\partial_{x})^{J_{1}-J_{2}}\over 2^{J_{1}-1}(n_{1}n_{2})^{J_{1}+1}}\left(\frac{{\cal G}(\gamma)}{x^{2}}\right)={2^{J_{2}+1}(xn_{2})^{J_{1}-J_{2}}\over(n_{1}n_{2})^{J_{1}+1}(x^{2})^{J_{1}+1}}{d^{J_{1}}\over d\gamma^{J_{1}}}\left[(1-\gamma)^{J_{1}}\gamma^{J_{2}}{\cal G}^{(J_{2})}(\gamma)\right]\,.

(191)

Here
\gamma={2(xn_{1})(xn_{2})/(x^{2}(n_{1}n_{2}))}
is the Lorentz-invariant and dimensionless angle variable. The function
{\cal G}(\gamma)
contains the coupling constant dependence. After the Fourier transform
x\to q
the left-hand side of (
191
) gives rise to a very simple relation between the corresponding kernels
K_{\text{XY}}
in (
190
). Stripping off the kinematic prefactor
\sim(q^{2})^{J_{2}}(qn_{2})^{J_{1}-J_{2}}/(n_{1}n_{2})^{J_{1}+1}
(i.e., the Fourier transform of the derivatives on the left-hand side of (
191
)), and replacing it with the factor
\sim{(q^{2})^{J_{1}+J_{2}}/((qn_{1})^{J_{1}+1}(qn_{2})^{J_{2}+1})}
from the definition (
61
), one finds

\displaystyle K_{\text{XY}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,0,0)=z^{-J_{X}}K_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,0,0)\,,

(192)

where
J_{X}\geq J_{Y}
.
In application to (
190
) this implies that for
\omega_{i}=0
all the flow correlations are given by the same universal function of
z
, up to an overall power of
z
.

5.2
Summary of the relationships between the kernels for
\omega_{i}\neq 0

Below we show that for
\omega_{i}\neq 0
the relation (
192
) is generalized in the form of differential operators expressing each
K_{\text{XY}}
in terms of the basic scalar one
K_{\text{SS}}
:

\displaystyle K_{\text{XY}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=z^{-J_{X}}\sum_{p,q=0}^{2}C_{\text{XY}}^{p,q}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\partial^{p}_{\omega_{1}}\partial^{q}_{\omega_{2}}\,K_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\,,

(193)

where
J_{X}\geq J_{Y}
and the coefficients
C_{\text{XY}}
are polynomials in
z
and
\omega_{i}
and rational functions of the Mellin parameters
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
. The order of the differential operator is at most 2 in each variable, depending on the choice of the detectors.

Here we list some of these non-vanishing coefficients:

(i) Charge-scalar correlation:

\displaystyle C_{\text{QS}}^{0,0}=1+\frac{j_{2}\omega_{1}z}{j_{1}+j_{2}}\,,\qquad C_{\text{QS}}^{1,0}=\frac{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)}{2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)}\,,\qquad C_{\text{QS}}^{0,1}=-\frac{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}{2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)}\,;

(194)

(ii) Charge-charge correlation:

\displaystyle C_{\text{QQ}}^{0,0}=1+\frac{\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)^{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+z\omega_{1}\omega_{2})+j_{1}j_{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})}{2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right){}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle C_{\text{QQ}}^{1,0}=\frac{\omega_{1}\left(2j_{1}\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)+\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)\right)}{4\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right){}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle C_{\text{QQ}}^{0,1}=\frac{\omega_{2}\left(2j_{2}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)+\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)\right)}{4\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right){}^{2}}\,;

(195)

(i) Energy-scalar correlation:

\displaystyle C_{\text{ES}}^{0,0}=1+\frac{z\omega_{1}\left[j_{2}\left((j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}+j_{1}-1\right)\omega_{1}z+\left((j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}-1\right)\left(j_{1}+2j_{2}\right)\right]}{\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+1\right)}\,,

\displaystyle C_{\text{ES}}^{1,0}=\frac{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)\left[\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)((j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}-1)+2j_{1}j_{2}\omega_{1}z\right]}{\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+1\right)}\,,

\displaystyle C_{\text{ES}}^{0,1}=\frac{\omega_{1}\left[j_{2}\omega_{1}z\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)+((j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}-1)(z-1)\right]}{\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+1\right)}\,,
