\displaystyle C_{\text{ES}}^{1,1}=\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\Big[\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+2\right)(1+z\omega_{1}+z\omega_{2}+z^{2}\omega_{1}\omega_{2})+(j_{1}+j_{2})(2z-1)+z+1\Big]}{3\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+1\right)}\,,

\displaystyle C_{\text{ES}}^{2,0}=\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\left(z\omega_{1}+1\right){}^{2}}{6\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+1\right)}\,,\qquad C_{\text{ES}}^{0,2}=\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\left(z\omega_{2}+1\right){}^{2}}{6\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+1\right)}\,.

(196)

We see that setting
\omega_{i}=0
reproduces the relations (
192
).
The differential operators (
193
) for EQC and EEC are presented in the ancillary file. The detailed derivation of the various differential operators from the
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry Ward identity (
186
) is explained in Appendix
H
.

Another interesting result of the above analysis is the observation that the basic kernel
K_{\text{SS}}
satisfies a simple homogeneous partial differential equation:

\displaystyle\Big[j_{1}j_{2}\left(j_{1}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)+j_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)-\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}\right)

\displaystyle+j_{1}\omega_{1}\left(\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)+\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)\right)\,\partial_{\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle+j_{2}\omega_{2}\left(\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)+\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)\right)\,\partial_{\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)\,\partial_{\omega_{1}}\partial_{\omega_{2}}\Big]K_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=0\,.

(197)

Its solutions are discussed in Appendix
H
.

We remark that the differential operators that relate the various kernels depend on the Mellin parameters
j_{1},j_{2}
, so they do not commute with the Mellin integral (
190
). Consequently, the event shapes (
190
) are not related to each other in a simple way, except for two special cases discussed in the next subsection.

5.3
Relationship between generalized event shapes in special cases

We might ask the question what happens if we switch off one of the frequencies? It turns out that the relations between the kernels described above simplify dramatically. The differential operators (
193
) are reduced to simple factors
independent of the Mellin parameters

j_{1},j_{2}
. Consequently, we can establish very simple relations between the Mellin integrals (
190
), i.e. between the flow correlations, and not only between the kernels.

Vanishing frequency
\omega_{1}=0

Let us start with the asymmetric correlations QSC, ESC and EQC. From (
194
) and (
196
) it is obvious that setting
\omega_{1}=0
trivializes the differential operators, reducing them to 1. The situation is similar for EQC. Denoting the ratio

\displaystyle r_{\text{XY}}(z,\omega_{2})={K_{\text{XY}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,0,\omega_{2})\over K_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,0,\omega_{2})}\,,

(198)

we find

\displaystyle r_{\text{QS}}=\frac{1}{z}\,,\qquad r_{\text{ES}}=\frac{1}{z^{2}}\,,\qquad r_{\text{EQ}}=\frac{1}{z^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}\right)\,.

(199)

At the same time, setting
\omega_{2}=0
in (
194
) leaves the derivative
\left.\partial_{\omega_{2}}K_{\text{SS}}\right|_{\omega_{2}=0}
in place, and we do not observe any substantial simplification of the differential operator. The same applies to the other two asymmetric correlations ESC and EQC.

The two symmetric correlations QQC and EEC also undergo drastic simplifications if we set
\omega_{1}=0
(or similarly
\omega_{2}=0
, due to the symmetry). Take for example QQC: putting
\omega_{1}=0
in (
5.2
) we get

\displaystyle C_{{QQ}}^{0,0}=1+\frac{\left(j_{1}^{2}+3j_{2}j_{1}+j_{2}^{2}\right){\omega_{2}}}{2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)^{2}}\,,\qquad C_{{QQ}}^{0,1}=\frac{j_{2}{\omega_{2}}({\omega_{2}}+1)}{2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)^{2}}\,,\qquad C_{{QQ}}^{1,0}=0\,.

(200)

This is not all, we still have to explore the consequences of the differential equation (
5.2
), which now reads simply

\displaystyle\big[(\omega_{2}+1)\partial_{\omega_{2}}+j_{1}\big]K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{1}=0,\omega_{2})=0\,.

(201)

This equation allows us to eliminate the term with
\partial_{\omega_{2}}
in (
193
),

\displaystyle C_{{QQ}}^{0,0}-\frac{j_{1}}{\omega_{2}+1}C_{{QQ}}^{0,1}=1+\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}\,,

(202)

so that the differential operator (
193
) collapses to (compare with the third relation in (
199
))

\displaystyle r_{\text{QQ}}=\frac{1}{z}\left(1+\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}\right)\,.

(203)

The analysis of the differential operator for EEC yields an equally simple relation,

\displaystyle r_{\text{EE}}=\frac{1}{z^{2}}\left(1+\omega_{2}+\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}}{6}\right)\,.

(204)

As mentioned
earlier, the remarkable feature of the relations (
199
), (
203
) and (
204
) is that they do not depend on the Mellin parameters
j_{i}
. Inserting them in (
190
) yields equivalent relations between the various event shapes,

\displaystyle{\text{XYC}(0,\omega_{2},z)\over\text{SSC}(0,\omega_{2},z)}=r_{\text{XY}}\,,\qquad J_{X}\geq J_{Y}\,.

(205)

Most importantly, these relations hold both perturbatively (at weak and at strong coupling) and nonperturbatively (at finite coupling).

Event shapes
{\rm XYC}_{+-}
at strong coupling

As explained in Section
4.1
, the computation of the event shape
{\rm SSC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}
is drastically simplified by using the integral representation (
4.1
) of the kernel
K^{+-}_{\rm SS}
. The expression in (
4.1
) is analytic in
j_{1},j_{2}
, while the Mellin amplitude
M^{\text{strong}}
from (
4
) has simple poles. Consequently, the Mellin integral over
j_{2}
with
j_{1}+j_{2}
fixed amounts to evaluating (
4.1
) at
j_{2}=0
. To this end we have to analyze the behavior of the factors
\Gamma(-s)\Gamma(j_{2}+s)/\Gamma(j_{2})
under the
s-
integral, when
s,j_{2}\to 0
. The pole in
\Gamma(j_{2}+s)
at
s=-j_{2}\to 0
lies outside the contour whereas the pole in
\Gamma(-s)
at
s=0
has the trivial residue
-1
. After setting
j_{2}=0
, the only remaining
\omega
dependence is in the factor
(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}
in front of the integral. The Mellin integral over
j_{1}
is easily done by residues resulting in the simple expression (
157
).

The same scenario applies when we act with the differential operators (
193
). We observe another drastic simplification, namely the Mellin integral over
j_{2}
turns the differential operators into simple factors
independent of
j_{1}
. They go through the
j_{1}-
integral and the resulting event shapes
{\rm XYC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}
are obtained by multiplying
{\rm SSC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}
by these factors.

Let us illustrate the mechanism on the simplest example of
{\rm QSC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}
. When the differential operator (
193
) with coefficients from (
194
) acts under the integral in (
4.1
), it produces terms
\sim s\to 0
. Similarly, its action on the factor
(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}
in front of the integral produces terms
\sim j_{2}\to 0
. So, we just need to evaluate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}}\left[\frac{1}{z}\sum_{q=0}^{2}C^{0,q}_{\rm QS}(j_{1},0|\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\partial^{q}_{\omega_{2}}\,(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}}\left[\frac{1}{z}\left(1-\frac{1}{2j_{1}}\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}z+1)\partial_{\omega_{2}}\right)\,(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}\right]=\frac{\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+2}{2z\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)}\,.

(206)

As explained above, this result is independent of
j_{1}
, so it immediately gives the ratio of the event shapes
{\rm QSC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}/{\rm SSC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}
listed in (
228
) and (
226
), respectively. The calculation with all the other differential operators (
193
) is equally simple and we obtain all the event shapes
{\rm XYC}_{+-}^{\rm strong}
from Section
6.2
. The non-trivial feature of the operators (
193
) is that their action on
(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}
gives a result independent of
j_{1}
.

Note that the situation is different if we employ the Mellin amplitude
M^{\text{weak}}
from (
4
). In order to evaluate the residue at the double pole
j_{2}=0
, we need to consider the complete sequence of poles of the integrand in (
4.1
) at
s=0,1,2,\ldots
. This results in an infinite sum which, together with that originating from the poles at
j_{1}=-1,-2,-3,\ldots
gives rise to the logs in (
156
). Further, the action of the differential operators (
193
) on the kernel (
4.1
) modifies the residue at the double pole
j_{2}=0
, so the result is not as simple as in (
5.3
).

6
Summary of the generalized event shapes in
{\cal N}=4
SYM

In this section, we present the explicit expressions for the two-point correlations (
50
) in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM at weak and strong coupling.
22
22

22

For the reader’s convenience, we presented the expressions for all two-point correlations in a Mathematica readable format in the ancillary file included with an arXiv submission.

Following (
2.6
) we distinguish six different functions
\rm XYC
with
X,Y=\{S,Q,E\}
.

6.1
Weak coupling

At weak coupling, the correlations take the form

\displaystyle{\rm XYC}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)={\rm XYC}^{(0)}+{a\over 4}\,{\rm XYC}^{(1)}+O(a^{2})\,,

(207)

where
a=g^{2}N/(4\pi^{2})
.
In the Born approximation, the correlations are given by (
107
) and (
3.4
). The functions
{\rm XYC}^{(0)}
vanish for
0<z<1

and
\omega_{i}>-1
.
To one-loop order, depending on the signs of
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
we find the following expressions:

Scalar-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\left(\frac{\omega_{2}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{1-z}+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{2(1-z)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

(208)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{1-z}\right)}{1-z}+\frac{(\omega_{1}+1)z\log{\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over\omega_{1}+1}\right)}}{2(1-z)(\omega_{1}z+1)}+\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)z\log{\left({\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+1\over\omega_{2}+1}\right)}}{2(1-z)(\omega_{2}z+1)}

(209)

Charge-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{QSC}^{(1)}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{\omega_{1}z}{2}+1\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{2}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{(1-z)z}+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+2\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{4(1-z)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

(210)

\displaystyle\text{QSC}^{(1)}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{\omega_{1}z}{2}+1\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{(1-z)z}+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{4(1-z)}

(211)

\displaystyle\text{QSC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(1+\frac{\omega_{1}z}{2}\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{1-z}\right)}{(1-z)z}+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{1}+1}\right)}{4(1-z)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}(1+\omega_{2}z)+2(\omega_{2}+1)\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{2}+1}\right)}{4(1-z)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

(212)

Charge-charge correlation

\displaystyle\text{QQC}^{(1)}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+2(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+2))\log\left(\frac{\omega_{2}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{4(1-z)z}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{2}+2)(\omega_{2}(\omega_{1}z+2)+\omega_{1}+2)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+1}{(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}\right)}{8(1-z)(\omega_{2}z+1)}

(213)

\displaystyle\text{QQC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(2\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+4\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{1-z}\right)}{4(1-z)z}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)\left(\omega_{2}(1+\omega_{1}z)+2(\omega_{1}+1)\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{1}+1}\right)}{8(1-z)\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)\left(\omega_{1}(1+\omega_{2}z)+2(\omega_{2}+1)\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{2}+1}\right)}{8(1-z)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

(214)

Energy-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{ESC}^{(1)}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}z^{2}+6\omega_{1}z+6\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{2}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{6(1-z)z^{2}}-\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\log\left(\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z}{\omega_{2}z+1}\right)}{6(\omega_{2}+1)z}

(215)

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(6\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}+6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)+\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{2}\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{12\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)(1-z)z\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle\text{ESC}^{(1)}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}z^{2}+6\omega_{1}z+6\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{6(1-z)z^{2}}-\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\log\left(\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)z}{\omega_{1}z+1}\right)}{6\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)z}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{12\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)(1-z)z}

(216)

\displaystyle\text{ESC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}z^{2}+6\omega_{1}z+6\right)\log{\left({1\over 1-z}\right)}}{6(1-z)z^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{1}+1}\right)}{12\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)(1-z)z}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(6\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}+6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)+\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{2}+1}\right)}{12\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)(1-z)z\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle-\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}\omega_{2}z}{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{12\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)z}+\frac{\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}z}{\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{12\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z}\right)

(217)

Energy-charge correlation

\displaystyle\text{EQC}^{(1)}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(6\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)+\omega_{1}\left(z\left(2\omega_{1}+6\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+6\right)+6\right)\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{2}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{12(1-z)z^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(6+6{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)\over\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)}+{\omega_{1}^{2}\left(1+\omega_{2}z\right)^{2}\over\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{24(1-z)z\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}\log\left(\frac{z\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)}{\omega_{2}z+1}\right)}{12z(\omega_{2}+1)}

(218)

\displaystyle\text{EQC}^{(1)}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(6\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)+\omega_{1}\left(z\left(2\omega_{1}+6\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+6\right)+6\right)\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}z+1}{1-z}\right)}{12(1-z)z^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+2\right)+2\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}}\right)}{24\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)(1-z)z}

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}\log\left(\frac{z\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)}{\omega_{1}z+1}\right)}{12z\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)}

(219)

\displaystyle\text{EQC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(6\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)+\omega_{1}\left(z\left(2\omega_{1}+6\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+6\right)+6\right)\right)\log{\left({1\over 1-z}\right)}}{12(1-z)z^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+2\right)+2\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{1}+1}\right)}{24\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)(1-z)z}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)\left(6\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}+6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)+\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{2}+1}\right)}{24\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)(1-z)z\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle-\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}\omega_{2}z}{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{24\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)z}-\frac{\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}z}{\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{24\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z}\right)

(220)

Energy-energy correlation

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{(1)}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}(\omega_{2}z(\omega_{2}z+6)+6)+6\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}((\omega_{2}+3)z+3)+6)+6(\omega_{2}(\omega_{2}+6)+6)\right)\log{\left({\omega_{2}z+1\over 1-z}\right)}}{36(1-z)z^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)(\omega_{2}^{2}+6\omega_{2}+6)\left({\omega_{1}^{2}(\omega_{2}z+1)^{2}\over(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}+{6\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}z+1)\over(\omega_{2}+1)}+6\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+1}{(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}\right)}{72(1-z)z(\omega_{2}z+1)}

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}}{36(\omega_{2}+1)z}\log{\left({z(\omega_{2}+1)\over\omega_{2}z+1}\right)}

(221)

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(6\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)+\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}^{2}z^{2}+6\omega_{1}z+6\right)+6\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}^{2}z+3\omega_{1}(z+1)+6\right)\right)\log{\left({1\over 1-z}\right)}}{36(1-z)z^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)}{\omega_{1}+1}+6\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{1}+1}\right)}{72(1-z)z\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{2}^{2}+6\omega_{2}+6\right)\left(\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}{\omega_{2}+1}+6\right)\log\left(\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1}{\omega_{2}+1}\right)}{72(1-z)z\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}

\displaystyle-\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}\omega_{2}z}{\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{72\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)z}+\frac{\log\left(\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}\omega_{1}z}{\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{72\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)z}\right)

\displaystyle-\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\frac{\log\left(\frac{z\left(\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+\frac{1}{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\sqrt{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)^{2}-4\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z}\right)\right)^{2}}{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+1\right)}\right)}{12z\sqrt{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)^{2}-4\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z}}

(222)

The following comments are in order.

The above relations are valid for
0<z<1
and
\omega_{i}>-1
.
We verify that for
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=0
they simplify to

\displaystyle\text{XYC}^{(1)}(0,0,z)={1\over(1-z)z^{J_{X}}}\log{\left({1\over 1-z}\right)}\,,

(223)

where
J_{X}\geq J_{Y}
. This relation is in agreement with the findings of Ref.
Belitsky:2013bja
.

For
\omega_{1}=0
and nonzero
\omega_{2}
it is possible to show that the above expressions verify the relation (
205
).
For nonzero
\omega_{i}
, the first term in the above expressions for the correlations describes the elastic contribution discussed in Section
3.4
.
For the correlations SSC, QSC and QQC, the remaining terms describe the annihilation and production of particles by the detectors, depending on the sign of
\omega_{i}
(see Section
3.4
). In addition to such terms, the correlations ESC, EQC and EEC also contain terms that scale at small
z
as
(\log z)/z
. They are accompanied by powers of
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
and take the form

\displaystyle\text{EXC}^{(1)}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle z\to 0}}{{\sim}}\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{J_{X}}\times{\log z\over z}\,,

(224)

where
J_{X}
is the spin of the detector
X=\{S,Q,E\}
. These additional terms come from the cross-talk between the detectors.
For the energy-energy correlation (
6
), such terms are accompanied by a square-root
\sqrt{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)^{2}-4\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z}
. Its origin and physical interpretation are discussed in Appendix
I
.

We did not display the expressions for
\text{XYC}_{--}
and
\text{XXC}_{-+}
. They can be obtained by applying the crossing relation (
2.6
) to
\text{XYC}_{++}
and
\text{XXC}_{+-}
, respectively. Doing so, we examined the relation

\displaystyle\text{XYC}_{--}+\text{XYC}_{++}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\text{XXC}_{+-}+\text{XXC}_{-+}\,,

(225)

which was previously observed to hold for the scalar-scalar correlation (
136
).
We found that it is satisfied for the correlations SSC, QSC and QQC, which do not receive contributions from the cross-talk between the detectors. At the same time, it is violated for the correlations involving the energy detector.
As explained in Section
4.4
,
this can be understood using the general formula (
170
) and the properties of the corresponding kernels.

6.2
Strong coupling

At strong coupling, in the leading supergravity approximation, we find the correlations:

Scalar-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{+-}=

\displaystyle\frac{z^{2}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}{2\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

(226)

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}=

\displaystyle{z^{2}\over 2}\bigg[\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{3}}+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{6\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{5}}-\frac{3\omega_{1}\left(2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\omega_{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+2\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{3}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}\bigg]

(227)

Charge-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{QSC}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{z\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+2\right)}{4\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

(228)

\displaystyle\text{QSC}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\frac{z\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)}{4\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{2}}

(229)

\displaystyle\text{QSC}_{++}

\displaystyle={z\over 4}\bigg[\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{2}}+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+2\right)}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{6\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{4}}-\frac{2\left(3\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+3\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\omega_{1}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+3\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{3}+2\omega_{2}^{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{2}}\bigg]

(230)

Charge-charge correlation

\displaystyle\text{QQC}_{+-}=

\displaystyle\frac{z(\omega_{2}+1)(\omega_{2}+2)(\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z+\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+2)}{8(\omega_{2}z+1)^{3}}

(231)

\displaystyle\text{QQC}_{++}=

\displaystyle{z\over 16}\bigg[\frac{2\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)\left(2\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+2\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z+2\right)}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{2\left(\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)\left(\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}-2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+2\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{4\left(\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}^{3}+7\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+7\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+7\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}^{2}+7\omega_{2}^{3}\omega_{1}+2\omega_{1}^{3}+2\omega_{2}^{3}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{24\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+3\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+3\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{24\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+2(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{5}}\bigg]

(232)

Energy-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\text{ESC}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}{\omega_{2}+1}+6\right)}{12\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

(233)

\displaystyle\text{ESC}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)}{12(\omega_{1}z+1)}

(234)

\displaystyle\text{ESC}_{++}

\displaystyle={1\over 12}\bigg[\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)}{(\omega_{1}z+1)}+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}\left(\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)}{\omega_{2}+1}+6\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}}{z\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)}-\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{3}+(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6)\omega_{2}\right)}{(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z)}

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(6\omega_{1}^{2}+(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6)\omega_{2}\right)\omega_{1}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{2}}-\frac{2\left(\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+3(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\right)\omega_{1}^{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}\bigg]

(235)

Energy-charge correlation

\displaystyle\text{EQC}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}\left({\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}\over(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}+{6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)\over\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)}+6\right)}{24\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}

(236)

\displaystyle\text{EQC}_{-+}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(2+2\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z\right)}{24\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{2}}

(237)

\displaystyle\text{EQC}_{++}

\displaystyle={1\over 24}\bigg[\frac{4\left(\omega_{2}+2\right)\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{3}}\left({\omega_{1}^{2}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)^{2}\over(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}+{6\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{2}z+1\right)\over\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)}+6\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(2+2\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}z+1\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(2+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})}{z(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{3}}-\frac{12\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}\left(2\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+3(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\right)}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{3}-\left(\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{2}+24\right)+54\right)+12\right)\omega_{1}^{2}-54\omega_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}-24\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\omega_{1}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{20\omega_{2}\omega_{1}^{3}+12\omega_{1}^{3}+2\left(\omega_{1}+2\right)\left(2\omega_{1}\left(\omega_{1}+3\right)+3\right)\omega_{2}^{2}}{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(\omega_{2}+2)\omega_{1}^{3}+\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\omega_{2}^{2}}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}\omega_{1}z}\bigg]

(238)

Energy-energy correlation

\displaystyle\text{EEC}_{+-}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}\left(\omega_{2}^{2}+6\omega_{2}+6\right)\left(\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}(\omega_{2}z+1)^{2}}{(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}z+1)}{\omega_{2}+1}+6\right)}{72(\omega_{2}z+1)^{3}}

(239)

\displaystyle\text{EEC}_{++}

\displaystyle={1\over 72}\bigg[\frac{(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}{(\omega_{2}z+1)^{3}}\left(\omega_{2}^{2}+6\omega_{2}+6\right)\left(\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}(\omega_{2}z+1)^{2}}{(\omega_{2}+1)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}z+1)}{\omega_{2}+1}+6\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\omega_{1}+1)^{2}}{(\omega_{1}z+1)^{3}}\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6\right)\left(\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}(\omega_{1}z+1)^{2}}{(\omega_{1}+1)^{2}}+\frac{6\omega_{2}(\omega_{1}z+1)}{\omega_{1}+1}+6\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}(\omega_{1}^{2}-4\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+\omega_{2}^{2})}{z(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{5}}((\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})^{2}+6(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})+6)

\displaystyle-{\omega_{1}^{3}(\omega_{2}^{2}+6\omega_{2}+6)+\omega_{2}^{3}(\omega_{1}^{2}+6\omega_{1}+6)\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)}

\displaystyle-{2(4\omega_{1}^{3}\omega_{2}^{3}+24\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)+39\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)+18\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)\left(\omega_{1}^{2}-\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right))\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{2}}

\displaystyle-{116\omega_{1}^{3}\omega_{2}^{3}+\omega_{1}^{3}\omega_{2}^{3}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)+264\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)+180\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{3}}

\displaystyle-{36\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)\left(\omega_{1}^{2}-\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{3}}-{\omega_{1}^{3}\omega_{2}^{3}(312+6\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right))\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{4}}

\displaystyle-{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\omega_{1}^{2}-4\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)+432\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)+108\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right))\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{4}}

\displaystyle-{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}(6\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\left(6+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)\left(\omega_{1}^{2}-4\omega_{2}\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}\right)+216(\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}))\over(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{5}}\bigg]

(240)

Let us summarize the properties of the above results.

We verify that for
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=0
they are in agreement with the findings of Ref.
Belitsky:2013bja
;
Belitsky:2014zha
,

\displaystyle\text{XYC}(0,0,z)=\frac{1}{2}z^{2-J_{X}}\,,

(241)

where
J_{X}\geq J_{Y}
. For
\omega_{1}=0
and
\omega_{2}\neq 0
they satisfy the relation (
205
).

We observe that the expressions for the correlations
\text{XYC}_{++}
are more complicated compared to
\text{XYC}_{+-}
and
\text{XYC}_{-+}
. They contain additional terms involving inverse powers of

\tilde{q}^{2}/q^{2}-1=\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z
. As we explained in Section
4.5
, such terms are generated by a bulk point singularity.

As at weak coupling, the remaining correlations
\text{XYC}_{--}
can be found using the crossing relation (
2.6
). They have a form similar to
\text{XYC}_{++}
. Then, examining the sum

\text{XYC}_{--}+\text{XYC}_{++}
we observe that all the terms with the bulk singularity induced scale
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z
cancel against each other and the resulting expression exactly matches the sum
\text{XYC}_{-+}+\text{XYC}_{+-}
. We therefore conclude that the relation
(
225
) holds at strong coupling. We recall that at weak coupling this relation is broken for the correlations that receive a contribution from the detector cross-talk. In terms of the OPE, the cross-talk is associated with the contribution of the leading, twist-two operators in the product of the detector operators. At weak coupling, these operators develop anomalous dimensions that manifest themselves through
\log z
terms in the small
z
limit. At strong coupling, these operators become infinitely heavy and do not contribute. This explains why the cross-talk contribution is not present at strong coupling.

7
Quantum gravity corrections to event shapes

In this section we analyze
{1/c_{T}}\sim 1/N_{c}^{2}
corrections to the generalized energy-energy correlations at strong coupling. Such corrections come from gravitational loops in the bulk theory. Here we only consider the case
\omega_{1}=0
which simplifies the analysis dramatically. We use the Mellin amplitude recently computed in
Alday:2018kkw
to study the structure of the generalized event shapes at one loop in supergravity. As expected on general grounds
Kologlu:2019bco
, we find that the result is divergent when
\omega_{i}\to 0
. To understand the nature of this divergence we derive a dispersive representation of the generalized event shapes using CFT dispersion relations in Mellin space
Penedones:2019tng
. We then discuss the expected structure of the undeformed (
\omega_{i}=0
) event shapes at finite
\lambda
and
c_{T}
.

7.1
One-loop supergravity

Conventional event shapes, like the energy-energy correlation, cannot be computed perturbatively in
{1/c_{T}}
at strong coupling. The reason is that the correlation function becomes more and more singular in the Regge limit at higher loops which renders the event shape of interest divergent.
This divergence is an artifact of perturbation theory and, based on general grounds, it is absent at finite
c_{T}
, see
Kologlu:2019bco
.
Since the correlation functions at finite
c_{T}
are not accessible, we can alternatively deform the observable in a way that makes it finite order by order in
{1/c_{T}}
. We can then try to understand what happens as we take the deformation parameter to zero. Introducing non-zero
\omega_{i}
is one natural way to do it.

The divergence of the undeformed event shape manifests itself in the appearance of divergences in the
\omega_{i}\to 0
limit. In other words, the
c_{T}\to\infty
and
\omega_{i}\to 0
limits do not commute. Here we compute the
\omega
-deformed event shapes using the one-loop Mellin amplitude in supergravity. The relevant one-loop supergravity correlation function was recently found in
Alday:2017xua
;
Aprile:2017bgs
;
Alday:2017vkk
.

The starting point of our analysis is the one-loop Mellin amplitude
Alday:2018kkw

\displaystyle M^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle={1\over 4c_{T}}(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(j_{1}+j_{2}+1)^{2}\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}c_{m,n}\Big[{1\over(j_{1}-m-1)(j_{2}-n-1)}

\displaystyle-{1\over(j_{1}-m-1)(j_{1}+j_{2}+2+n)}-{1\over(j_{2}-m-1)(j_{1}+j_{2}+2+n)}\Big]\,.

(242)

The expression inside the brackets is fully crossing symmetric. The expansion coefficients are given by

\displaystyle c_{m,n}

\displaystyle=c_{n,m}={1\over 5(m+n-1)_{5}}\Big(15m^{4}n^{2}+25m^{4}n+12m^{4}+30m^{3}n^{3}+120m^{3}n^{2}+114m^{3}n

\displaystyle+36m^{3}+15m^{2}n^{4}+120m^{2}n^{3}+216m^{2}n^{2}+77m^{2}n-8m^{2}+25mn^{4}+114mn^{3}

\displaystyle+77mn^{2}-76mn-40m+12n^{4}+36n^{3}-8n^{2}-40n\Big)\,.

(243)

The double sum in (
7.1
) is divergent. This is a reflection of the UV divergence in the bulk theory at one loop which needs to be renormalized. The divergence does not depend on
j_{i}
and leads to an ambiguity in the renormalized one-loop supergravity amplitude which we denote by
c_{\infty}
.

We introduce a crossing-symmetric regulator in the sum,
\sum_{m,n}c_{m,n}\to\sum_{m,n}c_{m,n}{x^{m}+x^{n}\over 2}
. To recover the original sum we take
x\to 1
. The divergent part of the sum is given by
\propto{1/(1-x)}

and is indeed
j_{i}
independent. We then arrive at

\displaystyle M^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle=(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(j_{1}+j_{2}+1)^{2}\left(c_{\infty}+\hat{M}^{\text{reg}}(j_{1},j_{2})\right),

(244)

where
\hat{M}^{\text{reg}}(j_{1},j_{2})
can be easily computed explicitly using the regulator above and dropping the divergent
{1/(1-x)}
part. It takes a relatively simple form of a sum of various polygamma functions with rational coefficients, and we do not present it here to save space.
23
23

23

It can be found in the ancillary file that we provided together with the submission. In fact, the explicit expression for the one-loop Mellin amplitude was recently found in
Aprile:2020luw
. We checked that our results agree. We thank Francesco Aprile for pointing this out to us.
After this step, the Mellin integral representation for the the generalized event shapes becomes well defined and it can be studied both analytically and numerically.
The result takes the following form

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)=c_{\infty}\,\text{SSC}_{++}^{p=2}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)+\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{reg}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\,,

(245)

where
\text{SSC}_{++}^{p=2}
is given in (
179
) and
c_{\infty}
is fixed in the nonperturbative theory. It is known explicitly using the power of supersymmetric locailzation
Chester:2019pvm

\displaystyle c_{\infty}={1\over c_{T}}\Big({5\sqrt{\lambda}\over 16}+18\zeta(3)-{3\pi^{2}\over 2}-{2141\over 192}+O(\lambda^{-{3\over 2}})\Big)\,.

(246)

Instead of keeping
\omega_{i}
general, here we focus our attention on understanding the small
\omega_{i}
limit.
Setting
\omega_{1}=0
does not spoil the finiteness of the result but dramatically simplifies the kernel, see (
151
).
According to (
4.5
),
\text{SSC}_{++}^{p=2}(0,\omega_{2},z)\sim\delta(\omega_{2})
and, therefore, the
\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(0,\omega_{2},z)
does not depend on the coefficient
c_{\infty}
for
\omega_{2}\neq 0
. In fact, any four-point contact interaction in AdS does not contribute to the generalized event shape for
\omega_{1}=0
and
\omega_{2}\neq 0
.
24
24

24

Microscopically this is related to the extra suppression of the double trace operators familiar from studies of undeformed event shapes.

The result for the generalized energy-energy correlation at one loop in supergravity takes the form

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(0,\omega_{2},z)={5\over 4c_{T}}\bigg[{1-6z+6z^{2}\over\omega_{2}^{2}}+{3\over 2}{2-15z+24z^{2}-10z^{3}\over\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle+{12\over 25}(1-36z+216z^{2}-400z^{3}+225z^{4})\log|\omega_{2}|\log(z^{2}|\omega_{2}|)

\displaystyle+\Big({716-20496z+111396z^{2}\over 125}-1560z^{3}+846z^{4}\Big)\log|\omega_{2}|+O(\omega_{2}^{0})\bigg]\ ,

(247)

where we only presented the terms that are singular in the
\omega_{2}\to 0
limit and did not include the contact term
\delta(\omega_{2})
.
25
25

25

The leading contact term contribution can be read off from (
4.5
) and (
246
) and it is given by
{5\sqrt{\lambda}\over 8c_{T}}\delta(\omega_{2})(1-6z+6z^{2})
.

One can also check that the expression above correctly implements the transformation property (
2.6
) which relates the cases
\omega_{2}>0
and
\omega_{2}<0
.

As an immediate consistency check, we verified that the stress-tensor Ward identities (
63
) continue to hold when we include the one-loop correction, because (
7.1
) satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dz\,\text{EEC}^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(z,0,\omega_{2})=\int_{0}^{1}dz\,z\,\text{EEC}^{\text{1-loop sugra}}(z,0,\omega_{2})=0\,.

(248)

7.2
Dispersive representation of the energy-energy correlator

To elucidate the origin of the
{1/\omega_{2}}
divergences in (
7.1
) and to understand what happens to them at finite coupling, it is useful to consider the dispersive representation for the Mellin amplitude
Penedones:2019tng
.
26
26

26

It would be very interesting to generalize this analysis to the coordinate space as well using the coordinate space CFT dispersion relations
Carmi:2019cub
;
Mazac:2019shk
;
Caron-Huot:2020adz
.

To make as many symmetries as possible manifest, we find it convenient to write down dispersion relations for the
Mellin amplitude
\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})\equiv{M(j_{1},j_{2})/((j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(j_{1}+j_{2}+1)^{2})}
introduced in (
144
).
The advantage of using
\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})
is that it is fully crossing-symmetric function of
j_{1}
and
j_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})=\tilde{M}(j_{2},j_{1})=\tilde{M}(j_{1},-1-j_{1}-j_{2})\ \,.

(249)

Consistency with the OPE implies that
M(j_{1},j_{2})
has simple poles at the positions dictated by the quantum numbers of the exchanged operators
Mack:2009mi
;
Mack:2009gy
.
Let us start by recalling the structure of these poles in variable
j
defined as

\displaystyle j=j_{1}+j_{2}\,.

(250)

Poles in
j
correspond to the
\text{detector}\times\text{detector}
OPE channel which due to our choice of detectors is the same as the
{\bf 105}
R-symmetry channel. The residues at the poles are fixed in terms of the three-point functions of the exchanged operator, see e.g.
Costa:2012cb
for the detailed derivation,

\displaystyle M(j-j_{2},j_{2})=-{1\over 2}{C_{\tau,J}^{2}{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(2(1-j_{2}))\over j+{\tau\over 2}+m-2}+O\left(j+{\tau\over 2}+m-2\right).

(251)

Here
\tau
and
J\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are the twist and spin of the exchanged operator correspondingly. At finite
c_{T}
and
\lambda
these include all operators present in the theory. Below, when analyzing the one-loop supergravity, only double trace operators will contribute.
Descendants are labeled by integer
m\geq 0
, and
{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(2(1-j_{2}))
are the so-called Mack polynomials known explicitly, see e.g.
Gopakumar:2021dvg
for an explicit formula.
C_{\tau,J}^{2}
is the square of the three-point function of the external operators and the operator that appears in the OPE.
Only even spin operators appear in the formula above due to the crossing symmetry
M(j_{1},j_{2})=M(j_{2},j_{1})
.

Let us now translate the relation (
251
) to the statement about the singularities of the crossing-symmetric modified Mellin amplitude
\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})
.
The relation (
251
) implies that it has simple poles in
j=j_{1}+j_{2}
with the following residues

\displaystyle\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})=-{1\over 2}{C_{\tau,J}^{2}{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(2(1-j_{2}))\over j+{\tau\over 2}+m-2}{1\over({\tau\over 2}+m-2)^{2}({\tau\over 2}+m-3)^{2}}+O\left(j+{\tau\over 2}+m-2\right),

(252)

where
J\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Of course, in addition it could have singularities at
j=0
and
j=-1
since
\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})\equiv{M(j-j_{2},j_{2})/(j^{2}(j+1)^{2})}
.

Since
\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})
is fully crossing-symmetric, see (
249
), by replacing
j_{2}\to-(1+j)
and
j\to-(1+j_{2})

in (
252
) we find that
\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})
has additional poles in
j_{2}
. They correspond to the OPE channel detector
\times
source.
The residues at these poles can be found by applying the crossing to (
252
)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})={1\over 2}{C_{\tau,J}^{2}{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(-2(2+j))\over j_{2}-{\tau\over 2}-m+3}{1\over({\tau\over 2}+m-2)^{2}({\tau\over 2}+m-3)^{2}}+O(j_{2}-{\tau\over 2}-m+3)\,.

(253)

The additional singularities at
j=-1,0
are mapped to
j_{2}=0,-1
under the crossing.

We now write down the dispersive representation for
\hat{M}(j_{1},j_{2})
for fixed
j=j_{1}+j_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})

\displaystyle=\oint{dj_{2}^{\prime}\over 2\pi i}{\tilde{M}(j-j_{2}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime})\over j_{2}^{\prime}-j_{2}}=-\sum_{\alpha=\{0,-1,j,j-1\}}{\rm Res}_{j_{2}^{\prime}=\alpha}{\tilde{M}(j-j_{2}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime})\over j_{2}^{\prime}-j_{2}}

\displaystyle-{1\over 2}\sum_{\tau,J-\text{even},m}{C_{\tau,J}^{2}{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(-2(2+j))\over({\tau\over 2}+m-2)^{2}({\tau\over 2}+m-3)^{2}}\Big({1\over{\tau\over 2}+m-3-j_{2}}+{1\over{\tau\over 2}+m-3-(j-j_{2})}\Big)\,.

(254)

In deriving this relation we used the non-perturbative bound on the Mellin amplitude
|M(j_{1},j_{2})|\leq|j_{2}|
as
|j_{2}|\to\infty
, see
Penedones:2019tng
, which translates to
|\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})|\leq|j_{2}|^{-3}
as
|j_{2}|\to\infty
. Using the crossing symmetry this also implies that
|\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})|\leq|j_{2}|^{-3}
for fixed
j
, therefore the arc at infinity does not contribute to (
7.2
).
The sum on the first line of (
7.2
) comes from the contribution of protected twist-two operators and it is fixed by the OPE.
27
27

27

Here we assume that the coupling is finite and there are no higher spin conserved currents in the spectrum.

As a result we get the following representation for the nonperturbative Mellin amplitude

\displaystyle M(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle=-{1\over 2}{(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(1+j_{1}+j_{2})\over j_{1}j_{2}}-{(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}(j_{1}+j_{2}+1)^{2}\over 2}\

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\tau,J-\text{even},m}{C_{\tau,J}^{2}{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(-2(2+j_{1}+j_{2}))\over({\tau\over 2}+m-2)^{2}({\tau\over 2}+m-3)^{2}}\Big({1\over{\tau\over 2}+m-3-j_{2}}+{1\over{\tau\over 2}+m-3-j_{1}}\Big)\,.

(255)

Let us emphasize that this formula is exact. The first term comes from the protected twist two operators and it coincides with the leading result at strong coupling (
4
). The infinite sum encodes corrections to this result.

For the one loop supergravity Mellin amplitude, the only operators that appear in the sum in (
7.2
) are the double trace operators which have twist
\tau_{n,J}=8+2n+\gamma(n,J)
with
\gamma(n,J)\sim{1/c_{T}}
.
28
28

28

The shift of the twist
\tau
by
(+4)
is a consequence of superconformal symmetry, see e.g. Eqs. (2.5) and (2.6) in
Alday:2017vkk
. In the language of
Aprile:2017bgs
, we have
\tau=4+2t
, where
t\geq 2
.
The
Mack polynomials
{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}
that enter (
7.2
) are importantly proportional to

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}\sim\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau\over 2}\right)}\,.

(256)

For the double trace operators this becomes

\displaystyle\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau_{n,J}\over 2}\right)}=\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\gamma_{n,J}\over 2}\right)}\sim{1\over c_{T}^{2}}

(257)

Double trace operators have three-point functions that scale as
C_{\tau,J}^{2}\sim c_{T}
which can be seen from form of the disconnected part of the correlator in (
139
). Combining this fact with (
257
) we correctly recover the
{1/c_{T}}
scaling of the leading correction to (
7.2
) in supergravity.

Next we can use the dispersive representation (
7.2
) to compute the
\omega
-deformed event shapes at finite coupling in terms of the OPE data. For simplicity we consider
\omega_{1}=0
and
\omega_{2}>0
. Using relations (
204
), (
205
), and (
151
), we get the following representation for the event shape

\displaystyle\text{EEC}_{+}(0,\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\omega_{2}+\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}}{6}\right)\Big[{1\over 2}+\sum_{\tau,J-\text{even}}2\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau\over 2}\right)}C_{\tau,J}^{2}(1+\omega_{2})^{-({\tau\over 2}-1)}\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})\Big],

(258)

where we have defined a function

\displaystyle\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv-{1\over 2}\int{dj\over 2\pi i}{\pi\over 2\sin\pi j}z^{-j-2}\left(1-z\right)^{j-1}j^{2}(1+j)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(1+\omega_{2})^{-m}{{\cal Q}_{J,m}^{\tau}(-2(j+2))\over 2\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau\over 2}\right)}}{1\over({\tau\over 2}+m-2)^{2}({\tau\over 2}+m-3)^{2}}\,.

(259)

It encodes the contribution to the energy-energy correlation of a set of poles in (
7.2
) associated to a given primary operator.
29
29

29

For correlation functions these are known as Polyakov blocks, see e.g.
Carmi:2019cub
;
Mazac:2019shk
;
Caron-Huot:2020adz
for details. Therefore
\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})
encodes the contribution of a given Polyakov block to the energy-energy correlation.
In writing the formula above we used the fact that for
\omega_{2}>0
we can close the
j_{1}
contour to the right half-plane since this is the direction in which
(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}
decays.

It is possible to compute
\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})
explicitly for the first few values of
J
. For
J=0
we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J=0}(z,0,\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=-(1-6z+6z^{2})\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\tau-6}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma(\tau)}{\pi^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)^{4}}\ _{2}F_{1}\left({\tau-6\over 2},{\tau-6\over 2},\tau-1,{1\over 1+\omega_{2}}\right)\,.

(260)

For
J>0
we similarly get polynomials in
z
,
\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})=\sum_{i=0}^{J+2}c_{i}(\tau,\omega_{2})z^{i}
. Curiously, we also observed the relation

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})\sim(1-6z+6z^{2})\Big(1+O(\tau^{-1})\Big),

(261)

which will be useful for our discussion below. Note that the dependence of (
261
) on
z
matches the leading
{1/\omega_{2}^{2}}
term in (
7.1
).

It is now easy to understand how the terms singular for
\omega_{2}\to 0
are generated in (
7.1
). The underlying mechanism has already been discussed in the literature, see for example
Maldacena:2015iua
, and it is related to the contribution of double trace operators to the sum (
258
).
In this case the factor
\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau/2}\right)}
becomes
\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\gamma_{n,J,i}/2}\right)}
where
n
is related to the twist,
J
is spin, and
i
accounts for the degeneracies of the double trace operators in question. We now see that

\displaystyle\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\gamma_{n,J,i}\over 2}\right)}\leq 1\,,\qquad c_{T}-\text{finite}.

(262)

If on the other hand we first expand at large
c_{T}
using the fact that
\gamma_{n,J,i}\sim{1/c_{T}}\ll 1

we get

\displaystyle\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\gamma_{n,J,i}\over 2}\right)}={\pi^{2}\over 4}\gamma_{n,J,i}^{2}+O(1/c_{T}^{4})\,.

(263)

To compute the contribution of double trace operators to (
258
) we need to sum both over spin
J
and twists of the operators labeled by
n
. Because the anomalous dimensions
\gamma_{n,J,i}
are growing functions of
n
, it is the sum over twists of the operators in (
258
) that produces terms that diverge in the limit
\omega_{2}\to 0
. Non-perturbatively we know that an infinite series of
{1/c_{T}}
corrections turn
\gamma_{n,J,i}^{2}
into (
262
) in this way regulating the sum.
This mechanism of regulating the divergences in perturbation theory is sometimes called “eikonalization”
Fitzpatrick:2015qma
. To summarize, we see that the large twist operators can generate divergences in
{1/c_{T}}
perturbation theory but they are absent at finite
c_{T}
.

Let us next work out the form of the
{1/\omega_{2}^{2}}
term in (
7.1
) in more detail. In order to do it we replace
C_{\tau,J}^{2}
in (
258
) with its leading asymptotic behaviour in mean field theory at tree level

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\langle C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle=c_{T}2^{5}\pi(J+1)4^{-J}4^{-\tau}\tau^{2}\,.

(264)

For the anomalous dimensions we use the results of
Aprile:2017bgs
to get

\displaystyle\lim_{c_{T}\to\infty}{\langle 2\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau\over 2}\right)}C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle\over\langle C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle}\sim{\pi^{2}\over 2}{\langle\gamma_{\tau,J}^{2}C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle\over\langle C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle}\sim{\tau^{11}\over c_{T}^{2}}\,.

(265)

Substituting these relations into (
258
) and taking into account (
260
) we find that the contribution of the operators with large twist and
J=0
involves the following integral

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\infty}d\tau\ \langle C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle{\langle 2\sin^{2}{\left({\pi\tau\over 2}\right)}C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle\over\langle C_{\tau,J}^{2}\rangle}\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J=0}(z,0,\omega_{2})(1+\omega_{2})^{-{\tau\over 2}}

\displaystyle\sim{1\over c_{T}}\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\infty}d\tau\,2^{-\tau}\tau^{{15\over 2}}\ _{2}F_{1}\left({\tau-6\over 2},{\tau-6\over 2},\tau-1,{1\over 1+\omega_{2}}\right)(1+\omega_{2})^{-{\tau\over 2}}\,,

(266)

where we replaced the sum over twist by an integral over
\tau>\tau_{0}
.
To understand its behaviour for
\omega_{2}\to 0
it is convenient to use the integral representation of the hypergeometric function. The result then takes the following form at large
\tau

\displaystyle{1\over c_{T}}\int_{0}^{1}dt\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\infty}d\tau\tau^{8}{(1-t)(1-t+\omega_{2})^{3}\over t^{4}}\Big({1-t+\omega_{2}\over t(1-t)}\Big)^{-{\tau\over 2}}\sim{1\over c_{T}\omega_{2}^{2}}\,.

(267)

The integral over
\tau
is trivial to do. The integral over
t
can be easily analyzed numerically or by the saddle point method, with the expected result that it behaves as
{1/\omega_{2}^{2}}
. In this way we have also correctly reproduced the dependence on
\omega_{2}
of the first term in (
7.1
). We have not tried to reproduce the
{1/\omega_{2}}
term using the formulas above. It would be interesting to do it by analyzing the
{1/\tau}
corrections as well as to generalize the above analysis to higher loops.

Let us also comment on how contact terms
\delta(\omega_{2})
originate from the finite coupling formulas above in the perturbative expansion. Consider an exchange by a heavy operator (
\tau\gg 1
), which could be for example a stringy mode, in the dispersion representation (
7.2
) of the Mellin amplitude
\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})
. Expanding the right-hand side of (
7.2
)
in
{1/\tau}
, we get polynomial in
j
and
j_{2}
corrections to the Mellin amplitude. As we explained, these yield contact terms in the generalized event shape
\text{EEC}(0,\omega_{2},z)
localized at
\omega_{2}=0
. Let us now see how this comes about directly from (
258
). In this case we can write the following distributional identity

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}(1+\omega_{2})^{-{\tau\over 2}}={2\over\tau}\delta(\omega_{2})+{4\over\tau^{2}}\Big(\delta(\omega_{2})+\delta^{\prime}(\omega_{2})\Big)+O(1/\tau^{3})\,.

(268)

These are terms that will appear in the generalized event shapes in string perturbation theory, with the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda
controlling the twist of the stringy modes. On the other hand, by going back to the finite coupling we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\lim_{\omega_{2}\to 0}(1+\omega_{2})^{-{\tau\over 2}}=1,

(269)

so that the limits
\tau\to\infty
and
\omega_{2}\to 0
do not commute. To summarize, the contact terms in the perturbative expansion of generalized event shapes signal the presence of enhanced terms in the undeformed event shapes.
In the example above,
2\delta(\omega_{2})/\tau
gets replaced with
1
after resummation of corrections in
1/\tau
.

7.3
Small
\omega
and the Regge limit

Let us show that the leading behavior of the EEC as
\omega_{2}\to 0
is related to the behavior of the Mellin amplitude in the Regge limit.
We use (
148
), (
151
) and (
204
) to write down the expression for
\text{EEC}(0,\omega_{2},z)
in the Mellin space

\displaystyle\text{EEC}(0,\omega_{2},z)=\left(1+\omega_{2}+\frac{\omega_{2}^{2}}{6}\right)\int{dj\over 2\pi i}{1\over 2}{\pi\over\sin\pi j}{z^{-j-2}\left(1-z\right)^{j-1}\over j^{2}(j+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\int{dj_{2}\over 2\pi i}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})\,\,.

(270)

In order to understand the small
\omega_{2}
expansion, the relevant integral is thus

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta-i\infty}^{-\delta+i\infty}dj_{2}\,(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})=\int_{0}^{\infty}dj_{2}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{2}}{\rm Disc}_{j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})\,,

(271)

where we evaluated the integral by closing the contour to the right for
\omega_{2}>0
and picking the contribution from the poles in
j_{2}
, or, equivalently, the discontinuity of the Mellin amplitude. Imagine that the discontinuity of the Mellin amplitude has the following Regge behavior as
j_{2}\to\infty

30
30

30

Since
\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})
is a meromorphic function of
j_{2}
, this formula should be understood as a statement about averaged discontinuity.

\displaystyle{\rm Disc}_{j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})\sim j_{2}^{\alpha}\,.

(272)

Via the integral (
271
) it will produce terms as singular as
{1/\omega_{2}^{1+\alpha}}
in the Regge limit.
At one loop we get
{\rm Disc}_{j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{1},j_{2})\sim j_{2}
as
j_{2}\to\infty
, which via (
271
) produces
{1/\omega_{2}^{2}}
.
It would be interesting to explore the limit
\omega_{2}\to 0
and correspondingly the Regge limit of the Mellin amplitude at two and higher loops using the results of
Bissi:2020woe
.

We however did check the relevant scalings by looking at the discontinuity
{\rm Disc}_{s}M(s,t)
of the corresponding flat space supergravity amplitude
M(s,t)
, where
s
and
t
are the standard Mandelstam invariants in flat space.
At one loop the supergravity amplitude takes the following form in the Regge limit, see for example formula (39) in
Alday:2018kkw
,

\displaystyle{\rm Disc}_{s}M^{1-\text{loop}}(s,t)\sim s\,.

(273)

The leading term being analytic in
t
corresponds to scattering at zero impact parameters, which is consistent from the analysis above where it emerged from intermediate operators of fixed spin and large twist. A more familiar eikonal phase in ten dimensions behaves instead as
{t^{2}\log(-t)/s}
. It controls the leading Regge behavior of scattering at non-zero impact parameters which manifests itself in the fact that it is not analytic in
t
. We observe the same phenomenon for the expansion of
{\rm Disc}_{j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})
in one loop supergravity. The term
{1/j_{2}}
comes with the
j
-dependent pre-factor which is non-analytic. Via (
271
) it will contribute to
\log\omega_{2}
term in (
7.1
). As opposed to
{1/\omega_{2}^{2}}
and
{1/\omega_{2}}
terms, it is non-analytic in
z
(due to the presence of
\log z
). This can be traced to non-analyticity in
j
of the
{1/j_{2}}
term in the large
j_{2}
expansion of
{\rm Disc}_{j_{2}}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})
. This in turn can be traced back to the universal high energy limit of the eikonal phase in gravity at non-zero impact parameters, see e.g.
Amati:1990xe
. It would be interesting to make this connection more explicit using the eikonal phase in the AdS analysis of
Cornalba:2007zb
.

8
Ordinary event shapes at finite
\lambda
and finite
c_{T}

At weak coupling, given the perturbative expansion of the correlation function one can systematically compute the perturbative corrections to the event shapes. The situation is very different at strong coupling. In this case there is no simple way of translating the perturbative expansion of the correlation function to the perturbative expansion of the event shape. At the technical level, this is related to the fact that both the stringy
{1/\lambda}
and the
{1/c_{T}}
gravitational loop corrections yield expressions for the correlation functions that grow too fast in the Regge limit for the event shape of interest to be well defined. In the collider physics setup studied in this paper, the Regge limit divergence is translated to a large detector time effect. It arises because the correlation function describes the energy correlations with the property that the energy flux does not decay with time and generates a divergence when the working time of the detector goes to infinity.

The divergences are absent at finite
\lambda
and finite
c_{T}
where, based on general arguments, we know that the Regge limit is such that, say, the energy-energy correlation is well defined. Computing the corrections to the event shapes thus requires resummation of the naive perturbative expansion. Let us illustrate this phenomenon with a few examples.

8.1
Stringy corrections

The simplest example of this phenomenon arises when we consider stringy corrections. The leading order correction to the Mellin amplitude takes the form

Goncalves:2014ffa
;
Binder:2019jwn

\displaystyle\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})=\tilde{M}^{\text{strong}}(j_{1},j_{2})+{60\zeta(3)\over\lambda^{3/2}}+O(\lambda^{-5/2})\ \,.

(274)

Substituting this relation into (
7.3
) and taking into account (
4.5
), we can immediately write down the result for the generalized energy-energy correlations with non-zero
\omega_{2}
,

\displaystyle\text{EEC}(0,\omega_{2},z)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\omega_{2}+{\omega_{2}^{2}\over 6}\right)

\displaystyle\left[{1\over 2}\left(\theta(\omega_{2})+\theta(-\omega_{2}){(1+\omega_{2})^{2}\over(1+\omega_{2}z)^{3}}\right)+{120\zeta(3)\over\lambda^{3/2}}\delta(\omega_{2})(1-6z+6z^{2})+\dots\right]\,.

(275)

On the other hand, the direct computation of the
\omega_{2}=0
event shape in string theory gives
Hofman:2008ar

\displaystyle\text{EEC}(z)\equiv\text{EEC}(0,0,z)={1\over 2}\left(1+{4\pi^{2}\over\lambda}(1-6z+6z^{2})+\dots\right)\,,

(276)

where dots in this and the previous expression denote corrections suppressed by powers of
\lambda^{-1/2}
.

Let us discuss how the three formulas above are related to each other. The relation (
276
) can be understood
by taking into account that the leading correction to the Mellin amplitude (
274
) scales as

\tilde{M}(j_{1},j_{2})|_{\lambda^{-3/2}}\sim 1
in the Regge limit
j_{2}\to\infty
. For this reason the integral
\int dj_{2}\tilde{M}(j-j_{2},j_{2})
that appears in the computation of the energy-energy correlation (
7.3
) is divergent at
\omega_{2}=0
.
The stringy effects effectively cut the Mellin integral off at
j_{2}\sim\sqrt{\lambda}
leading to (
276
). The evaluation of the precise coefficient in (
276
) is a nontrivial task, requiring the resummation of the string perturbative corrections. Yet it can be done explicitly, see
Goncalves:2014ffa
for details. In terms of the dispersion relations the mechanism is precisely the same as described above in Section
7.2
, which also explains the dependence on
z
.

Comparing the relations (
8.1
) and (
276
), we observe that the leading correction to both expressions is proportional to the same function of
z
but has different dependence on
\lambda
. The presence of the
\delta(\omega_{2})
term in (
8.1
) signifies that the corresponding detector measures a time-independent energy flux. If we imagine a detector with a finite working time
T
this would produce an effect of order
{T/\lambda^{3/2}}
. Letting the working time
T\to\infty
produces then a divergent result. What happens at finite
\lambda
however is that at times
T_{\text{str}}\sim\lambda^{1/2}
string perturbation theory breaks down and the effect of the resummation is that the energy flux effectively becomes zero for times
T>T_{\text{str}}
.

8.2
Gravitational loops

Let us now try to generalize this discussion to include gravitational loops. It is natural to expect that the same mechanism is at play: the parts of the one-loop result (
7.1
) that diverge in the
\omega_{2}\to 0
limit get enhanced by
\lambda
or
c_{T}
dependent factors coming from the long time effects in the detector.
The presence of such terms, which are enhanced compared to the naive expectation coming from the perturbative expansion of the correlation function, is a characteristic feature of strongly coupled conformal field theories with semi-classical gravity duals.

Based on the OPE data of double trace operators and the mechanism discussed in section
7.2
, we associate the terms
{1/\omega_{2}^{2}}
and
{1/\omega_{2}}
with high energy scattering at zero impact parameters. In the flat space limit this manifests itself through the fact that the corresponding terms in the Regge limit of
{\rm Disc}_{s}M(s,t)
are analytic in
t
. In string theory, a fixed-angle scattering is very soft in the high energy limit
Gross:1987ar
. We therefore expect that the divergences for
\omega_{2}\to 0
get regularized in the same way as in the example with string corrections above: namely the
j_{2}
integral is cut off at
j_{2}\sim\sqrt{\lambda}
. Equivalently, we expect that the divergences to leading order get substituted by
{1/\omega_{2}}\to\sqrt{\lambda}
. From this argument we expect the leading stringy correction to be
31
31

31

The same term originates from the correction
c_{\infty}\delta(\omega_{2})
discussed in the previous section.

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{\text{QG}}(z)\propto{\lambda\over c_{T}}(1-6z+6z^{2})+\dots\ \,.

(277)

Computing the precise coefficient requires a more detailed analysis. The correction (
277
) originates from two different sources:
T{\sqrt{\lambda}/c_{T}}
and
T^{2}/c_{T}
which grow with time and both get saturated at
T\sim T_{\text{str}}\sim\sqrt{\lambda}
.

We expect a different mechanism to be at play for the terms that diverge in the
\omega_{i}\to 0
limit which originate from scattering at non-zero impact parameters. Such divergences will be present in the
{1/c_{T}}
expansion of the event shape even at finite string tension, or, equivalently,
\lambda
. These terms get unitarized at finite
c_{T}
via the eikonalization of the gravitational loops. Because of this we also expect to have
c_{T}
-enhanced terms in the expansion of the event shapes. Effects coming from non-zero impact parameters originates from operators of arbitrary large spin, see e.g.

Cornalba:2006xk
;
Cornalba:2006xm
. Given that
\widetilde{\text{EEC}}_{\tau,J}(z,0,\omega_{2})
are polynomials in
z
of the maximal power
z^{J}
, any non-analyticity in
z
can only come from the large spin operators. As an example at one loop, the divergent and non-analytic in
z
piece
\sim{1\over c_{T}}\log|\omega_{2}|\log z
should originate from the expansion of the eikonal phase in AdS
Cornalba:2007zb
. Indeed, the flat space limit of the part of the one-loop supergravity Mellin amplitude that generates
{1\over c_{T}}\log|\omega_{2}|\log z
term matches the expected expansion of the eikonal phase

DiVecchia:2019myk
;
DiVecchia:2019kta
.

The
\log|\omega_{2}|
divergence in (
7.1
) corresponds to the long-time tail which grows as
\log T
as a function of the detector working time
T
. We thus see that at finite but large working times the leading contribution to the energy correlation takes the form

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{\text{QG}}(z,T)\propto{\log T\over c_{T}}\left(1-36z+216z^{2}-400z^{3}+225z^{4}\right)\log z\,.

(278)

This correction is universal (it does not depend on the details of the spectrum of the bulk theory) and originates from the fact that the dual theory is gravitational, with gravity being the leading interaction at high energies and large impact parameters. As a nontrivial consistency check, we verified that (
279
) satisfies the Ward identities (
248
).

To recover the energy-energy correlation
\text{EEC}(z)
we have to take the limit
T\to\infty
. Doing it rigorously requires the resummation of the
1/c_{T}
perturbation theory. This might be possible using the eikonalization of the gravitational amplitudes in AdS but we do not attempt it in the present paper. Still, let us discuss the simplest possible scenario in which the energy flux that grows as
\log T
gets saturated at some time
T_{\text{QG}}
. Here
T_{\text{QG}}
is some characteristic time that goes to infinity as
c_{T}\to\infty
. From the eikonalization of the gravitational amplitudes we expect that
\log T_{\text{QG}}\sim\log c_{T}
. In this simple scenario, we get that the leading quantum gravity correction to the energy-energy correlation takes the form

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{\text{QG}}(z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\propto}}{\log c_{T}\over c_{T}}\left(1-36z+216z^{2}-400z^{3}+225z^{4}\right)\log z\,.

(279)

It would be very interesting to derive the leading quantum gravity correction to the energy-energy correlation (
279
) rigorously.

9
Conclusions and future directions

In this paper we have introduced a new class of observables in a collider physics setting which we dubbed generalized event shapes. They are defined as the matrix elements of light-ray operators (
2
) with non-zero momentum, (
7
). These light-ray operators have a finite resolution in time, given by
{1/\hat{\omega}}
, and thus allow us to probe the longitudinal structure of the state. By setting
\hat{\omega}=0
they smoothly transit over to the familiar event shapes.

The generalized event shapes are
not
the standard differential cross sections, since they are sensitive to the relative phases of the various scattering amplitudes. This makes them harder to measure, but from the theoretical point of view they are very natural observables. They are IR safe and can be evaluated both in terms of scattering amplitudes and correlation functions.
If defined in terms of integrated Wightman functions, for
\hat{\omega}\neq 0
the new observables retain all the information contained in the former. We therefore expect that they form a convenient basis of collider-type observables (at least in a CFT). Indeed, many popular jet observables can be restated in terms of energy correlations, see e.g.
Chen:2020vvp
.

The generalized event shapes exhibit new features compared to the usual event shapes. One interesting example is that they develop a bulk point singularity at strong coupling. Approaching the singularity requires analytic continuation of the physical parameters and in this way it is reminiscent of the flat space singularity in the de Sitter correlators, see e.g.
Arkani-Hamed:2015bza
. Another characteristic feature of the generalized event shapes is that they are non-analytic around
\hat{\omega}=0
. This is related to the fact that, depending on the sign of
\hat{\omega}
, the detector operators can either create or annihilate particles. Nonperturbatively this fact manifests itself in Eq. (
1
).

We studied the generalized event shapes perturbatively in planar
{\cal N}=4
SYM both at weak and strong coupling. The results are summarized in Section
6
. First, we considered the case where the source, the sink and the detectors are built out of local scalar operators. In this case, at weak coupling we obtained the leading-order result using both scattering amplitudes and correlation functions. At strong coupling we have only the correlation functions approach at our disposal. Second, we used the
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry Ward identities to compute generalized event shapes with detectors defined by conserved currents, namely charge and energy correlations. In the special case where only one of the detectors has
\hat{\omega}\neq 0
, in Section
5.3
we found remarkably simple relations between the various observables. When both detectors carry non-zero momentum we found that relatively simple relations exist not among the observables themselves but among their Mellin kernels, see Eq. (
193
).

Unlike the ordinary event shapes, the generalized event shapes can be computed perturbatively order by order in the
{1/c_{T}}
and
{1/\lambda}
at strong coupling. A characteristic feature of the stringy and quantum-gravitational corrections to the generalized event shapes is that they are divergent in the limit
\hat{\omega}\to 0
. These divergences signify a longitudinal broadening of the state and the presence of long-time tails in the measured radiation. For ordinary event shapes, which perform measurements at arbitrarily late times, this leads to corrections which are enhanced compared to those coming from the perturbative expansion of the correlation function.
We studied the one-loop supergravity corrections to the generalized event shapes in Section
7
. We also derived a dispersive representation of the generalized event shape and used it to analyze the result. The structure of the ordinary event shapes at finite
\lambda
and finite
c_{T}
is discussed in Section
8
.

Let us list some interesting open questions that we did not address in the present paper:

•

Probably the most important question is to understand whether the generalized event shapes, or some of their cousins, can be measured in an experiment. We commented on the special case of low energy elastic scattering, where this should be possible indirectly. It would be interesting to find out if the same or a similar method generalizes to higher energies.

•

It would be very interesting to study generalized event shapes in QCD. The computation is analogous to the scattering amplitude one in Section
3
. We expect that the final result will be qualitatively similar to that in
{\cal N}=4
SYM, though the details will differ. This is what happens to the ordinary event shapes and we expect this relation between
{\cal N}=4
SYM and QCD to hold for the generalized event shapes as well.

•

We motivated our study by thinking about energy fluxes in real time, but for technical reasons it was more convenient to work with detectors that carry a definite momentum, rather than detectors that work for a given period of time. We demonstrated the transition between the two pictures on the simple example of the one-point generalized event shapes in Appendix
J
. We observed the effect of the longitudinal broadening at strong coupling which manifests itself through the divergences in the
\hat{\omega}\to 0
limit, or, equivalently, the long-time tails of the radiation measured by the detectors. It would be interesting to connect our results to the broadening picture explored in
Hatta:2012kn
.

•

We emphasized that for
\hat{\omega}\neq 0
we can render a broader class of correlations well defined. A prominent example is the charge-charge correlation which is not IR safe for
\hat{\omega}=0
beyond one-loop order. It would be interesting to understand the physics of the
\hat{\omega}\to 0
limit of such observables and see if there is a way of constructing an observable that stays finite in that limit.

•

We did not discuss the OPE of light-ray operators with non-zero momentum. It would be useful to generalize the analysis of
Kologlu:2019bco
;
Kologlu:2019mfz
;
Chang:2020qpj
to this case. As we have seen, one obvious difference is that the spin selection rule
J=J_{1}+J_{2}-1
(here
J
is the spin of a light-ray operator that appears in the OPE of light-ray operators of spin
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
) does not hold anymore since
\hat{\omega}
carries non-zero spin. The light-ray OPE can also shed interesting light on the
\hat{\omega}\to 0
limit of the observables discussed in this paper.

•

We found that thinking about generalized event shapes is helpful for understanding the stringy and quantum gravity corrections to the ordinary event shapes. The basic phenomenon is that the
{1/\hat{\omega}}
divergences present in the generalized event shapes turn into
\lambda
or
c_{T}
enhanced terms in the ordinary event shapes. To establish this fact it is convenient to use a dispersive representation of the event shapes (we did it for the leading stringy corrections). For gravitational loop corrections that unitarize the scattering amplitudes at large impact parameters, a more thorough analysis should be possible.

•

The way how we introduced time dependence in the measurement at infinity is not unique. Our choice of generalized event shapes was dictated by the symmetries of the problem: given a source that carries a definite momentum, it is natural to have detectors that carry definite momenta as well. Another advantage of these detectors is that they naturally suppress the potential divergences at large working times
u
, see Eq. (
2.2
). This makes the corresponding matrix elements well defined, even if the original event shape at
\hat{\omega}=0
was not. Other bases of time-dependent light-ray operators have been discussed in the literature, see for instance
Besken:2020snx
.

•

It is known that the light transform of the stress-energy tensor, also called ANEC operator, is a non-negative operator in any unitary QFT

Faulkner:2016mzt
;
Hartman:2016lgu
. If instead we restrict the time integration to some finite interval we get what is known as the quantum null energy condition (QNEC)

Bousso:2015mna
;
Bousso:2015wca
;
Balakrishnan:2017bjg
. It bounds the energy flux measured over a time interval
T
from below by the shape variations of the entanglement entropy. It would be interesting to explore the implications of QNEC to the generalized event shapes.

•

In this paper, we only studied one- and two-point correlations. It would be interesting to extend the consideration to multi-point correlations, see e.g.
Chen:2019bpb
;
Chen:2021gdk
. The computation of the corrections to the lowest order in the coupling within the amplitude approach would require incorporating tree-level form factors with an arbitrary number of on-shell particles. Results for such form factors have been obtained in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
Bork:2014eqa
;
Bianchi:2018peu
. In the correlation function approach one would need expressions for the mutli-point correlation functions involving conserved currents. Such correlation functions can be computed following the approach developed in Refs.
Chicherin:2015bza
;
Chicherin:2014uca
;
Fleury:2019ydf
.

•

By placing the detector operators with non-zero null momentum both at past and future null infinity we can naturally define transition amplitudes in CFT. It would be interesting to explore the properties of such objects using CFT methods. It would be also very interesting to understand the connection between such objects and the transitions studied in
Korchemsky:2018hnb
;
Gillioz:2020mdd
.

•

Generalized event shapes can be defined and studied in gravity (see
Gonzo:2020xza
for a recent discussion of the ordinary event shapes). Indeed, they are IR safe observables and can be computed using the standard scattering amplitudes techniques. Moreover, in the case of gravitational waves for obvious reasons we cannot place the detectors around the collision point and essentially all the information acquired in the observation comes from the longitudinal, or time-dependent, details of the signal.

Appendix A
Conformal properties of the deformed light-ray operators

Here we recall some key points about the embedding formalism and index-free notation used in Section
2
. A more detailed introduction to it can be found, e.g., in
Costa:2011mg
. We then apply the formalism to give an alternative derivation of the conformal (
21
) and dilatation (
2.1
) transformations of the deformed light-ray operators.

Embedding formalism and index-free notation

The embedding formalism provides a redundant description of
d-
dimensional conformal tensors by raising them to
(d+2)-
dimensional ones. For example, a vector
V_{A}(X)
with index
A=(+,-,\mu)
has
d+2
components instead of the expected
d
for a vector
V_{\mu}(x)
. The
(d+2)-
dimensional null vector
X^{A}
(with
X\cdot X\equiv X^{+}X^{-}+X^{\mu}X_{\mu}=0
) is also redundant, having
d+1

independent coordinates instead of
d
.

This redundancy is removed in three steps:

1) Require
scale invariance
,

\displaystyle\Phi(\lambda X)=\lambda^{-\Delta}\Phi(X)

(280)

with some weight which will later on be identified with the conformal weight. Assuming that
X^{+}\neq 0
this allows us to define the scale invariant field

\displaystyle\phi(x)=(X^{+})^{\Delta}\Phi(X)\,,

(281)

where the
d-
dimensional vector
x^{\mu}
is identified with

\displaystyle{X^{A}\over X^{+}}=\left(1,\ {X^{-}\over X^{+}}\equiv-x^{2},\ {X^{\mu}\over X^{+}}\equiv x^{\mu}\right)\ \Rightarrow\ X\cdot X=0\,.

(282)

2) Impose
transversality
of the tensor fields, e.g. for the vector
