\displaystyle X\cdot V\equiv X^{A}V_{A}(X)=\frac{1}{2}(X^{+}V^{-}+X^{-}V^{+})+X^{\mu}V_{\mu}=0\,.

(283)

This condition removes one degree of freedom.

3) Consider the tensor field modulo
gauge transformations
, e.g. for the vector

\displaystyle V_{A}(X)\ \to\ V_{A}(X)+X_{A}\,s(X)\,,

(284)

where
s(X)
is an arbitrary scalar field that scales as
s(\lambda X)=\lambda^{-\Delta-1}s(X)
. This is the second redundant degree of freedom of the vector field that needs to be removed. Clearly, the transversality condition (
283
) is invariant under such gauge transformations.

The index-free notation consists in projecting the index
A
of the vector field with an auxiliary null vector
Z^{A}
(with
Z\cdot Z=0
),

\displaystyle V(X,Z)=Z^{A}V_{A}(X)\,.

(285)

To respect the gauge invariance (
284
) the vectors
X
and
Z
are assumed orthogonal,

\displaystyle Z\cdot X=0\,.

(286)

Then the transversality (
283
) takes the form of a
gauge invariance
condition (recall (
14
)),

\displaystyle V(X,Z+\beta X)=V(X,Z)\,.

(287)

The auxiliary vector
Z^{A}
undergoes independent scale transformations reflecting the homogeneous polynomial dependence on
Z
of the projected tensor. In the example of the vector field (
285
) we obviously have
V(X,\rho Z)=\rho V(X,Z)
. This scaling, together with (
280
), is generalized to the case of arbitrary positive integer spin
J
, see (
13
), or even to non-integer and negative spins by analytic continuation.

One can reduce the
(d+2)-
dimensional null vector
Z^{A}
, satisfying the orthogonality condition (
286
), to a
d-
dimensional null vector
z^{\mu}
by exploiting the gauge invariance (
287
). Indeed, consider the linear combination
Z^{A}+\beta X^{A}
and choose
\beta=-Z^{+}/X^{+}
:

\displaystyle Z^{A}+\beta X^{A}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta=-{Z^{+}\over X^{+}}}}{{=}}\ \left(0,\ Z^{-}-{Z^{+}\over X^{+}}X^{-}\equiv-2z\cdot x,\ Z^{\mu}-{Z^{+}\over X^{+}}X^{\mu}\equiv z^{\mu}\right)\Rightarrow\ z^{2}=0\,.

(288)

The vector
z^{\mu}
is traditionally used for describing symmetric traceless tensors, see e.g.
Dobrev:1977qv
.

Conformal transformations

The main point of the embedding formalism is that the
d-
dimensional conformal group acts linearly on the
(d+2)-
dimensional vectors, e.g.
X^{A}\to\Lambda^{A}{}_{B}X^{B}
with
\Lambda\in SO(2,d)
. The benefit is that one can easily build invariants of such linear transformations, see e.g.
Costa:2011mg
. The price to pay is the redundancy discussed above and the necessity to fix various gauges, if we want to recover the familiar Lorentz tensors, e.g. the vector field
V(X,Z)\to V_{\mu}(x)
.

According to this concept, a
Z-
projected tensor field is
invariant
under
SO(2,d)
,

\displaystyle O(\Lambda X,\Lambda Z)=O(X,Z)\,,

(289)

and in addition has the scaling properties (
13
).

The conformal group
SO(2,d)
is extended to
O(2,d)
by adding the discrete operation of
inversion
,
X^{d+2}\to-X^{d+2}
or equivalently,
X^{+}\leftrightarrow-X^{-}
. In the fixed frame (
282
) we find

\displaystyle x^{\mu}={X^{\mu}\over X^{+}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\rightarrow}}\ -{X^{\mu}\over X^{-}}=-{X^{+}\over X^{-}}{X^{\mu}\over X^{+}}={x^{\mu}\over x^{2}}\,.

(290)

The inversion of the vector
Z^{A}
in the fixed frame (
288
) is accompanied by a compensating gauge transformation (
287
) with parameter
\beta=Z^{-}/X^{-}-Z^{+}/X^{+}
. This implies
Dobrev:1977qv

\displaystyle z^{\mu}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\rightarrow}}\ z^{\mu}-\frac{2z\cdot x}{x^{2}}x^{\mu}\,,\qquad(I[z^{\mu}])^{2}=0\,,\qquad I^{2}[z^{\mu}]=z^{\mu}\,.

(291)

Infinitesimal translations and conformal boosts

The conformal algebra is generated by
d-
dimensional translations
P^{+\mu}
and conformal boosts
K^{-\mu}
. In the embedding space they are realized by
SO(2,d)
matrices of the block form

\displaystyle\Lambda^{A}{}_{B}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\pm}{}_{\pm}&2b^{\pm}{}_{\nu}\\
\hline\cr-b^{\mu}{}_{\pm}&\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\end{array}\right)

with infinitesimal parameters
b^{\pm}\equiv b_{\mp}
. We find

\displaystyle\delta X^{\pm}=2b^{\pm\mu}X_{\mu}\,,\qquad\delta X^{\mu}=-b^{-\mu}X^{+}-b^{+\mu}X^{-}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta(X\cdot X)=0\,,

(294)

so that

\displaystyle\delta x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\delta\left({X^{\mu}\over X^{+}}\right)=-b^{-\mu}+b^{+\mu}\,x^{2}-2(b^{+}x)\,x^{\mu}\,,

(295)

where
b^{-\mu}
is the parameter of infinitesimal translations and
b^{+\mu}
of conformal boosts.

The auxiliary null vector
Z^{A}
transforms in the same way,

\displaystyle\delta Z^{\pm}=2b^{\pm\mu}Z_{\mu}\,,\qquad\delta Z^{\mu}=-b^{-\mu}Z^{+}-b^{+\mu}Z^{-}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta(Z\cdot Z)=0\,.

(296)

Applied to the fixed frame variable
z^{\mu}
in (
288
) this transformation becomes
32
32

32

The transformation (
297
) can also be obtained from the inversion (
291
) and the relation
K=IPI
.

\displaystyle\delta z^{\mu}=\delta\left(Z^{\mu}-{Z^{+}\over X^{+}}X^{\mu}\right)=2b^{+\mu}(xz)-2x^{\mu}(zb^{+})\quad\Rightarrow\ \delta(z^{2})=0\,.

(297)

We see that
z^{\mu}
transforms only under conformal boosts and
\delta z^{\mu}|_{x=0}=0
.

Transformations of tensor fields

As we explained, the
Z-
projected fields
O(X,Z)
are inert under conformal transformations, see (
289
). To recover the familiar tensor field transformations we have to ‘undress’
O(X,Z)
to an ordinary field with Lorentz indices. Let us see how this works in the example of the vector field (
285
). First, we use the gauge freedom (
284
) and (
287
) to fix the gauges

\displaystyle V^{-}(X)=Z^{+}=0\,.

(298)

A conformal boost with parameter
b^{+}
should be accompanied by compensating gauge transformations to maintain the gauges (
298
). Then we use the fact that the projected vector field
V(X,Z)
is invariant under the conformal transformations to write down

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\delta V(X,Z)

\displaystyle=z^{\mu}\left(\delta V_{\mu}(x)+\delta x^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}V_{\mu}(x)-2\Delta(b^{+}x)\,V_{\mu}(x)+2b^{+}_{\mu}x^{\nu}V_{\nu}(x)-2x_{\mu}b^{+\nu}V_{\nu}(x)\right)\,.

(299)

Here
\delta x^{\nu}
is the coordinate transformation (
295
) and the weight factor comes from the definition (
281
) of the ordinary field and from (
294
). We have also used the transformation (
297
) of
z^{\mu}
. Stripping off
z^{\mu}
, we find the familiar conformal transformation of a vector field. The generalization to tensors of arbitrary integer spin is straightforward.

Transformations of the deformed light-ray operators

Comparing (
18
) and (
14
), we observe that the light-ray operators acquire a phase under the gauge transformation.
When we fix the frame (
288
), the gauge parameter
\beta=-Z^{+}/X^{+}
appears in the exponential factor

\displaystyle{\bf L}_{\hat{\omega}}[O]\left(X,Z-{Z^{+}\over X^{+}}X\right)=e^{i\hat{\omega}Z^{+}/X^{+}}{\bf L}_{\hat{\omega}}[O](X,Z)\,.

(300)

According to (
295
) and (
297
) both arguments of
O\left(x-{z\over\alpha},z\right)
in (
20
) are inert under conformal boosts at the origin
x=0
. However, the exponential factor in (
300
) transforms:

\displaystyle\delta_{b^{+}}\left({Z^{+}\over X^{+}}\right)={2b^{+}\cdot Z\over X^{+}}-{2b^{+}\cdot X\,Z^{+}\over(X^{+})^{2}}\ \ \underset{X^{+}=1,\ Z^{\mu}=z^{\mu}}{\overset{x^{\mu}=X^{\mu}/X^{+}=0}{\xrightarrow{\hskip 56.9055pt}}}\ \ 2(b^{+}z)\,.

(301)

This leads to the relation (
21
) but its derivation is different from that in (
22
). There we implicitly ‘undressed’ the tensors from the projection variables
z^{\mu}
and then applied the standard tensor transformation rules like (
A
). Here we treated both
x
and
z
on the same footing and we saw that the effect (
21
) originates from the compensating
\beta-
gauge transformations needed to restore the special frame (
288
).

The dilatation transformation can be obtained from the commutator
[P^{+\mu},K^{-\nu}]=\eta^{\mu\nu}D+L^{\mu\nu}
. Commuting two variations with parameters
b^{+}
and
b^{-}
, we find

\displaystyle\delta_{D}x^{\mu}=\tau\,x^{\mu}\,,\quad\delta_{D}z^{\mu}=0\,,\quad\delta_{D}X^{+}=-\tau X^{+}\,,\quad\delta_{D}Z^{+}=-\tau Z^{+}\,,

(302)

where
\tau=-2b^{+}\cdot b^{-}
is the infinitesimal dilatation parameter. The variation of
x
in the combination
x-z/\alpha
on the right-hand side of (
20
) is accompanied by a compensating transformation of the integration parameter
\alpha
,
\delta_{D}\alpha=-\tau\,\alpha
.
The change of variable in the integral in (
20
) produces the weight
\lambda^{1-J}
(with
\lambda=1+\tau
) in (
2.1
). The extra weight
\lambda^{-\Delta}
is absorbed by the defining factor
(X^{+})^{-\Delta}
in (
12
) (see also (
281
)), which is implicit in (
20
).
This reproduces (
2.1
), without setting
x=0
in the dilatation transformations.

Appendix B
Reality property of generalized event shapes

In this appendix we
derive the reality condition for the one-point generalized event shapes (
47
).
We have

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}(\hat{\omega},n)\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J}\over\eta_{{\cal O}}}\langle\phi^{\dagger}(\tilde{q})\Big((\mathsf{CRT}){\cal O}(\hat{\omega},n)(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\Big)^{\dagger}\phi(q)\rangle

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J}\over\eta_{{\cal O}}}\langle(\mathsf{CRT}){\cal O}(\hat{\omega},n)(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(\tilde{q})|(\mathsf{CRT})(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(q)\rangle

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J}\over\eta_{{\cal O}}}\langle(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(q)|{\cal O}(\hat{\omega},n)(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(\tilde{q})\rangle

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J}\over\eta_{{\cal O}}}\langle\phi^{\dagger}(q)(\mathsf{CRT}){\cal O}(\hat{\omega},n)(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(\tilde{q})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\phi(q)^{\dagger}{\cal O}(-\hat{\omega},n)\phi(\tilde{q})\rangle=\langle{\cal O}(-\hat{\omega},n)\rangle_{q+\hat{\omega}_{1}n_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{2}n_{2}},

(303)

Let us set, for example,
\Delta_{\phi}=2
,
d=4
. We then get for the detectors of interest

\displaystyle f_{\text{S}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=f_{\text{S}}\left(-{\omega\over\omega+1}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle f_{\text{Q}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=(1+\omega)f_{\text{Q}}\left(-{\omega\over\omega+1}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle f_{\text{E}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=(1+\omega)^{2}f_{\text{E}}\left(-{\omega\over\omega+1}\right)\ \,.

(304)

The explicit expressions (
58
) and (
59
) satisfy (
B
) indeed.

For the one-point generalized event shapes (
2.5
) we have

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{1}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n){\cal O}_{2}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J_{1}+J_{2}}\over\eta_{{\cal O}_{1}}\eta_{{\cal O}_{2}}}\langle\phi^{\dagger}(\tilde{q})\Big((\mathsf{CRT}){\cal O}_{1}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{2}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\Big)^{\dagger}\phi(q)\rangle

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J_{1}+J_{2}}\over\eta_{{\cal O}_{1}}\eta_{{\cal O}_{2}}}\langle(\mathsf{CRT}){\cal O}_{1}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{2}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(\tilde{q})|(\mathsf{CRT})(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(q)\rangle

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J_{1}+J_{2}}\over\eta_{{\cal O}_{1}}\eta_{{\cal O}_{2}}}\langle(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(q)|{\cal O}_{1}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{2}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(\tilde{q})\rangle

\displaystyle={(-1)^{J_{1}+J_{2}}\over\eta_{{\cal O}_{1}}\eta_{{\cal O}_{2}}}\langle\phi^{\dagger}(q)(\mathsf{CRT}){\cal O}_{1}(\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{2}(\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})(\mathsf{CRT})^{-1}\phi(\tilde{q})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\phi^{\dagger}(q){\cal O}_{1}(-\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{2}(-\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})\phi(\tilde{q})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\cal O}_{1}(-\hat{\omega}_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}_{2}(-\hat{\omega}_{2},n_{2})\rangle_{q+\hat{\omega}_{1}n_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{2}n_{2}}\ \,.

(305)

Going from the second to the third line we used the anti-unitary property of
\mathsf{CRT}
. Together with (
50
) this results in the relation (
54
).

Appendix C
One-point function

In this appendix we consider the simplest correlation function involving a single flow operator

\displaystyle\langle{\phi(\tilde{q})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)\phi(q)}\rangle=\int d^{d}x_{1}e^{-i\tilde{q}x_{1}}\int d^{d}x_{2}e^{-iqx_{2}}\langle{\phi(x_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)\phi(x_{2})}\rangle\,,

(306)

where the scalar operators
\phi(x_{2})
and
\phi(x_{1})
define the source and sink, respectively, and the flow operator is given by (
31
). Its calculation
will serve as an illustration of the general formalism for computing more complicated observables from correlation functions.

For simplicity we work in
d=4
dimensions, for arbitrary
d
the formulae become more complicated. For the lowest values of the spin
J=0,1,2
, the flow operator in (
306
) is identified as the scalar, charge or energy detector, respectively.
After inserting the definition (
31
) of
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)
in (
306
), we encounter a three-point Wightman correlation function involving
two scalar operators
\phi
with scaling dimension
\Delta
and the spinning twist-two operator
O_{J}(x)\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{J}}(x)\bar{n}^{\mu_{1}}\dots\bar{n}^{\mu_{J}}
. Its form is fixed by conformal symmetry up to an overall normalization factor,

\displaystyle\langle{\phi(x_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(x_{3})\phi(x_{2})}\rangle={\mathcal{N}_{J}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{32}^{2}x_{12}^{2\Delta-2}}{\left({{2i(x_{13}\bar{n})\over x_{13}^{2}}+{2i(x_{32}\bar{n})\over x_{32}^{2}}}\right)}^{J}\,,

(307)

where we used a shorthand notation for
x_{ij}=x_{i}-x_{j}
. The poles of the correlation function come with an ‘
\pm i0
’ prescription whose sign depends on the time separation of the operators:

\displaystyle x_{12}^{2}\to x_{12}^{2}-i0x_{12}^{0}\,,\qquad x_{13}^{2}\to x_{13}^{2}-i0x_{13}^{0}\,,\qquad x_{32}^{2}\to x_{32}^{2}-i0x_{32}^{0}\,.

(308)

In (
307
) we have tacitly assumed that the operator
O_{J}(x)
has the canonical scaling dimension
2+J
. This is certainly true for
J=1
and
J=2
since the corresponding operators are the conserved
U(1)
current and the stress-energy tensor. In a generic interacting conformal theory, the operators with
J=0
and
J\geq 3
are not protected and their scaling dimension differs from the canonical dimension
2+J
, unless there exists an additional symmetry that protects them.
33
33

33

This happens in particular in
{\cal N}=4
SYM where the scalar operator with
J=0
is half-BPS.
Since we are mostly interested in the case
J\leq 2
, we shall assume that the operators
O_{J}(x)
are protected.

To obtain
\langle{\phi(x_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)\phi(x_{2})}\rangle
from (
307
), we have to replace
x_{3}=rn+\alpha\bar{n}
and apply the operations specified in (
31
)
(take the limit
r\to\infty
and integrate over
\alpha
). This can be done using the following identity

\displaystyle{1\over x_{13}^{2}x_{32}^{2}}{\left({{2i(x_{13}\bar{n})\over x_{13}^{2}}+{2i(x_{32}\bar{n})\over x_{32}^{2}}}\right)}^{J}=P_{J}\left(i(\bar{n}\partial_{x_{1}}),i(\bar{n}\partial_{x_{2}})\right){1\over x_{13}^{2}x_{32}^{2}}\,,

(309)

where
P_{J}(s_{1},s_{2})
is a homogenous polynomial in
s_{i}
of degree
J
. Its form can be found by comparing the coefficients in front of the powers of
(x_{13}\bar{n})
on both sides of (
309
) ,

\displaystyle P_{J}(s_{1},s_{2})=\sum_{k=0}^{J}{\left({J\atop k}\right)}{(-s_{1})^{k}\over k!}{s_{2}^{J-k}\over(J-k)!}\,,

(310)

and can be identified as Gegenbauer polynomials. Replacing
x_{3}=rn+\alpha\bar{n}
we find for
r\gg 1

\displaystyle{1\over x_{13}^{2}x_{32}^{2}}

\displaystyle\to{1\over 4r^{2}(n(x_{1}-\alpha\bar{n})-i0)((n(\alpha\bar{n}-x_{2})-i0)}

\displaystyle=-{1\over 4r^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,e^{-is_{1}(n(x_{1}-\alpha\bar{n}))-is_{2}(n(\alpha\bar{n}-x_{2}))}\,,

(311)

where we only kept the leading term.
Here the minus sign in the exponent in the second relation is dictated by the ‘
-i0
’ prescription of the propagator in the first line.

The rational for using the integral representation (
C
) is that, upon substituting (
C
) into (
309
), the action of the differential operator amounts to inserting the polynomial
P_{J}(s_{1}(n\bar{n}),-s_{2}(n\bar{n}))=(n\bar{n})^{J}P_{J}(s_{1},-s_{2})
into the integral over
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
.
Putting together the above relations and carying out the
\alpha-
integration in (
31
) we find

\displaystyle\langle{\phi(x_{1})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)\phi(x_{2})}\rangle=-{\pi\mathcal{N}_{J}\over 2x_{12}^{2\Delta-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,e^{-is_{1}(nx_{1})+is_{2}(nx_{2})}\delta(s_{1}-s_{2}-\hat{\omega})P_{J}(s_{1},-s_{2})\,,

(312)

where the delta function arises from the integration over
\alpha
in (
31
) and the factor of
1/r^{2}
in (
C
) cancels against the analogous factor in (
31
). Note that the dependence on the auxiliary null vector
\bar{n}
dropped out of this relation, as it should be. The dependence on
J
resides in the normalization factor and in the polynomial
(
310
).

To obtain the correlation (
306
) we have to Fourier transform (
312
) with respect to
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. This can be done using the following relation

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Delta}(q)=\int{d^{4}x\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{-i(qx)}\over(-x^{2}+i0x^{0})^{\Delta}}=\theta(q){2\pi^{3}(q^{2}/4)^{\Delta-2}\over\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma(\Delta-1)}\,,

(313)

where
\theta(q)\equiv\theta(q^{0})\theta(q^{2})
ensures that
\sigma_{\Delta}(q)\neq 0
only for time-like
q
’s. In this way we find

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)}\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle\equiv\int d^{4}x_{2}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{-iqx_{2}}\langle{\phi(0)\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)\phi(x_{2})}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{J}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,\delta(s_{1}-s_{2}-\hat{\omega})P_{J}(s_{1},-s_{2})\sigma_{\Delta-1}(q-ns_{2})\,,

(314)

where the proportionality factor
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{J}
does not depend on
\omega
and, therefore, it cancels in the ratio
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)}\rangle_{q}/\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\omega=0,n)}\rangle_{q}
. The expression for
\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\omega=0,n)}\rangle_{q}
was previously derived in
Belitsky:2013xxa
;
Belitsky:2013bja
.

The relation (
C
) has a simple interpretation in a weakly coupled CFT, as shown in Figure
5
. The operator
\phi(x_{2})
excites the vacuum and creates the state
\int d^{4}x_{2}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{-iqx_{2}}\phi(x_{2})
containing an arbitrary number of particles with the total momentum
q
. These particles propagate to the final state and are absorbed by the sink
\phi(x_{1})
. The flow operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)
plays the role of a detector located on the celestial sphere in the direction
n
. It selects a particle carrying momentum
ns_{2}
and transfers the light-like momentum
n\hat{\omega}
to it, so that the momentum of the outgoing particle is
ns_{1}=ns_{2}+n\hat{\omega}
. Thus, the integration variables
s_{i}
in (
C
) have the meaning of particle energies and
the polynomial
P_{J}(s_{1},-s_{2})
defines the transition amplitude. Finally,
\sigma_{\Delta-1}(q-ns_{2})
gives the probability for the remaining particles with total momentum
q-ns_{2}
to be absorbed by the sink.

Figure 5:
Diagrammatic interpretation of the one-point correlation (
C
). The grey blob represents the detector and the arrows indicate the propagation direction of the particles.

Changing the integration variable to
s_{2}=q^{2}/(2(qn))s
and replacing
\hat{\omega}=q^{2}/(2(qn))\omega
, we find after some algebra

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\hat{\omega},n)}\rangle_{q}\sim{(q^{2})^{J+\Delta-2}\over(qn)^{J+1}}\int^{1}_{\max(0,-\omega)}ds\,P_{J}(s+\omega,-s)(1-s)^{\Delta-3}\,.

(315)

Here the upper integration bound follows from the condition
(q-ns_{2})^{2}\geq 0

and the lower bound is due to the condition
s_{1}=s_{2}+\hat{\omega}\geq 0
. It is easy to check that this relation is in agreement with (
42
).
We find that up to a normalization factor the corresponding scaling function is given by

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{O}}(\omega,J)=c_{J}\int^{1}_{\max(0,-\omega)}ds\,P_{J}(s+\omega,-s)(1-s)^{\Delta-3}\,,

(316)

where the coefficient
c_{J}={(-1)^{J}\Gamma(J+\Delta-1)}(J!)^{2}/{\left({\Gamma(\Delta-2)(2J)!}\right)}
is chosen in such a way that
f_{\mathcal{O}}(0,J)=1
.
Notice that the lower integration bound depends on the sign of
\omega
. As we show in a moment, this makes the function
f_{\mathcal{O}}(\omega,J)
non-analytic around
\omega=0
.

Replacing the polynomial
P_{J}
in (
316
) with its explicit expression (
310
) we find that for the lowest values of the spin
J=0,1,2

the function
f_{\mathcal{O}}(\omega,J)
is given by (
58
) and (
59
).
It is straightforward to verify that these expressions satisfy the crossing symmetry relation (
49
) (upon replacing
\Delta_{\phi}=\Delta
,
\Delta_{O}=J+2
and
d=4
).

The relations (
58
) and (
59
) define the function
f_{\mathcal{O}}(\omega,J)
on the positive and negative semi-axes of
\omega
, respectively. Although the functions
(
58
) and (
59
) are continuous at the origin, their derivatives
f^{\prime}_{S}
,
f^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{Q}
and
f^{(5)}_{E}
have a jump at
\omega=0
.
For an arbitrary spin
J
, this happens for the derivative of order
2J+1
.

Fixing the one-point function from symmetries

Let us now ask if we can fix the result for the one-point function using symmetries. A similar discussion for the case
\hat{\omega}=0
can be found in Section 5.1.3 in
Kologlu:2019bco
. From the formulas above, it is clear that the conditions (
17
), (
18
), and (
19
) are not enough to fix the result. Indeed, consider the following ansatz for the one-point function

\displaystyle\langle\phi(X_{1}){\bf L}_{\hat{\omega}}[O](X_{3},Z_{3})\phi(X_{2})\rangle={1\over(-2X_{1}\cdot X_{3})^{{\Delta-J\over 2}}(-2X_{2}\cdot X_{3})^{{\Delta-J\over 2}}}{1\over(-2X_{1}\cdot X_{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}+{J-\Delta\over 2}}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,s_{1}^{\Delta-2}f\left({s_{2}\over s_{1}}\right)\delta(s_{1}-s_{2}-\hat{\omega})e^{{i\over 2}\Big(s_{2}{Z_{3}\cdot X_{2}\over X_{2}\cdot X_{3}}-s_{1}{Z_{3}\cdot X_{1}\over X_{1}\cdot X_{3}}\Big)},

(317)

where
f\left({s_{2}/s_{1}}\right)
is an unknown function. We note that (
C
) has the correct transformation properties for any
f\left({s_{2}/s_{1}}\right)
. Moreover, assuming that
f(1)\neq 0
, for
\hat{\omega}=0
it yields the known expression for the three-point function.

We can fix the function
f(s_{2}/s_{1})
in (
C
) by noting that the light-ray operator in (
C
) is polynomial
in the polarization vector
Z
. To implement this condition, we observe that a local operator satisfies the shortening condition

\displaystyle(w\cdot\partial_{z})^{J+1}O(x,z)=0,

(318)

where we introduced an extra polarization vector which is null and transverse to
z^{\mu}
, see section 3.1 in
Chang:2020qpj
for more details,

\displaystyle z^{2}=z\cdot w=w^{2}=0\,.

(319)

In writing (
318
) we assumed that
O(x,z)
is a local operator with spin
J
. The relation (
318
)
is just an economic way of saying that
O(x,z)
is a polynomial in the polarization tensor
z
of degree
J
. We can now pull
(w\cdot\partial_{z})^{J+1}
through the
\omega
-deformed light transform to get a shortening condition when acting on (
C
). This fixes the unknown function
f\left({s_{2}/s_{1}}\right)
.

Let us do this exercise explicitly for the simplest case
J=0
. In this case we get the relation

\displaystyle\Big((z\cdot\partial_{x})(w\cdot\partial_{z})-(z\cdot\partial_{z})(w\cdot\partial_{x})\Big){\bf L}_{\hat{\omega}}[O](x,z)=0,\qquad\text{(scalar $O$)},

(320)

which was also discussed in
Chang:2020qpj
. It leads to the following equation for the three-point function

\displaystyle\Big((z\cdot\partial_{x})(w\cdot\partial_{z})-(z\cdot\partial_{z})(w\cdot\partial_{x})\Big)\langle\phi(x_{1}){\bf L}_{\hat{\omega}}[O](x,z)\phi(x_{2})\rangle=0\,.

(321)

Plugging (
C
) into (
321
) we find

\displaystyle f(x)=c_{0}\,x^{{\Delta-2\over 2}},

(322)

which is indeed the correct expression. Analogous computations can be done for
J>0
as well.

Appendix D
Free scalar field at null infinity

Let us consider a free massless real scalar field in
d=4
dimensions. It can be represented in terms of creation and annihilation operators,

\displaystyle\varphi(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{d^{4}p\over(2\pi)^{3}}\delta_{+}(p^{2})(a_{p}e^{-i(px)}+a^{\dagger}_{p}e^{i(px)})\,,

(323)

where
\delta_{+}(p^{2})=\theta(p^{0})\delta(p^{2})
.

Following the discussion in Section
3.1
, we send the scalar field to null infinity by replacing
x=rn+\alpha\bar{n}
and taking the limit
r\to\infty

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}r\varphi(rn+\alpha\bar{n})=\lim_{r\to\infty}r\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{d^{4}p\over(2\pi)^{3}}\delta_{+}(p^{2})\Big[a_{p}e^{-ir(pn)-i\alpha(p\bar{n})}+a^{\dagger}_{p}e^{ir(pn)+i\alpha(p\bar{n})}\Big]\,.

(324)

It is convenient to apply Sudakov’s decomposition of the momenta

\displaystyle p=p_{+}n+p_{-}\bar{n}+p_{\perp}\ ,

(325)

where
n^{2}=\bar{n}^{2}=0
and
(p_{\perp}n)=(p_{\perp}\bar{n})=0
. Switching to the variables
(p_{+},p_{-},p_{\perp})
in (
324
) we get

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}r(n\bar{n})

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{dp_{+}dp_{-}d^{2}p_{\perp}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\theta(p_{+})\theta(p_{-})\delta(2p_{+}p_{-}(n\bar{n})-p_{\perp}^{2})

\displaystyle\times\Big[a_{p_{+},p_{-},p_{\perp}}e^{-i(n\bar{n})(p_{-}r+p_{+}\alpha)}+a^{\dagger}_{p_{+},p_{-},p_{\perp}}e^{i(n\bar{n})(p_{-}r+p_{+}\alpha)}\Big]\,.

(326)

Next we rescale
p_{-}\to{p_{-}/((n\bar{n})r)}
and take the limit
r\to\infty
to get

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{dp_{+}dp_{-}d^{2}p_{\perp}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\theta(p_{+})\theta(p_{-})\delta(p_{\perp}^{2})\left[a_{p_{+},0_{-}p_{\perp}}e^{-i(p_{-}+(n\bar{n})p_{+}\alpha)}+a^{\dagger}_{p_{+},0_{-},p_{\perp}}e^{i(p_{-}+(n\bar{n})p_{+}\alpha)}\right]\,.

(327)

Integrating over
p_{-}
and using the identity
\int d^{2}p_{\perp}\delta(p_{\perp}^{2})=\pi\int dp_{\perp}^{2}\delta(p_{\perp}^{2})=\pi
we arrive at

\displaystyle{\lim_{r\to\infty}r\varphi(rn+\alpha\bar{n})=i\int_{0}^{\infty}{dp_{+}\over 8\pi^{2}}\left[a^{\dagger}_{p_{+},0_{-},0_{\perp}}e^{i(n\bar{n})p_{+}\alpha}-a_{p_{+},0_{-},0_{\perp}}e^{-i(n\bar{n})p_{+}\alpha}\right]}\,.

(328)

Here the creation and annihilation operators are defined for the momentum
(p_{+},0_{-},0_{\perp})
that is aligned along the null vector
n
. Denoting this momentum by
sn^{\mu}
we can rewrite the relation (
328
) in a covariant form,

\displaystyle{\lim_{r\to\infty}r\varphi(rn+\alpha\bar{n})=i\int_{0}^{\infty}{ds\over 8\pi^{2}}\left[a^{\dagger}_{sn}e^{i(n\bar{n})s\alpha}-a_{sn}e^{-i(n\bar{n})s\alpha}\right]}\,.

(329)

The complex scalar field satisfies a similar relation with the only difference that the operator
a^{\dagger}_{sn}
is replaced by the annihilation operator
b^{\dagger}_{sn}
with
opposite charge. Then, substituting (
329
) into (
3.1
) and performing the integration over
\alpha
we obtain a representation of the scalar flow operator that coincides with the second line of (
3.1
).

Appendix E
Derivation of the SSC kernels

Here we present the details of the derivation of the scalar-scalar kernels, Eqs. (
4
) and (
4
), in Mellin space. The derivation is a direct generalization of the analysis in
Belitsky:2013xxa
. We start with the four-point correlation function (
139
), in which we retain only the interaction part. It is described by the function
\Phi(u,v)
which has the Mellin representation (
142
).
Using the symmetry properties of the Mellin amplitude
M(j_{1},j_{2})
and doing the crossing transformation
j_{1}\to-1-j_{1}-j_{2}
, we can recast (
142
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\cal C}_{0}}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}[\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(1-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})]^{2}M(j_{1},j_{2})u^{-1-j_{1}-j_{2}}v^{j_{2}}\,,

(330)

where the integration contour satisfies
{\rm Re}(j_{1})
,
{\rm Re}(j_{2})<0
and
{\rm Re}(j_{1}+j_{2})>-1
.

The relevant integral takes the form
34
34

34

The relation between (
330
) and the variables used in
Chang:2020qpj
is
\gamma_{12}=j_{1}+j_{2},~~~\gamma_{14}=1-j_{2}
.

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal C}_{0}}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}[\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(1-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})]^{2}M(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle\times{1\over(2\pi)^{4}}\int d^{4}x_{3}d^{4}x_{4}\,e^{i(\tilde{q}x_{4})-i(qx_{3})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}\,e^{-i\alpha_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}(n_{1}\bar{n}_{1})-i\alpha_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2}(n_{2}\bar{n}_{2})}

\displaystyle\times{1\over x_{12}^{4}x_{34}^{4}}\Big({x_{14}^{2}x_{23}^{2}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\Big)^{j_{2}-1}\Big({x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}\over x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\Big)^{2-j_{1}-j_{2}},

(331)

where we kept the ordering of the operators implicit. Next we set
x_{i}=r_{i}n_{i}+\alpha_{i}\bar{n}_{i}
(for
i=1,2
) at the detector points and take the limit
r_{i}\to\infty
according to (
31
). We also rescale
\alpha_{i}\to{\alpha_{i}/(n_{i}\bar{n}_{i})}
to get (skipping the first line of (
E
))

\displaystyle{1\over(2\pi)^{4}}\int d^{4}x_{3}d^{4}x_{4}e^{i(\tilde{q}x_{4})-i(qx_{3})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}e^{-i\alpha_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}-i\alpha_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2}}{2^{j_{1}+j_{2}-4}\over(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}x_{34}^{4}}

\displaystyle\times\left[{x_{34}^{2}(n_{1}n_{2})\over(\alpha_{1}-(x_{4}n_{1})-i\epsilon)((x_{3}n_{2})-\alpha_{2}-i\epsilon)}\right]^{1-j_{2}}\left[{x_{34}^{2}(n_{1}n_{2})\over(\alpha_{2}-(x_{4}n_{2})-i\epsilon)((x_{3}n_{1})-\alpha_{1}-i\epsilon)}\right]^{1-j_{1}}\,\,.

(332)

Here we introduced the
i\epsilon
prescription that captures the ordering of the operators according to (
137
). Note that the dependence on
\bar{n}_{i}
has dropped out, as expected. The identity

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\alpha{e^{-i\hat{\omega}\alpha}\over(\alpha-n\cdot x_{3}-i\epsilon)^{a}(n\cdot x_{4}-\alpha-i\epsilon)^{b}}

\displaystyle={i^{a+b}\over\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\ s_{1}^{a-1}s_{2}^{b-1}2\pi\delta(s_{1}-s_{2}+\hat{\omega})e^{is_{1}(nx_{3})-is_{2}(nx_{4})},

(333)

where
{\rm Re}(a)
,
{\rm Re}(b)>0
,
allows us to perform the integral over the detector times, or equivalently the light transform.

As a result we get the following expression

\displaystyle{1\over(2\pi)^{2}}\int d^{4}x_{3}d^{4}x_{4}\,e^{i(\tilde{q}x_{4})-i(qx_{3})+is_{1}(x_{3}n_{1})+is_{1}^{\prime}(x_{3}n_{2})-is_{2}(x_{4}n_{1})-is_{2}^{\prime}(x_{4}n_{2})}{2^{j_{1}+j_{2}-4}\over\Gamma(1-j_{2})^{2}\Gamma(1-j_{1})^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{1}^{\prime}ds_{2}ds_{2}^{\prime}\delta(s_{1}-s_{2}+\hat{\omega}_{1})\delta(s_{1}^{\prime}-s_{2}^{\prime}+\hat{\omega}_{2}){1\over(n_{1}n_{2})^{j_{1}+j_{2}}}{1\over(-x_{34}^{2}+i\epsilon x_{43}^{0})^{j_{1}+j_{2}}}\,.

(334)

One of the space-time integrations produces the momentum conserving delta function
(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(\tilde{q}-q-\hat{\omega}_{1}n_{1}-\hat{\omega}_{2}n_{2})
as in (
2.5
). The remaining integral over
x_{34}
can be done using the formula

\displaystyle\int d^{d}x{e^{-i(qx)}\over(-x^{2}+i\epsilon x^{0})^{\Delta}}=\theta(q){2\pi^{d/2+1}\over\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma(\Delta+1-{d\over 2})}{\left({q^{2}/4}\right)}^{\Delta-d/2}\,.

(335)

In this way we arrive at the desired formula for the scalar-scalar correlation

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}(\omega_{1},n_{1}){\cal O}(\omega_{2},n_{2})\rangle_{q}={1\over 8\pi^{2}}{\text{SSC}(z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\over(qn_{1})(qn_{2})},

(336)

where

\displaystyle\text{SSC}(z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\int_{{\cal C}_{0}}{dj_{2}dj_{1}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})M(j_{1},j_{2}),

(337)

and the kernel admits the following representation

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}

\displaystyle={(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)(qn_{1})(qn_{2})\over 2^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}(n_{1}n_{2})^{j_{1}+j_{2}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\theta(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1})\theta(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{2})\theta(q-n_{1}s_{1}-n_{2}s_{2})

\displaystyle\times\left[(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1})s_{2}\right]^{-j_{2}}\left[s_{1}(s_{2}+\hat{\omega}_{2})\right]^{-j_{1}}\left[(q-n_{1}s_{1}-n_{2}s_{2})^{2}\right]^{j_{1}+j_{2}-2}\,.

(338)

We rescale the integration variables
s_{i}\to{2(qn_{i})s_{i}/q^{2}}
and introduce the dimensionless variables (
43
) and the cross ratio (
51
) to get

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle={1\over 2}(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\theta(s_{1}+\omega_{1})\theta(s_{2}+\omega_{2})\theta(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+s_{1}s_{2}z)

\displaystyle\left[(s_{1}+\omega_{1})s_{2}\right]^{-j_{2}}\left[s_{1}(s_{2}+\omega_{2})\right]^{-j_{1}}\left(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+s_{1}s_{2}z\right)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-2},

(339)

As explained in the bulk of the paper, it is sufficient to consider two choices of the signs of
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
, namely
+-
and
++
. The two other choices are obtained from the relations (
2.6
). Here we restrict ourselves to the case
\omega_{1}>0
, so that the integration range of
s_{1}
in (
E
) is

\displaystyle 0\leq s_{1}\leq{1-s_{2}\over 1-s_{2}z}\,.

(340)

The result takes the following form

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})={\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\Gamma(1-j_{1})\over 2\Gamma(j_{2})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\omega_{1}^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int_{{\rm max}[0,-\omega_{2}]}^{1}ds_{2}s_{2}^{-j_{1}}(1-s_{2})^{j_{2}-1}(s_{2}+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}\Big(1+\omega_{1}-s_{2}(1+\omega_{1}z)\Big)^{j_{1}-1}\,.

(341)

Note that we first compute the kernel in the region where the integrals converge, namely
{\rm Re}(j_{1}+j_{2})>1
, and then define it in the region where we use the Mellin amplitude via analytic continuation.

Next we switch to the more convenient integration variable
s_{2}=(1+{1+\omega_{2}\over|\omega_{2}|}X)^{-1}
to get

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}

\displaystyle={\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\Gamma(1-j_{1})\over 2\Gamma(j_{2})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\omega_{1}^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}|\omega_{2}|^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{1})^{j_{1}-1}(1+\omega_{2})^{j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\infty}dX\,\theta(1+{\rm sign}(\omega_{2})X)X^{j_{2}-1}(1+{\rm sign}(\omega_{2})X)^{-j_{1}}\Big(X+{(1-z)\omega_{1}|\omega_{2}|\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{j_{1}-1}\,.

(342)

At this point we can in principle simply evaluate the last integral in terms of hypergeometric functions. The convergence of the integral requires first considering the region
{\rm Re}(j_{2})>0
and then analytically continuing to the region of interest,
{\rm Re}(j_{2})<0
.

To do this more explicitly it is convenient to perform the integration in a slightly different manner. We first represent the last factor on the right-hand side of (
E
) in Mellin form:

\displaystyle\Big(X+{(1-z)\omega_{1}|\omega_{2}|\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{j_{1}-1}=\int{ds\over 2\pi i}{\Gamma(1-j_{1}+s)\Gamma(-s)\over\Gamma(1-j_{1})}X^{j_{1}-1-s}\Big({(1-z)\omega_{1}|\omega_{2}|\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{s}\,.

(343)

After that we integrate over
X
,

\displaystyle\omega_{2}>0:

\displaystyle~~~\int_{0}^{\infty}dXX^{j_{1}+j_{2}-s-2}(1+X)^{-j_{1}}={\Gamma(1-j_{2}+s)\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2}-1-s)\over\Gamma(j_{1})},

\displaystyle\omega_{2}<0:

\displaystyle~~~\int_{0}^{1}dXX^{j_{1}+j_{2}-s-2}(1-X)^{-j_{1}}={\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2}-1-s)\over\Gamma(j_{2}-s)}\,.

(344)

Finally, we shift the Mellin variable
s\to j_{1}+j_{2}-1+s
to get

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}^{++}

\displaystyle={1\over 2}\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2})z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1-z)^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\int{ds\over 2\pi i}{\Gamma(-s)\Gamma(j_{2}+s)\Gamma(j_{1}+s)\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2}-s)\over\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1})}\Big({(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{s}\,.

(345)

This is precisely the formula (
4.2
) we quoted in the bulk of the paper. The integration contour in (
E
) is fixed as follows: we first consider a straight line for
{\rm Re}(j_{2})>0
that runs parallel to the imaginary axis and separates the poles generated by the product of gamma functions in the numerator, i.e. the poles coming from
\Gamma(\dots-s)
from the left and the poles coming from
\Gamma(\dots+s)
from the right. We then deform the contour to the region of interest
{\rm Re}(j_{2})<0
.

Let us also write down the explicit result of the integration

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}^{++}={\pi\over 2\sin\pi(j_{1}+j_{2})}(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1-z)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(j_{2},j_{1},j_{1}+j_{2},{(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle+{\Gamma(1-j_{2})\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)\over 2\Gamma(j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{1})^{j_{1}-1}(1+\omega_{2})^{j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(1-j_{2},1-j_{1},2-j_{1}-j_{2},{(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\right)\,.

(346)

In the case
\omega_{2}<0
we get instead

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}^{+-}

\displaystyle={\pi\over 2\sin\pi(j_{1}+j_{2})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1-z)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \times\int{ds\over 2\pi i}\Gamma(-s){\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(j_{2}+s)\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2}-s)\over\Gamma(1-j_{1}-s)\Gamma(j_{2})\Gamma(1-j_{1}-j_{2})}\Big(-{(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\Big)^{s},

(347)

which is the formula (
4.1
) quoted in the bulk of the paper. Doing the integral we get

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}^{+-}

\displaystyle={1\over 2}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1-z)^{j_{1}-1}(-\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{j_{2}-j_{1}}{\Gamma(1-j_{1})^{2}\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\over\Gamma(1+j_{2}-j_{1})}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(j_{2},1-j_{1},1+j_{2}-j_{1},{(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})\over(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}\right).

(348)

This form is not particularly convenient for taking the limit
\omega_{i}\to 0
. Using a standard identity for the hypergeometric functions we can instead write it as follows

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}^{+-}={\pi\over 2\sin\pi(j_{1}+j_{2})}(1+\omega_{1})^{-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1-z)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(j_{2},j_{1},j_{1}+j_{2},{(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle+{\Gamma(1-j_{1})^{2}\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)\over 2\Gamma(j_{2})^{2}}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}(-\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}(1+\omega_{1})^{j_{1}-1}(1+\omega_{2})^{j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\ _{2}F_{1}\left(1-j_{2},1-j_{1},2-j_{1}-j_{2},{(1-z)\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\right)\,.

(349)

Null limit of the kernel

Here we comment on the existence of a special simplifying limit in which the momentum of the sink becomes null. The representation (
4.2
) suggests to consider the limit

\displaystyle{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)\over 1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}=1\,,

(350)

which corresponds to

\displaystyle z=-{1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\over\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}\,\,.

(351)

Physically, this condition means that either the source or the sink carries null momentum,

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:specialkin}:~~~q^{2}=0~~\text{or}~~(q+\hat{\omega}_{1}n_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{2}n_{2})^{2}=0\,.

(352)

Keeping
q^{2}
timelike and requiring that
z
takes the physical values
0<z<1
yields the constraint
\omega_{i}<0
and
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}<-1
.

In this kinematics the Mellin integral in (
4.2
) simplifies dramatically and can be immediatelly computed using Barnes’ lemma with the following result

\displaystyle K^{\text{null}}_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\ \ ={\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\over 2(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\left(-{1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}\right)^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\Gamma(1-j_{1})\Gamma(1-j_{2})\Gamma(j_{1}+j_{2})\,,

(353)

where we have set
z=-{1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\over\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}
according to (
350
). Note that on this locus two-point generalized event shape becomes one-dimensional since the kernel depends nontrivially only on the combination
{z\over 1-z}=-{1+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\over(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})}
.

Appendix F
Computing
\text{SSC}_{++}
using correlation functions

Here we show the details of the computation of the correlation
\text{SSC}_{++}
in (
4.2
) and (
4.2
). The starting point is the following representation of the relevant Mellin kernel

\displaystyle K^{++}_{\text{SS}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=-{1\over 2}{j_{1}j_{2}\over j_{1}+j_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dX\int_{0}^{1}dt{(1-t)^{2}\over t^{2}z^{2}(1+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}X)}Y_{1}^{j_{2}-1}Y_{2}^{j_{1}-1}\,,

(354)

where

\displaystyle Y_{1}={(1-t)(1-z+(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})X)\over tz(1+\omega_{2})(1+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}X)}\geq 0\,,\qquad\quad Y_{2}={(1-t)(1+\omega_{2})X\over z}\geq 0\,.

(355)

The advantage of this representation is that for simple Mellin amplitudes like (
4
) the integrals over
j_{i}
are now very easy to take both at weak and at strong coupling. The basic integrals that we need are

\displaystyle\int_{-\epsilon-i\infty}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}{dj\over 2\pi i}Y^{j-1}=\delta(1-Y)-\delta(Y)\,,

\displaystyle\int_{-\epsilon-i\infty}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}{dj\over 2\pi i}{1\over j}Y^{j-1}=-{\theta(1-Y)\over Y_{+}}\,,

(356)

where
\epsilon>0
and
{\theta(1-Y)\over Y_{+}}
is the distribution that acts on test functions as
\int_{0}^{1}{dY\over Y}(f(Y)-f(0))
.

At weak coupling we get the distribution

\displaystyle\int{dj_{2}j_{1}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}\Big({1\over j_{2}}+{1\over j_{1}}\Big)Y_{1}^{j_{2}-1}Y_{2}^{j_{1}-1}

\displaystyle=-{\theta(1-Y_{1})\over(Y_{1})_{+}}\Big(\delta(1-Y_{2})-\delta(Y_{2})\Big)-{\theta(1-Y_{2})\over(Y_{2})_{+}}\Big(\delta(1-Y_{1})-\delta(Y_{1})\Big)\,.

(357)

We then act with this distribution on the test function

\displaystyle g(Y_{1},Y_{2})

\displaystyle=-{\partial(t,X)\over\partial(Y_{1},Y_{2})}\left.{(1-t)^{2}\over 2t^{2}z^{2}(1+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}X)}\right|_{t=t(Y_{1},Y_{2}),X=X(Y_{1},Y_{2})}

(358)

and obtain the result (
4.2
).

At strong coupling we get instead

\displaystyle\int_{-\epsilon-i\infty}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}{dj_{2}j_{1}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}(j_{1}+j_{2})(1+j_{1}+j_{2})Y_{1}^{j_{2}-1}Y_{2}^{j_{1}-1}

\displaystyle=(\hat{j}_{1}+\hat{j}_{2})(1+\hat{j}_{1}+\hat{j}_{2})\int_{-\epsilon-i\infty}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}{dj_{2}j_{1}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}Y_{1}^{j_{2}-1}Y_{2}^{j_{1}-1},

(359)

where
\hat{j}_{2}=Y_{1}\partial_{Y_{1}}+1
and
\hat{j}_{1}=Y_{2}\partial_{Y_{2}}+1
. Using (
F
) and (
F
) one can check that the result takes the form

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{++}^{\text{strong}}

\displaystyle={1\over 2}(\partial_{Y_{1}}^{2}g(1,0)+\partial_{Y_{2}}^{2}g(0,1)-\partial_{Y_{1}}^{2}g(1,1)-\partial_{Y_{2}}^{2}g(1,1)-2\partial_{Y_{1}}\partial_{Y_{2}}g(1,1))\,

(360)

yielding (
4.2
).

Appendix G
Bulk point singularity

According to (
4.2
), the expressions for the correlations at strong coupling with
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
of the same sign contain a new scale
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+z\omega_{1}\omega_{2}
. To elucidate its origin, we revisit the calculation of the SSC with the Mellin amplitude of the form (
175
).
For
p=0
and
p=1
it coincides with the expression at weak and strong coupling. For
p\geq 1

the corresponding expression for the four-point correlation function has a bulk singularity.

Applying (
E
) and (
337
), we change the integration variable as
s_{2}=1/(1+\omega_{1}s)
and obtain for
\omega_{1}>0

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{p}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\rm Re}\,j_{i}=-\delta}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}[j_{1}j_{2}(1+j_{1}+j_{2})]^{p}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\over j_{1}j_{2}}{\Gamma(-j_{2})\Gamma(1+j_{1}+j_{2})\over\Gamma(1+j_{1})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{s_{\rm max}}ds\,s^{j_{1}-1}(1+\omega_{2}(1+\omega_{1}s))^{-j_{2}}(s(1+\omega_{1})+(1-z))^{j_{2}-1}\,,

(361)

where
s_{\rm max}
is given by
\infty
and
-(1+\omega_{2})/(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})
for positive and negative
\omega_{2}
, respectively.
The integration over small
s
produces a pole
1/j_{1}
. For
p>1
it is compensated by the prefactor and, therefore, does not contribute. This implies that

\text{SSC}_{p}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=0
for negative
\omega_{2}
whereas for positive
\omega_{2}
it does not contains powers of
1/(1+\omega_{1}z)
and
1/(1+\omega_{2}z)
.

For
p\geq 2
we rewrite the previous relation as

\displaystyle\text{SSC}_{p}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}\partial_{2})^{p-1}(1+\partial_{1}+\partial_{2})]^{p}(\partial_{1}+\partial_{2})I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\Big|_{\epsilon_{i}=0}\,,

(362)

where
\partial_{i}=\partial_{\epsilon_{i}}
and the notation was introduced for

\displaystyle I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\rm Re}\,j_{i}=-\delta}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}{\Gamma(-j_{2})\Gamma(1+j_{1}+j_{2})\over\Gamma(1+j_{1})}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{1}j_{1}+\epsilon_{2}j_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{s_{\rm max}}ds\,s^{j_{1}-1}(1+\omega_{2}(1+\omega_{1}s))^{-j_{2}}(s(1+\omega_{1})+(1-z))^{j_{2}-1}\,.

(363)

Using the identity

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm Re}\,j_{i}=-\delta}{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}{\Gamma(-j_{2})\Gamma(1+j_{1}+j_{2})\over\Gamma(1+j_{1})}X_{1}^{j_{1}}X_{2}^{j_{2}}=\delta(1+X_{2}-X_{1})\,,

(364)

we obtain

\displaystyle I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s_{\rm max}}{ds\over s(s(1+\omega_{1})+1-z)}\delta{\left({1+{s(1+\omega_{1})+1-z\over z(1+\omega_{2}(1+\omega_{1}s))}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{1}}-{s\over z}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{2}}}\right)}\,.

(365)

The integral is localized on the solution of the equation

\displaystyle{1+{s_{\star}(1+\omega_{1})+1-z\over z(1+\omega_{2}(1+\omega_{1}s_{\star}))}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{1}}-{s_{\star}\over z}\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{2}}}=0

(366)

satisfying the condition
0\leq s_{\star}\leq s_{\rm max}
and it is given by

\displaystyle I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})

\displaystyle={z(1+\omega_{2}(1+\omega_{1}s_{\star}))\over s_{\star}(s_{\star}(1+\omega_{1})+1-z)(\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z-\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{1}}(1+\omega_{2}(1+2s_{\star}\omega_{1}))+\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{\epsilon_{2}}(1+\omega_{1}))}\,.

(367)

At small
\epsilon_{i}
we have

\displaystyle s_{\star}={1\over\omega_{2}}+z+O(\epsilon_{i})\,,\qquad I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=-{\omega_{2}^{2}z\over(1+\omega_{2}z)(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)}(1+O(\epsilon_{i}))\,.

(368)

Notice that
s_{\star}<0
for
-1<\omega_{2}<0
and, therefore,
I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})=0
in this case. For
\omega_{2}>0
we observe that
I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
contains two factors in the denominator. As mentioned above, the first one
(1+\omega_{2}z)
disappears in the
final expression for
\text{SSC}_{p}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
. The derivatives in (
362
) increase the power of
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z
in the denominator. The close examination shows that each power of
\epsilon_{i}
in the expansion of
I(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
is accompanied by a factor of

1/(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z)^{2}
. This leads
to (
177
). For
p=2
we obtain from (
362
) the relation (
179
) with the coefficient functions given by

\displaystyle c_{0}=2520\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right){}^{2},

\displaystyle c_{1}=1260\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)\left(5\omega_{1}+5\omega_{2}-4\right),

\displaystyle c_{2}=280\left(20\omega_{1}^{2}+40\omega_{2}\omega_{1}-43\omega_{1}+20\omega_{2}^{2}-43\omega_{2}+9\right),

\displaystyle c_{3}=140\left(15\omega_{1}^{2}+30\omega_{2}\omega_{1}-72\omega_{1}+15\omega_{2}^{2}-72\omega_{2}+41\right),

\displaystyle c_{4}=60\left(5\omega_{1}^{2}+10\omega_{2}\omega_{1}-58\omega_{1}+5\omega_{2}^{2}-58\omega_{2}+75\right),

\displaystyle c_{5}=10\left(\omega_{1}^{2}+2\omega_{2}\omega_{1}-44\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}-44\omega_{2}+141\right),

\displaystyle c_{6}=-4\left(3\omega_{1}+3\omega_{2}-38\right),

\displaystyle c_{7}=3\,.

(369)

Appendix H
Details of the derivation in Section
5

Summary of the SSC correlation

In this subsection we recall some of the key steps in obtaining the SSC correlation as an integrated correlation function.

It proves very efficient to go to new coordinates
x^{\mu}\to y^{\mu}
by performing a conformal transformation
Cornalba:2007zb
;
Hofman:2008ar
(see Section
2.2
),

\displaystyle y^{+}=-{1\over x^{+}}\,,\qquad y^{-}=x^{-}-{\vec{x}^{2}\over x^{+}}\,,\qquad\vec{y}={\vec{x}\over x^{+}}\,,\qquad dy^{2}={dx^{2}\over(x^{+})^{2}}\,,

(370)

where the notation was introduced for the light-like coordinates
x^{\pm}
and
\vec{x}=(x,\bar{x})
,

\displaystyle x_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=x_{\mu}(\sigma^{\mu})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\bigg[\begin{array}[]{ll}\ x^{+}&\bar{x}\\
\ x&x^{-}\end{array}\bigg]\,,\qquad dx^{2}=dx^{+}dx^{-}-dx\,d\bar{x}\,.

Then sending the detectors at points 1 and 2 to null infinity is equivalent to setting
y^{+}_{1,2}=0
. The calculations are greatly simplified by using conformal invariance to ‘gauge away’ some of the coordinates at the source points 3 and 4. So, we work in the special frame

\displaystyle y^{+}_{1}=y^{+}_{2}=0\,,\qquad y^{+}_{3}=1\,,\qquad\vec{y}_{3}=\vec{y}_{4}=y_{4}^{-}=0\,,\qquad y_{4}^{+}\to\infty\,.

(373)

At the end the covariant form can be easily restored by undoing the gauges and switching back to the coordinates
x_{3,4}
.

The next step is the Fourier transform from the detector times to frequencies,
y^{-}_{1,2}\to\hat{\omega}_{1,2}
. Applied to the correlation function
G_{00}
in (
181
), this procedure results in

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{00}(y;n_{i},\hat{\omega}_{i}){}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dy_{1}^{-}dy_{2}^{-}e^{-i(y^{-}_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}+y^{-}_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2})}\langle{\phi^{\dagger}(y_{4})O(0^{+},y_{1}^{-},\vec{y}_{1})O(0^{+},y_{2}^{-},\vec{y}_{2})\phi(y_{3})}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dy_{1}^{-}dy_{2}^{-}e^{-i(y^{-}_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}+y^{-}_{2}\hat{\omega}_{2})}\,F(y)\,,

(374)

where the function
F
was defined in (
184
). After integrating over
y_{1}^{-},y_{2}^{-}
we restore the covariant notation and obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{00}(x;\gamma,\Omega_{i})={1\over x^{2}}{\cal G}(\gamma,\Omega_{i})\,,

(375)

where
x\equiv x_{34}
. The function
\mathcal{G}
generalizes that in (
191
). It depends on the dimensionless variables

\displaystyle\gamma={2(xn_{1})(xn_{2})\over x^{2}(n_{1}n_{2})}\,,\qquad\Omega_{i}=\hat{\omega}_{i}(xn_{i})\,.

(376)

The Mellin kernel for the dimensionless function
{\cal G}(\gamma,\Omega_{i})
in the coordinate representation is given by the double Schwinger integral (see Appendix
E
)

\displaystyle K_{{\cal G}}(j_{i}|x)=(-\gamma)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dS_{1}dS_{2}\,e^{-i\left(S_{1}+S_{2}\right)}\left({S_{2}\left(S_{1}+\Omega_{1}\right)}\right)_{+}^{-j_{2}}\left({S_{1}\left(S_{2}+\Omega_{2}\right)}\right)_{+}^{-j_{1}}\,,

(377)

where the parameters
S_{i}=s_{i}(xn_{i})
are dimensionless (see (
376
)). The
S-
factors are understood as the
+
distribution
1/S_{+}^{j}
and
1/(S+\Omega)_{+}^{j}
. This makes differentiating with respect to
S
or
\Omega
safe, as long as
j\neq-1,-2,\ldots\
.

After the Fourier transform to momentum space
x\to q
we get (see (
E
))

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}(j_{i}|q)=((n_{1}n_{2})/2)^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\,{\mathbb{I}}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\,\Big\{\sigma_{j_{1}+j_{2}}(q-n_{1}s_{1}-n_{2}s_{2})\Big\}\,,

(378)

where

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\big\{\sigma\big\}=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,\left(s_{2}\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)\right)_{+}^{-j_{2}}\left(s_{1}\left(s_{2}+\hat{\omega}_{2}\right)\right)_{+}^{-j_{1}}\,\sigma\,,

(379)

\displaystyle\sigma_{j}(\hat{q})=\int{d^{4}x\mathop{\rm e}\nolimits^{-i\hat{q}x}\over(-x^{2}+i0x^{0})^{j}}=C_{j}\,\theta(\hat{q}^{0})\theta(\hat{q}^{2})(\hat{q}^{2})^{j-2}\,,\quad\hat{q}=q-n_{1}s_{1}-n_{2}s_{2}

(380)

and
C_{j}={\pi^{3}/(2^{2j-3}\Gamma(j)\Gamma(j-1))}
.
The variables
s_{i}
have the same dimension and Lorentz charge as the frequencies
\hat{\omega}_{i}
.
It is convenient to introduce new dimensionless variables
z
and
\omega_{i}
(cf. (
51
) and (
43
)), as well as to rescale the integration variables
s_{i}\to{(q^{2}/2(qn_{i})})s_{i}
. After this the scalar kernel becomes

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}=C_{j_{1}+j_{2}}\,z^{1-j_{1}-j_{2}}\,\mathbb{I}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\Big\{\theta(q^{0}-n^{0}_{i}s_{i})\,\theta(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})\,(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})^{j_{1}+j_{2}-2}\Big\},

(381)

where the integral operator
\mathbb{I}_{j_{1},j_{2}}
is defined in (
379
) but now with
\hat{\omega}\to\omega
.
In the rest frame
q^{\mu}=(q^{0},\vec{0})
with
q^{0}>0
and
n^{\mu}_{i}=(1,\vec{n}_{i})
with
\vec{n}_{i}^{2}=1
we have
q^{2}=(q^{0})^{2}
,
(qn_{i})=q^{0}
. The condition
\hat{q}^{0}\geq 0
becomes
2-s_{1}-s_{2}\geq 0
, thus reducing the integration domain to
0\leq s_{i}\leq 1
. The conditions
s_{i}+\omega_{i}\geq 0
restrict the domain further, if one or both
\omega_{i}<0
. In this section most of the time we will consider the simplest case
\omega_{i}\geq 0
and will comment on the choice
\omega_{1}<0,\ \omega_{2}>0
in Section
H
. The last condition
\hat{q}^{2}\geq 0\ \Rightarrow\ 1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2}\geq 0
can be resolved by the following change of variables:

\displaystyle s_{1}\to u\,,\qquad s_{2}\to\frac{(1-u)v}{1-zu}\,;\qquad u\in[\max(0,-\omega_{1}),1]\,,\quad v\in[0,1]\,.

(382)

Relations QSC/SSC and ESC/SSC

In this subsection we work out the simplest example QSC/SSC in detail and explain the main steps. The principal difference compared to the standard case is that instead of integrating over the detector time
x^{-}
, we now Fourier transform it into the energy
\hat{\omega}
. Thus, if earlier we could neglect the total time derivative
\partial_{x^{-}}
contained in the operator (
187
), now it contributes to the Fourier transform terms
\sim\hat{\omega}
. This calls for a more efficient formalism.

Spinor formalism

We introduce the following notation for the
\lambda
-projections consistent with their Lorenty charges. The auxiliary spinors
\lambda_{\pm}\equiv\langle{\pm}|
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{\pm}\equiv[\pm|
satisfy completeness conditions

\displaystyle|{+}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle{-}|_{\beta}-|{-}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle{+}|_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\ \Rightarrow\ \langle{+}|^{\alpha}|{-}\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\langle{+|-}\rangle=1\,,

\displaystyle|+]^{\dot{\alpha}}[-|_{\dot{\beta}}-|-]^{\dot{\alpha}}[+|_{\dot{\beta}}=\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\dot{\beta}}\ \Rightarrow\ [+|^{\dot{\alpha}}|-]_{\dot{\alpha}}\equiv[+|-]=1\,.

(383)

The four-vectors have the projections

\displaystyle y^{+}=\langle{-}|^{\alpha}y_{\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}}|-]^{\dot{\alpha}}=[-|_{\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{y}^{{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}|{-}\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\langle{-}|y|-]=[-|\tilde{y}|{-}\rangle\,,\

\displaystyle y^{-}=\langle{+}|y|+]=[+|\tilde{y}|{+}\rangle\,,\qquad y=\langle{+}|y|-]=[-|\tilde{y}|{+}\rangle\,,\qquad\bar{y}=\langle{-}|y|+]=[+|\tilde{y}|{-}\rangle\,.

(384)

In terms of projected variables and in the frame (
373
) the matrices (
5.1
) become

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{1[234]}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle&&\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{+}\rangle\\
\langle{+}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle&&\langle{+}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{+}\rangle\\
\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\bar{y}_{12}(y_{2}\bar{y}_{1}-y^{-}_{3})&&&y_{2}\bar{y}_{12}y^{-}_{1}\\
y_{2}(\bar{y}_{1}y^{-}_{13}+\bar{y}_{2}y^{-}_{3})-y_{1}(\bar{y}_{1}y^{-}_{23}+\bar{y}_{2}y^{-}_{3})+y^{-}_{21}y^{-}_{3}&&&y^{-}_{1}(y_{2}y^{-}_{13}+y_{1}y^{-}_{32})\\
\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle{{\cal X}}_{[12]3}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bar{y}_{12}&&-y_{2}\bar{y}_{1}-y^{-}_{23}\\
y_{1}\bar{y}_{2}+y^{-}_{13}&&y_{1}y^{-}_{23}+y_{2}y^{-}_{31}\\
\end{array}\right)\,,\qquad{\cal X}_{[12]4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bar{y}_{12}&&-y^{-}_{2}\\
y^{-}_{1}&&0\\
\end{array}\right)\,.

The operator (
187
) is decomposed into two projections:

\displaystyle{\cal D}=\tilde{\lambda}_{-{\dot{\alpha}}}\tilde{\partial}^{{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}_{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{-}^{\alpha}}=\partial_{y^{-}}\partial_{+}+\partial_{\bar{y}}\partial_{0}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{2}=\partial_{\bar{y}}^{2}\partial_{0}(\partial_{0}-1)+2\partial_{y^{-}}\partial_{\bar{y}}\partial_{+}(\partial_{0}-1)+\partial_{y^{-}}^{2}\partial_{+}^{2}\,,

(393)

where the projected spinor derivatives act as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{0}|{\pm}\rangle=\mp|{\pm}\rangle\,,\quad\partial_{+}|{-}\rangle=|{+}\rangle\,,\quad\partial_{+}|{+}\rangle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{0}y^{\pm}=\pm y^{\pm}\,,\quad\partial_{0}\bar{y}=\bar{y}\,,\quad\partial_{0}y=-y\,,\quad\partial_{+}y^{+}=y\,,\quad\partial_{+}\bar{y}=y^{-}\,,\quad\partial_{+}y^{-}=\partial_{+}y=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{y^{-}}\equiv[-|\tilde{\partial}_{y}|{-}\rangle\ \Rightarrow\ \partial_{y^{-}}y^{-}=1\,,\quad\partial_{\bar{y}}\equiv-[-|\tilde{\partial}_{y}|{+}\rangle\ \Rightarrow\ \partial_{\bar{y}}\bar{y}=1\,,\ \text{otherwise 0}\,.

(394)

These rules allow us to expand out the differential operators in (
186
) and are easily automatized. Below we show in detail how this is done for the simplest relation QSC/SSC.

QSC differential operator in coordinate space

The QSC correlation is obtained from the relations (
186
)–(
5.1
) and is given by

\displaystyle\text{QSC}=(\partial_{y^{-}_{1}}\partial_{+}+\partial_{\bar{y}_{1}}\partial_{0})\left[\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle{{\cal G}(\gamma,\Omega_{i})\over(y_{12}\bar{y}_{12}y^{-}_{3})^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\partial_{y^{-}_{1}}\left[\Big(\langle{+}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle+\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{+}\rangle\Big){{\cal G}\over(y_{12}\bar{y}_{12}y^{-}_{3})^{2}}\right]+\partial_{\bar{y}_{1}}\left[2\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle{{\cal G}\over(y_{12}\bar{y}_{12}y^{-}_{3})^{2}}\right]\,,

(395)

where (see (
376
))

\displaystyle\Omega_{i}=(y^{-}_{3}-y_{i}\bar{y}_{i})\hat{\omega}_{i}\,,\qquad\gamma=-{(y_{3}^{-}-y_{1}\bar{y}_{1})(y_{3}^{-}-y_{2}\bar{y}_{2})\over y_{12}\bar{y}_{12}y_{3}^{-}}\,.

(396)

The next step is the Fourier transform
y^{-}\to\hat{\omega}
(recall (
H
)). The first term in (
H
) with the total detector time derivative
\partial_{y^{-}_{1}}
gives rise to terms proportional to
\Omega_{1}
. This is in contrast with the standard case (i.e.
\hat{\omega}_{i}=0
) where the time derivatives were suppressed by the time integral. We distribute the derivatives in (
H
) and restore the covariant form to obtain the generalization of the first relation in (
191
),

\displaystyle\text{QSC}(x)=\frac{(xn_{2})}{(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}x^{4}}\left[\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2\gamma}\Big((\gamma-2){\cal G}_{\Omega_{1}}-\gamma{\cal G}_{\Omega_{2}}+i{\cal G}\Big)+(\gamma-1){\cal G}_{\gamma}+{\cal G}\right]\,.

(397)

On the other hand, we have the identity

\displaystyle\frac{(n_{2}\partial_{x})}{2(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}}\left[\frac{{\cal G}(\gamma,\Omega)}{x^{2}}\right]=\frac{(xn_{2})}{(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}x^{4}}\,\left[(1-\gamma){\cal G}_{\gamma}+\frac{\Omega_{1}}{\gamma}{\cal G}_{\Omega_{1}}-{\cal G}\right]\,.

(398)

Recalling that
\text{SSC}(x)={\cal G}(x)/x^{2}
(see (
375
)), we can combine (
397
) and (
398
) into

\displaystyle\text{QSC}(x)=-\frac{1}{(n_{1}n_{2})}\,(n_{2}\partial_{x})\text{SSC}(x)+\frac{(xn_{2})}{(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}x^{4}}\,\Omega_{1}\left[{\cal G}_{\Omega_{1}}-{\cal G}_{\Omega_{2}}+\frac{i}{\gamma}{\cal G}\right]\,.

(399)

QSC differential operator in momentum space

Our final goal is to express the QSC kernel as a differential operator in momentum space acting on the SSC kernel. We start by inserting the Mellin kernel
K_{{\cal G}}(x)
defined in (
377
) into the right-hand side of (
397
) and then we switch back to the dimensionfull variables
s_{i}
and
\hat{\omega}_{i}
:

\displaystyle K_{\text{QS}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}}(-x^{2}(n_{1}n_{2})/2)^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}{\mathbb{I}}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\,\Bigg\{e^{-is_{k}(xn_{k})}\Bigg[\frac{i\hat{\omega}_{1}}{2}+\left(1-j_{1}-\frac{j_{2}s_{1}}{s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}}\right)\frac{1}{(xn_{1})}

\displaystyle+\frac{j_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}}{(n_{1}n_{2})x^{2}(s_{2}+\hat{\omega}_{2})}(xn_{1})+\frac{2j_{1}\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)+j_{2}\left(2s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)}{(n_{1}n_{2})x^{2}\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)}(xn_{2})\Bigg]\Bigg\}\,.

(400)

When Fourier transforming the right-hand side of (
H
),
K_{\text{QS}}=\int d^{4}xe^{-ixq}\,K_{\text{QS}}(x)
,
we encounter the basic Fourier integral (
380
) and two new ones:

\displaystyle\int d^{4}xe^{-ix\hat{q}}\,(xn_{k})(-x^{2})^{-j-1}=-i\partial_{s_{k}}\sigma_{j+1}=\frac{i}{j}[(qn_{k})-(n_{k}n_{i})s_{i}]\,\sigma_{j}\,,

(401)

\displaystyle\int d^{4}xe^{-ix\hat{q}}\,\frac{(-x^{2})^{-j}}{(xn_{1})}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-i\partial/\partial{s_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\sigma_{j}\,.

(402)

We use the relation (
402
) in (
H
) by pulling out a total derivative
\partial_{s_{1}}
from the pole
1/(xn_{1})
,

\displaystyle\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)^{-j_{2}}s_{1}^{-j_{1}}\left(1-j_{1}-\frac{j_{2}s_{1}}{s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}}\right)=\partial_{s_{1}}\Big[\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)^{-j_{2}}s_{1}^{-j_{1}}\Big]

(403)

and integrating it by parts.
35
35

35

The boundary terms vanish under suitable conditions on the parameters
j_{i}
.
Thus, all the terms in (
H
) are reduced to the basic Fourier integral (
380
). Switching over to the dimensionless variables
s_{i}\to{(q^{2}/2(qn_{i})})s_{i}
, we obtain the following Schwinger integral with the measure defined in (
381
):

\displaystyle K_{\text{QS}}=

\displaystyle\frac{i(qn_{2})}{2(n_{1}n_{2})\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)}\mathbb{I}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\Bigg\{\Bigg[2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{2}z\omega_{1}\right)-\frac{j_{2}\omega_{1}\left(z\omega_{1}+1\right)}{s_{1}+\omega_{1}}+\frac{j_{1}\omega_{1}\left(z\omega_{2}+1\right)}{s_{2}+\omega_{2}}\Bigg]\sigma_{j_{1}+j_{2}}\Bigg\}\,.

(404)

The terms with poles
1/(s_{i}+\omega_{i})
can be interpreted as derivatives of
K_{\text{SS}}(q)\sim\mathbb{I}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\{\sigma_{j_{1}+j_{2}}\}
with respect to
\omega_{i}
. In this way we arrive at the final result

\displaystyle K_{\text{QS}}=\frac{i(qn_{2})}{(n_{1}n_{2})}\Bigg\{1+\frac{\omega_{1}}{2\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)}\Big[2j_{2}z+(1+z\omega_{1})\partial_{\omega_{1}}-(1+z\omega_{2})\partial_{\omega_{2}}\Big]\Bigg\}K_{\text{SS}}\,.

(405)

This is our first example of a differential operator (
193
) relating two correlations.
We remark that Eq. (
405
) contains the kinematic prefactor
i(qn_{2})/(n_{1}n_{2})
which has been stripped off the kernels in (
190
), (
192
) and (
193
). Setting
\omega_{i}=0
reproduces the standard relation (
192
).

ESC differential operator

The case ESC/SSC is treated in a similar way. The analog of (
H
), after doing the spinor variables algebra is (recall (
H
)–(
H
))

\displaystyle\text{ESC}=12\partial_{\bar{z}_{1}}^{2}\left[\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle^{2}\,G\right]+12\partial_{z_{1}^{-}}\partial_{\bar{z}_{1}}\left[\Big(\langle{+}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle+\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{+}\rangle\Big)\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle\,G\right]

\displaystyle+2\partial_{z_{1}^{-}}^{2}\left[\Bigg(2\langle{+}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{+}\rangle\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle+\Big(\langle{+}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{-}\rangle+\langle{-}|{\cal X}_{1[234]}|{+}\rangle\Big)^{2}\Bigg)\,G\right],

(406)

with
G={\cal G}/(z_{12}\bar{z}_{12}z^{-}_{3})^{3}
. After distributing the derivatives and restoring the covariant notation, the analog of (
H
) contains a pole
1/(xn_{1})
(the measure
\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{j_{1},j_{2}}
is not shown):

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\omega}_{1}}{4(xn_{1})}\left[\frac{j_{2}(xn_{2})\left(\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)s_{1}+\left(j_{1}-1\right)\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)}{zx^{2}\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right){}^{2}}-\frac{i\left(\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)s_{1}+\left(j_{1}-1\right)\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)}{\left(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int ds_{1}\,\partial_{s_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad-\frac{1}{4}s_{1}\hat{\omega}_{1}\left(1+\frac{ij_{2}(xn_{2})}{zx^{2}(s_{1}+\hat{\omega}_{1})}\right)\,.

(407)

Here we pulled
\partial_{s_{1}}
out and integrated it by parts (recall (
403
)). The remaining terms are polynomial of the type
(xn_{1})^{p}(xn_{2})^{q}
. Their Fourier transforms are done by repeated use of the relations (
380
), (
401
) and (
402
). The sum of all the terms contains poles in
s_{i}+\omega_{i}
(but no positive powers!) which are converted to derivatives with respect to
\omega_{i}
. The result for the coefficients of the differential operator (
193
) is shown in (
196
).
Setting
\omega_{i}=0
we recover the standard relation (
192
).

Remarks

We conclude this subsection by a couple of remarks. The first term in (
399
) is a total
x-
space derivative with a simple Fourier transform, so we might have saved some work by using the shorter from (
399
) of the relation (
397
). However, the Fourier transform of the total derivative term is not so obvious in the cases QQC, EQC and EEC, as we explain in the next subsection.

A related issue is a key feature of the right-hand side of (
H
), namely the absence of positive powers of
s_{i}+\omega_{i}
. They would have been an obstruction for the interpretation of the right-hand side as a differential operator, as we did for example in (
405
). This property is automatic in the cases QSC and ESC. However, in the cases QQC, EQC and EEC positive powers do appear, already in the total derivative term written as a Schwinger integral. Since we know the expected result of its Fourier transform, we can derive highly non-trivial identities relating the obstruction terms to simpler ones. It turns out that these identities suffice for eliminating the obstruction terms also in the main (non-derivative) part. In this way we derive a differential operator for the relation to the SSC kernel. We illustrate this phenomenon in the next subsection on the simplest case QQC.

Relation QQC/SSC

Differential operator in coordinate space

The QQC correlation is obtained from (
186
)–(
5.1
) (see (
H
) and (
396
)):

\displaystyle\text{QQC}=(\partial_{y^{-}_{1}}\partial_{1_{+}}+\partial_{\bar{y}_{1}}\partial_{1_{0}})(\partial_{y^{-}_{2}}\partial_{2_{+}}+\partial_{\bar{y}_{2}}\partial_{2_{0}})\left[\langle{1_{-}}|{{\cal X}}_{[12]3}|{2_{-}}\rangle\langle{1_{-}}|{{\cal X}}_{[12]4}|{2_{-}}\rangle{{\cal G}(\gamma,\Omega_{i})\over(y_{12}\bar{y}_{12}y^{-}_{3})^{2}}\right]\,.

(408)

Notice that the spinor derivatives
\partial_{+},\partial_{0}
act separately at points 1 and 2. Doing the algebra and restoring the covariant form we obtain a differential operator that consists of two parts:

(i) total derivative applied to a function of
\gamma
and
\Omega
(the generalization of (
191
)):

\displaystyle\text{QQC}_{\rm tot.der.}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}}\Box_{x}\,\left(\frac{{\cal G}(\gamma,\Omega)}{x^{2}}\right)={1\over(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}x^{4}}\Big\{\partial_{\gamma}[(1-\gamma)\gamma\partial_{\gamma}]

\displaystyle-\left((\gamma-1)\partial_{\gamma}+1\right)\Omega_{1}\partial_{\Omega_{1}}-\left((\gamma-1)\partial_{\gamma}+1\right)\Omega_{2}\partial_{\Omega_{2}}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\Omega_{1}\Omega_{2}\partial_{\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}}\Big\}{{\cal G}}\,;

(409)

(ii) deviation from the total derivative by terms proportional to
\Omega
:

\displaystyle\text{QQC}_{\rm dev.}

\displaystyle={1\over(n_{1}n_{2})^{2}x^{4}}\,{i\Omega_{1}\over 4\gamma}

\displaystyle\times\left\{\frac{i}{2}\Omega_{2}+2\gamma+2\gamma\left(\gamma-1\right)\partial_{\gamma}+\left(\gamma-2\right)\Omega_{2}\partial_{\Omega_{1}}+i\gamma\Omega_{2}\partial_{\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}}\right\}{{\cal G}}+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,.

(410)

Differential operator in momentum space. Total derivative identity

The deviation term (
H
) is treated as in Section
H
. The analog of (
H
) contains no positive powers of
s_{i}+\omega_{i}
, so it can be turned into a differential operator. The latter is simplified with the help of the differential equation for the scalar kernel (
5.2
) with the result shown in (
5.2
).

The total derivative term (
H
) requires special care. Although its Fourier transform is expected to be very simple, this is by no means obvious. Indeed, in order to obtain this Fourier transform we need to prove the following highly non-trivial identity:

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{j_{1},j_{2}}

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\Bigg[j_{1}\left(z\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}+2\right)-\omega_{1}-1-\frac{j_{2}\omega_{1}\left(z\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+1\right)}{s_{1}+\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle+(s_{2}+\omega_{2})\Bigg(\frac{j_{2}\omega_{1}\left(z\omega_{1}+1\right)}{s_{1}+\omega_{1}}-\left(zj_{2}\omega_{1}+j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)\Bigg)\Bigg]\sigma_{j_{1}+j_{2}}+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\Bigg\}=0\,.

(411)

Notice the presence of positive power of
s_{i}+\omega_{i}
in the second line. This is an example of ‘obstruction’ terms being replaced by terms of the differential operator type.

The proof of (
H
) makes use of the variables (
382
) spanning the interval
u,v\in[0,1]
if
\omega_{i}\geq 0
. We can rewrite the integrand in (
H
) is a sum of total derivatives:
36
36

36

ES is grateful to Dima Chicherin for a discussion on this point.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dudv\ \Bigg\{\partial_{v}\Big[v^{1-j_{2}}(1-v)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}(\omega_{2}(1-uz)+v(1-u))^{-j_{1}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times u^{-j_{1}}(1-u)^{j_{1}-1}(1-zu)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-2}\left(zu^{2}-2zu+1\right)(\omega_{1}+u)^{-j_{2}}

(412)

\displaystyle+\partial_{u}\Big[u^{1-j_{1}}(1-u)^{j_{1}}(1-zu)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}(\omega_{1}+u)^{-j_{2}}(\omega_{2}(1-uz)+v(1-u))^{-j_{1}}\Big]v^{-j_{2}}(1-v)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}\Bigg\}\,.

The boundary terms in the two integrals vanish if
0<{\rm Re}(j_{1})<1
,
{\rm Re}(j_{2})<1
and
{\rm Re}(j_{1}+j_{2})>1
.
If
\omega_{1}<0
and
\omega_{2}\geq 0
, the variable
u
is in the interval
u\in[-\omega_{1},1]
. In this case the factor
(\omega_{1}+u)^{-j_{2}}
in (
412
) vanishes at the boundary
u=-\omega_{1}
provided that
{\rm Re}(j_{2})<0
. The application domain of this identity can be extended by analytic continuation in
j_{1},j_{2}
.

Relations EQC/SSC and EEC/SSC

The new feature in the cases EQC/SSC and EEC/SSC is that obstruction terms with positive powers of
s_{i}+\omega_{i}
appear not only in the Fourier transform of the total derivative part but also in the deviation terms. Requiring that the Fourier transforms of the total derivative terms equal the expressions originating from (
191
), we find two new identities of the type (
H
). More identities are generated by differentiating the existing ones with respect to
\omega_{i}
, thus lowering the powers of
s_{i}+\omega_{i}
. For example, the derivative
\partial_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}
of (
H
) removes all the positive powers, resulting in a differential equation for
K_{\text{SS}}(q)
. We find altogether 11 linearly independent identities. Adding the appropriate linear combinations of identities to the deviation terms, we succeed in removing all the obstruction terms. The resulting differential operators for EQC and EEC are listed in the ancillary file.

An interesting byproduct of the procedure is a particular linear combination of identities that amounts to a simple homogeneous partial differential equation for the scalar kernel
K_{\text{SS}}
, see (
5.2
).
It arises as a consistency condition for the Fourier transform of the Schwinger integral in (
377
) and its space-time derivatives.

Equation (
5.2
) can be solved by separation of variables. For example, we can substitute

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{i})=({\omega_{2}}+1)^{j_{2}-j_{1}}({\omega_{1}}{\omega_{2}})^{-j_{2}}F\left(X\right)\,,\qquad X=\frac{z{\omega_{1}}{\omega_{2}}+{\omega_{1}}+{\omega_{2}}+1}{{\omega_{1}}{\omega_{2}}(z-1)}\,.

(413)

We obtain an ordinary differential equation for the function
F(X)
:

\displaystyle(1-X)XF^{\prime\prime}(X)+\left((j_{1}-j_{2}-2)X+1\right)F^{\prime}(X)+\left(j_{1}-1\right)j_{2}F(X)=0\,.

(414)

The relevant solution

\displaystyle F(X)=c_{1}\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left(1-j_{1},j_{2};1;X\right)

(415)

is selected by the requirement that it be regular at
X=0
. This boundary condition amounts to setting
z{\omega_{1}}{\omega_{2}}+{\omega_{1}}+{\omega_{2}}+1=0
in the integral (
381
). The constant
c_{1}
is fixed by taking the limit
\omega_{i}\to 0
and comparing with the value of the integral (
378
).

An alternative substitution is

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{i})=({\omega_{1}}+1)^{j_{1}-j_{2}}(-{\omega_{1}}{\omega_{2}})^{-j_{1}}G(Y)\,,\qquad Y=\frac{({\omega_{1}}+1)({\omega_{2}}+1)}{{\omega_{1}}{\omega_{2}}(1-z)}\,,

(416)

yielding the differential equation

\displaystyle(Y-1)YG^{\prime\prime}(Y)+\left((j_{1}-j_{2}+2)Y-j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)G^{\prime}(Y)-j_{1}\left(j_{2}-1\right)G(Y)=0\ \Rightarrow

\displaystyle G(Y)=c_{2}\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left(j_{1},1-j_{2};1+j_{1}-j_{2};Y\right)\,.

(417)

Again, the relevant solution is selected by the boundary condition of regularity at
Y=0
. This condition amounts to setting
\omega_{1}=-1
. Both the solution and the integral (
381
) vanish at this point. The integration constant
c_{2}
can be fixed as before.

We remark that using well-known identities, these solutions can be rewritten in an alternative form involving two hypergeometric functions each. For example, the solution (
413
), (
415
) becomes (cf. (
E
))

\displaystyle K_{\text{SS}}(z,\omega_{i})=

\displaystyle(1-z)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\frac{\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{-j_{2}}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{-j_{1}}\Gamma\left(1-j_{1}-j_{2}\right)}{[\Gamma\left(1-j_{1}\right)\Gamma\left(1-j_{2}\right)]^{2}\Gamma\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\,_{2}F_{1}\left(j_{1},j_{2};j_{1}+j_{2};\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)}{({\omega_{1}}+1)({\omega_{2}}+1)}\right)

\displaystyle+{}

\displaystyle{}z^{-j_{1}-j_{2}}\frac{(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})^{-j_{1}-j_{2}+1}\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)^{j_{1}-1}\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)^{j_{2}-1}\sin\left(\pi j_{1}\right)\sin\left(\pi j_{2}\right)}{\pi^{2}\left(j_{1}+j_{2}-1\right)}

\displaystyle\times\,_{2}F_{1}\left(1-j_{1},1-j_{2};2-j_{1}-j_{2};\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}(1-z)}{({\omega_{1}}+1)({\omega_{2}}+1)}\right)\,.

(418)

Both terms in this expression are particular solutions of the differential equation (
5.2
). The boundary conditions are less obvious in this form. The first term admits a power series expansion
\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}c_{m,n}\omega_{1}^{m}\omega_{2}^{n}
while the second terms has a non-analytic behavior near the origin. It can be shown that the regular solution of (
5.2
) is unique up to normalization. So, the scalar kernels share the same regular part but differ by their non-analytic parts.

Appendix I
Singular terms in the energy-energy correlation

In this appendix we explain the origin of the last two terms in the expression (
6
) for the energy-energy correlation. A feature of these terms is that they scale at small
z
as
\log z/z
,

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{(1)}_{++}

\displaystyle\sim-{\log z\over z}\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{72\left(\omega_{1}+1\right)}+\frac{1}{72\left(\omega_{2}+1\right)}+\frac{1}{12\sqrt{\left(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right)^{2}-4\omega_{1}\omega_{2}z}}\right)\,.

(419)

As explained in Section
3.3
, such singular terms come from the detector cross talk.

Applying (
190
) and (
4
) we get

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{(1)}_{++}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)=-\int{dj_{1}dj_{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2}}\,K_{\text{EE}}(j_{1},j_{2}|z,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\,{(j_{1}+j_{2})^{2}\over j_{1}^{2}j_{2}^{2}}\,,

(420)

where the kernel
K_{\text{EE}}
can be obtained from the analogous kernel for the scalar detectors
K_{\text{SS}}
using the relation (
193
).
Replacing
K_{\text{SS}}
with its integral representation (
E
) and going to the limit
z\to 0
, we find after some algebra

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{(1)}_{++}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\sim

\displaystyle-\int{dj_{1}dj\over(2\pi i)^{2}}{z^{-2-j}\over(1+j)}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,f(s_{1},s_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\times(s_{1}(s_{2}+\omega_{2}))_{+}^{-j_{1}-2}(s_{2}(s_{1}+\omega_{1}))_{+}^{-j+j_{1}-2}\,

\displaystyle\times(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})^{j}\theta(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})\,,

(421)

where we changed the integration variable
j=j_{1}+j_{2}
and introduced the function

\displaystyle f(s_{1},s_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2})={{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\over 72}\left(s_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}+s_{2}^{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+4s_{2}s_{1}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}+6s_{2}s_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}+6s_{2}^{2}s_{1}\omega_{1}+6s_{2}^{2}s_{1}^{2}\right)}\,.

(422)

The integration contour in the Mellin integral in (
I
) satisfies
{\rm Re}(j_{1})<0
and
{\rm Re}\,j>-1
. The two factors in the second line of (
I
) are defined with the ‘
+
’ prescription, see (
423
). This is needed to make the integral over small
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
in (
I
) well defined.

Closing the integration contour over
j
in (
I
) to the left half-plane, we find that the leading contribution for
z\to 0
comes from the pole at
j=-1
. An additional pole arises after the integration over small
w\equiv 1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2}
. Applying the identify

\displaystyle{1\over w^{-j}}=-{1\over 1+j}\delta(w)+\left[1\over w\right]_{+}+O(1+j)

(423)

and taking the residue at the double pole, we obtain from (
I
)

\displaystyle\text{EEC}^{(1)}_{++}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle z\to 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle-{\log z\over z}\int{dj_{1}\over 2\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,f(s_{1},s_{2}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\times(s_{1}(s_{2}+\omega_{2}))_{+}^{-j_{1}-2}(s_{2}(s_{1}+\omega_{1}))_{+}^{j_{1}-1}\,\delta(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})\,.

(424)

As in the previous case, the integration over small
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
yields additional poles at
j_{1}=-1
and
j_{1}=0
, respectively. Their contribution to the right-hand side of (
I
) is

\displaystyle-{\log z\over z}\times{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\over 72}{\left({{1\over 1+\omega_{1}}+{1\over 1+\omega_{2}}}\right)}\,.

(425)

The remaining contribution from nonzero
s_{i}
looks as

\displaystyle-24{\log z\over z}\omega_{2}^{4}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,{(s_{1}+\omega_{1})^{2}\over(s_{2}+\omega_{2})^{2}}\delta(s_{1}\omega_{2}-s_{2}\omega_{1})\delta(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})

\displaystyle=-{\log z\over z}{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\over 12}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}ds_{2}\,\delta(s_{1}\omega_{2}-s_{2}\omega_{1})\delta(1-s_{1}-s_{2}+zs_{1}s_{2})

\displaystyle=-{\log z\over z}{\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\over 12}{1\over\sqrt{(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})^{2}-4z\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}}\,,

(426)

where
\delta(s_{1}\omega_{2}-s_{2}\omega_{1})
results from the integration over
j_{1}
in (
I
). Putting together the last two relations we recover (
419
).
Notice that the integral (
I
) vanishes for
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
of opposite signs. This explains why the expression (
6
) for
\text{EEC}_{+-}
does not contains terms like (
I
).

Appendix J
Time profile in the detector

In this appendix we address the question
what is the longitudinal profile observed by a calorimeter in the physical state

\displaystyle|\Psi\rangle\simeq\int d^{4}x\ e^{iP\cdot x}e^{-{t^{2}+\vec{x}^{2}\over 4\sigma^{2}}}J(x)|\Omega\rangle\,.

(427)

We only consider here the simplest case of the one-point energy correlation. Consider the energy calorimeter centered around the retarded time
t_{0}
and working in the interval
t_{0}-T\leq t\leq t_{0}+T

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{t_{0},T}(n)=\int_{-T}^{T}du\ T_{uu}(u-t_{0},\vec{n})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{d\hat{\omega}\over\pi}\ {\sin\hat{\omega}T\over\hat{\omega}}e^{i\hat{\omega}t_{0}}{\cal E}(\hat{\omega},n)\,.

(428)

We can easily relate the one-point function of
{\cal E}_{t_{0},T}(n)
to the one-point function studied in the bulk of the paper

\displaystyle\langle\Psi|{\cal E}_{t_{0},T}(n)|\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}q\int{d\hat{\omega}\over\pi}f_{\sigma}(q+\hat{\omega}n)f_{\sigma}(q){\sin\hat{\omega}T\over\hat{\omega}}e^{i\hat{\omega}t_{0}}\langle\langle J(q+\hat{\omega}n){\cal E}(\hat{\omega},n)J(q)\rangle\rangle,

(429)

where it is natural to choose the wave function to be centered around a given momentum
P^{\mu}
with some spread
1/\sigma
,

\displaystyle f_{\sigma}(q)

\displaystyle\sim e^{-\sigma^{2}\left[(q^{0}-P^{0})^{2}+(\vec{q}-\vec{P})^{2}\right]}\,.

(430)

In the coordinate space such a wave function is localized around the origin with a spread
\sigma
both in space and time.

Figure 6:
Time dependence of the energy measurement for
P^{\mu}=(P^{0},\vec{0})
with
P^{0}=1
. We set
\sigma=10
(the left panel) and
for
\sigma=1
(the right panel). In the latter case we observe small quantum oscillations around the asymptotic value.

Let us choose the source to have the scaling dimension
\Delta_{\phi}=2
for simplicity and use (
58
) and (
59
). We go to the center-of-mass frame and set
P^{\mu}=(P^{0},\vec{0})
with
P^{0}=1
.
We also consider a narrow wave packet so that
\sigma P^{0}\gg 1
. In this limit, the integral over
q
is localized at
q=P
. Finally, we set
t_{0}=0
since its effect is simply to shift the origin of the plots considered below. In this regime the result is captured by the following integral

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{\infty}{d\hat{\omega}\over\pi}e^{-2\sigma^{2}\hat{\omega}^{2}}{\sin\hat{\omega}T\over\hat{\omega}}\left(1+\hat{\omega}+{1\over 6}\hat{\omega}^{2}\right),

(431)

where we used the fact that
f_{\sigma}(P+\hat{\omega}n)=e^{-2\sigma^{2}\hat{\omega}^{2}}
. The lower limit on the integral comes from the condition that the time component of
P+\hat{\omega}n
is non-negative, which is necessary for
\langle\langle J(P+\hat{\omega}n){\cal E}(\hat{\omega},n)J(P)\rangle\rangle
to be non-zero.

We plot (
431
) in Figure
6
for different values of
\sigma
. The response of the calorimeter starts at
0
and then goes to its asymptotic value
1
on time scales set by the size of the spread of the wave function in the coordinate space
\sigma
.
As
\sigma
decreases two effects occur: first, the asymptotic value of the energy detector response is reached faster; second, quantum oscillations develop around the asymptotic value
1
.

37

37

We say quantum because in a classical field theory, where the energy flux is locally positive, the one-point function is monotonically increasing as we increase
T
.
